January 31, 1941
The Reverend George Noss, an American missionary of
the Reformed Church who has recently returned from Japan,
was received this morning by Mr. Schuler at the request of
844
Mr. Willard of IC. Mr. Noss has lived for over twenty years
in northern Japan and speaks and reads Japanese. At the
FILED
of almost every staple in the Japanese diet including re
and fish were observable. Breakdowns in Japan's transporta-
tion system seemed to be general. While the facts in regard
to these conditions were carefully kept from the newspapers,
PS/DB
the general public was well aware of the situation as
stories were carried by word of mouth. No widespread
unemployment was observable but there was what Mr. Noss
termed

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
1468
-2-
termed a great deal of "mis-employment" or the employment
of people at tasks to which they were not fitted or accus-
tomed as the result of the closing dowm of many peacetime
industries. He estimated that from 20 to 25 percent of
the gainfully employed found themselves in this situation.
The Japanese people, both hih and low, with whom
Mr. Noss had come in contact during the closing uays of his
stay in Japan were now completely disillusioned with regard
to the China incident and Government propaganda in regard
to Japan's southward advance had had very little effect on
the popular mind as "there had been too many Government pro-
nouncements". Despite a progressive deterioration of the
Japanese economic machine there seemed to be little prospect
of a social revolution, according to Mr. Noss. Barring such
a social revolution, it seemed to Mr. Noss that Japan was
headed for war. The liberal elements of the country were
powerless and would remain so so long as the army retained
hold on the country. The Japanese people with whom
Mr. Noss was in contact were, he said, in almost complete
ignorance of the American attitude toward Japan and were
fully convinced that the United States was bluffing and
that it would not under any circumstances go to war with
Japan. It was Mr. Noss' opinion that tension in Japan
had reached such a state that war between that
country

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
1469
-3-
country and the United States is inevitable. He doubted
that any measures taken oy the nited tates to alleviate
the situation would bear fruit. Ihinking apanese were,
according to ir. oss, divided at the present time into
two camps: those who felt that apan must make peace with
China and throw itself upon the mercy of the nited States;
and those who felt thet apan must stake everything on
its "southward advance". The latter were, he feared, in
the ascendancy at the moment.
As for the future of the Christian Church in Japan,
r. Noss was extremely pessimistic. ie had been informed,
he said, by the highest jay anese authorities of the Chris-
tian Church in Japan, that conditions whicn the police au-
thorities would eventually impose upon foreign missionaries
who remain in apan were as follows: that foreign mis-
sionaries must live in every respect according to Japanese
standards; that they must support openly the new order in
East sia and the objectives of the tripartite pact, even
to the extent of being willing to proselytize in China for
these aims; and that they must publicly advocate the insti-
tution and extension of the "new structure". according to
ir. Noss, the majority of American missionaries remaining
in Japan were of the opinion that anti-American feeling
had reached its peak several months ago; that they were
determined

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.98)
1470
-4-
determined to stay in Japan for this reason; and that
they bitterly resented the advice to withdraw from Japan
as a political device on the part of the State Department.
Mr. Noss described this group as "ostriches sticking
their heads in the sand".
Mr. Noss concluded with the remark that part of his
purpose in coming to the Department was to offer his
services if they could be used. Mr. Schuler thanke Mr.
Noss for calling and informed him that there were no
894.00
vacancies in the Department at the present time but that
his offer would be kept in mind.
Mr. Noss appears to be a sound, cautious observer
possessed of a considerable amount of common sense. He
leaves the impression of intense sincerity of convictions
without fanaticism.

January 28, 1941.
MR.
NOTED
During a goodly number of years preceding andn ETEAFFAIRS
Division of
1941
viser an American national named Henry W. Denison,
a man who is reputed to have been a wise, a faithful,
and a successful counselor. There has long been in
circulation a story to the effect that in the period
894.00/1008
Asary
immediately preceding the Russo-Japanese war, when
FE 1 1941
Rson
there had taken place several exchanges of communica-
of State
tions between the Japanese and the Russian Foreign
Offices, with results unsatisfactory to the Japanese
Foreign Office, his Japanese employers asked Mr. Denison
to draft another note to the Russian Government; that
Mr. Denison thereupon said, "If you wish to avoid war,
I'll write a strong note; if you wish to fight, I'll
A
write a conciliatory note"; that the Japanese replied
that it was their decision that if the Russians did not
yield Japan would go to war; that Mr. Denison then said,
"In that case I shall draft the note in conciliatory
0
terms"; and that Mr. Denison so drafted. [Note: I do

The
PS/DB

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
The note was sent; the Russians did not yield; and Japan
attaoked, without previous declaration of war, at Chemulpo
(a Korean port) and at Port Arthur, and then declared war.
It is believed that Japanese officialdom is not in
the habit of engaging, after having decided to make an
attack, in blustering indications of that intention.
The Japanese press is addicted to bluster and threats;
and it frequently and readily resorts to both. Mr.
Matsuoka has been addicted throughout his business career
to table pounding and practices of intimidation. Not so
the seasoned and habitually responsible statesmen of Japan.
We know that Japan was drawn and was pushed into the
tripartite alliance--partly by German blandishments,
a
partly by German threats, and partly by the insistence
of certain chauvinistic Army officers and a few dynamic
722.
civilians such as Mr. Shiratori. We have reason to be-
lieve that Mr. Matsuoka participated in the pulling and
the shoving. In any event, Mr. Matsuoka has committed
himself to a full vocal support of the principles and
speoifications of the treaty of alliance.
The Germans naturally wish to get from Japan's
partnership in the alliance all the profit possible.
The Germans are pressing the Japanese to make effective
contribution to the collective efforts of the Axis powers.
There is not a great deal that Japan can do in a material
way

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
way toward helping the Germans and the Italians in the
European theater of war. The Japanese are not yet ready
to attack the British in the Far East. One contribution
the Japanese can make: they can work toward stultifying
the efforts of the United States to be of assistance to
Great Britain. More, they can try to frighten the United
States into an attitude of apprehensive circumspection
in relation to Nazidom. At the same time and on their
own behalf, they can try to frighten the United States
into an attitude of apprehensive laissez faire in rela-
tion to the current diplomatic aggressions and to poten-
tial further military aggressions by Japan in the Far
East.
It is believed that such are the efforts which the
Japanese press and Mr. Matsuoka are engaged in at the
present time: attempts to intimidate the United States--
toward diminishing the amount of American assistance to
Great Britain, toward nourishing isolationist sentiment
in this country, toward paralyzing the American Govern-
ment in this country's relations with Germany, and toward
causing this Government to stand silent and idle while
Japan goes on with her program of aggression in the Far
East.
It

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
It is believed that in so far as relations between
Japan and the United States are concerned the Japanese
press and Mr. Matsuoka are bluffing. They are trying
both to serve Germany at little expense to Japan and
to make some gains for Japanese diplomacy: they are
911.94
trying to cause the United States to continue to be
ineffective--by making us fear that they, the Japanese,
may attack us.
We should keep all the time in mind one big
outstanding fact, that Japan is not prepared to fight
(i.e. to carry on) a war with the United States.
It is to be expected that we shall have oppor-
tunity after the arrival of the new Japanese Ambassador
to gain new and better indications than any that we
can get from the Japanese press and Mr. Matsuoka
regarding Japan's real attitude and intentions. In
any case, we should not accept at face value, as an
indication of Japanese national intention, the noises
made by the Japanese press and Mr. Matsuoka.

10729145
Sir:
no. 5315, January 22, 1941, on the organization of the
Imperial Rule Assistance Association and to comment on
the present s tatus and significance of the Association
as revealed by developments and discussions during the
present session of the Imperial Diet.
The

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
The Association has been vigorously attacked both in
and out of the Diet on the varied grounds that it is un-
constitutional, communistic, fascist, cumbersome in
structure, faulty in personnel anā that its legal and
political position remains undetermined. As the Diet
session has progressed, the issue has been narrowed to
a question of whether the Association should possess
a strong political character or whether it should be
merely an organ of national spiritual mobilization.
Fumni
Both Prime Minister Konoye and Home Minister Hiranuma
08
have stated that the Association, while maintaining a
J
definite political character, is not a political asso-
ciation but rather a "public" organization, an "auxiliary
organ" which will cooperate with the Government but will
not engage in policy-making.
The House of Representatives has passed a bill
appropriating Yen 8,000,000 to the Association and it
is expected that a thorough reorganization of its
structure and personnel will be carried out following
the conclusion of the Diet Session. The budget committee
of the House of Peers on February 27 approved the appro-
priation of $ 8,000,000 and passed a resolution calling
for reorganization of the I.R.A.A. While those favor-
ing the restriction of the I.R.A.A. to a spiritual mobilization
orgn stripped of all political significance are in the
minority, widespread criticism of the Association has
peroeptibly reduced its prestige. At the present moment
it appears doubtful that the I.R.A.A. will emerge as a
strong organ exerting a decisive influence in Japanese
politios.

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
politics.
Shortly following its inception the Association was
attacked principally on the basis that it was unconstitution-
al. Its defendants argued that the Japanese Constitution
was susceptible to a liberal interpretation, that actually
it did not define the relations between the Government and
the Diet. It wus later stated that the I.R.A.A. fell with-
in the provisions of Article 29 of the Constitution recog-
nizing the right of the people to form associations with-
in the limits of the law.
The Association has been called both communistic and
fascist. A Diet member produced in committee session
charts of the Soviet system to show resemblance to the struc-
ture of the I.R.A.A.
Other critics see totalitarian features
in the organization out of harmony with Japan's national
polity. They fear the complete extinction of the Diet
V
and the assumption of its prerogatives by a centralized
body controlling the nation. One interpellator singled
out the provision that the Prime Minister should "lead"
the Association as smacking of German totalitarianism in
that it suggested a national "Leader". Prince Konoye
denied that such was the intention and advised re-wording
the provision to avoid any misunderstanding. He expressed
opposition to any attempt to adapt totalitarian political
methods to Japan.
Recent discussions in the Diet and press have been
centered on the question of the political character of
the I.R.A.A.
Leaders of the Movement, Count Arima,
Kingoro Hashimoto, Seigo Nakano, and other avowed na-
tionalists

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
tionalists have advocated construction of a strong
organ politically influential.
Opposition in the Diet to the I.R.A.A. has come
from former party leaders who see in it the eclipse of
parliamentary government. This opposition has recently
becone vocal in the Chuo-tei, one of the groups which,
in the absence of political parties, have inevitably
arisen during this session of the Diet. Mr. Katsu
UGF
Kawasaki, leader of this faction, is a former Minseito
member who has served in the House of Representatives
for nine terms. In an attempt to restrict the power
of the Association, the Chuo-tei sponsored a bill to
reduce the appropriation for the I.R.A.A. from.¥8,000,000
to ¥3,000,000. Yen 100,000,000 was the amount first men-
tioned as needed for the I.R.A.A.'s annual expenditures.
Mr. Kawasaki, speaking in favor of this bill on Febru-
ary 22, criticized the Association for similarities to
the Nazi and Communist systems. He said it was natural
that the constitutionality of the I.R.A.A. should be
doubted and that it should be called a "Government
without the Government". He also accused it of harbor-
ing members of "red" leanings. He proposed a revision
of its structure through abolition of the Diet Bureau
and the central and prefectural cooperative councils.
Although the bill was defeated by a vote of 259 to 54,
the fact that unanimity in the so-called "Imperial Rule
Assistance

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Assistance Diet" had been shattered was deplored by
some vernaculars as suggesting to the nation that a
conflict between nationalism and liberalism was taking
place.
The veteran parliamentarian Yukio Ozaki clearly
presented a case against the I.R.A.A. in a written
interpellation recently answered by the Government.
He criticized the Association as lacking legal status,
pointing out that although it received state funds it
was neither a government office nor a political asso-
ciation. The Diet, he said, was the legitimate in-
stitution for assisting the Throne ana participation
by the I.R.A.A. in Diet affairs or attempts to influence
Diet decisions would be comparable to usurpation of
family prerogatives by an illegitimate son. Ozaki sug-
gested that should Prince Konoye continue to occupy the
office of Presidency following his resignation as Prime
Minister the Association would become a political party,
the very thing it sought to abolish.
The Government's reply defined the I.R.A.A. as a
legally established association engaged in public affairs
as specified in Article III of the Peace Preservation
Police Law. This law makes a distinction between
"political" and "public" associations, exempting the
latter from registration and certain other require-
ments. The difference between the Diet's "assistance
to the Throne" and that of the Association was explained by
saying

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
saying that the Diet is an organ of government es-
tablished by the Constitution whose actions have
legal effect whereas the Association aims only to
extend cooperation and assistance to the several
organs of Government. It was emphasized that there
was no dream of establishing within Japan's national
structure a form of dictatorship as exists in some
foreign countries.
The Government has promised that a thorough-
going reorganization of the I.R.A.A. will be effected
following the conclusion of the Diet session. Many
of its bureaus and sections are to be abolished, its
activities further clarified, personnel renovated, and
the budget reconsidered. Personnel has been loudly
criticized. In some prefectures "red" sympathizers
have reportedly been admitted to the Association.
The integrity of some officials has been questioned;
many are said to have spent unreasonable sums on
official trips throughout Japan.
Baron Hiranuma announced in a meeting of the
80F
budget committee of the House of Peers on February 26
that the Government was prepared to take full responsi-
bility for the reorganization of the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association. He did not explain t he details
of the contemplated reorganization but asserted that
the Government would insist that there be no conflict
with its policies, and that legal infractions by the
Association itself or by members of it would be punished.
Although

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
Although the reorganization of the I.R.A.A. must
be awaited before the nature of its activities and ex-
tent of its influence can be evaluated, it appears now
in the closing days of the Diet session that a compromise
will result whereby the Association becomes neither a
powerful political association nor merely an organization
for spiritual mobilization. The press reports that
Premier Konoye, Count Arima, and Mr. Yonezo Maeda,
president of the Diet Members Club, are in agreement
on the reconstruction to be carried out. Thus while
the Association will presumably retain its "political
character", described vaguely as "cooperation between
Government and people", and "making known to the people
the wishes of the Government, and to the Government
the wishes of the people", it will lack political authority.
1
Endless discussions in the Diet and press have
clearly reduced the prestige of the Association originally
hailed as a great National Movement uniting the subjects
of the Empire in single-hearted devotion to the ideal of
assisting the Throne. Local committees have expressed
dissatisfaction with the bickerings in Tokyo and the
press has clamored for reorganization and clarification
of the Association's activities. A journalist has ex-
pressed the general feeling of the publio in a pun on the
name of the Association (Tai-sei Yoku-san-kai): "It's
high time to stop arguing!"
Thus

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
Thus, while the former party leaders have lost
their battle to reduce the Asscciation to an impotent
status, their voiced opposition is significant and it
seems highly doubtful that the I.R.A.A. will become the
nucleus of a changed totalitarian Japan.
Respectfully yours,
on.
800
Joseph C. Grew.

Mr. Diffendorfer, who had just returned from a
brief visit to Japan to study the situatign there as
it affects the work of the American Methodist mission-
aries, gave an account of the impressions derived by
him from his visit. He said that everywhere he had
been conscious of a lack of unity among the Japanese
PS/JHS
people but that it was apparent to him that the extrem-
1st group was in the saddle and was working toward the
establishment of a complete totalitarian system in
Japan. He said he had been impressed by the increas-
ing numbers of Germans in Japan, by their growing
APR 21 1941
FILED
influence

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
influence and the influence of the rising momentum of
the German-inspired youth movement in Japan. This move-
ment was designed to carry out the complete establishment
of totalitariansm in Japan. On the other hand he believed
that the influence of the family was still strong in the
country and in this influence he saw a possible hope of
successfully combating the totalitarian movement.
Mr. Diffendorfer said he had been very much impressed
by Ambassador Grew and he said that everywhere in Japan
people spoke of Mr. Grew with high praise. Mr. Grew, he
said, had advised him to reach his judgments not by what
the Japanese were saying but by what they were doing.
Mr. Diffendorfer said that he had endeavored to follow
this advice. However, he had been agreeably surprised to
find that his party had been permitted to interview Koreans
while they were in Korea and those Koreans had apparently
talked freely with members of the party. Nevertheless, he
had observed that his party was under constant surveillance
by the Japanese authorities. Mr. Diffendorfer had talked
with a number of officials including Mr. Matsuoka, the
Minister for Foreign Affairs. Mr. Matsuoka appeared to be
under a considerable strain. Mr. Matsuoka said very em-
phatioally that Japan did not intend to get out of China
and was determined to pursue its ob jeotives of the "new
ordor

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
order in East Asia" and he advised the United States to
stay on its own side of the Pacific and mind its own busi-
ness.
Mr. Diffendorfer observed that there had been no
uniformity in the action of consuls in the matter of ad-
vising Americans to leave  try. He said that the
consular officers at Tokyo had not circularized Americans
in Japan proper in the matter of advising withdrawal. On
the other hand Mr. Marsh, the Consul General at Keijo, had
handled this matter very efficiently and Mr. Diffendorfer
was very much impressed by the effective manner in which
Mr. Marsh handled the situation.
Mr. Diffendorfer said he had left Japan with a strong
conviction that Americans should get out and he intended
to recommend this action to his Board at its meeting on
February 20 and also to speak in favor of it on February 19
at a meeting of the International Missionary Council. He
said that just prior to his departure from Japan Japanese
Christian leaders inoluding Bishop Abe had concurred in
the wisdom of such action. Mr. Diffendorfer felt that even
apart from the possibility of complications in the relations
between Japan and the United States that action would very
much relieve the situation as removing a cause of embarrass-
ment to Japanese Christians and other friends of America.
some
Mr. Diffendorfer felt that possibly in the case of/businessmen
the reason for remaining was even less strong than in the case of
missionaries.

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.98)
-4
missionaries. Mr. Diffendorfer asked what Mr. Hornbeck's
views on the matter were. Mr. Diffendorfer in reply to
a question indicated that he had not been aware of the
renewal of the advice to Americans to withdraw which
had been issued last week.
Mr. Hornbeck read to Mr. Diffendorfer the substance
of our instructions to officers in the Far East on the
subject of renewing advice to Americans to withdrew.
Mr. Hornbeck then indicated that he believed that he also
felt that it was probably true that some American business
representatives were in an even more difficult position
in Japan than American missionaries and would be well
adv1sed to withdraw. With regard to the action which
Mr. Diffendorfer proposed to take in the matter of with-
drawing all of the American Methodist missionaries,
Mr. Hornbeck felt thet this was a matter in which the
decision rested with Mr. Diffendorfer and his Board.
In the course of the conversation Mr. Diffendorfer
referred to a Germen by the name of Bitter who was a
leading spirit among the Jesuits in Japan. These Jesuits
included in their number priests of various nationalities,
including Americans. He said that Bitter was in the
German army during the World War and that he travels on a
Gorman

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.95)
-5
German passport. Father Bitter had said to Mr. Diffen-
dorfer that during his period of service in Japan he
had visited the United States thirty-four times and that
owing to the impossibility of obtaining from Rome or
Germany funds for the work of the mission in Japan he
visited America from time to time to solicit contribu-
tions from private sources in this country. Father
Bitter, he said, indicated that his mission workers had
no intention of leaving Japan.

The Situation in the Far East
894,00/
General Summary
Dissatisfaction was reported to have increased in
Japan over the lowering standard of living and the
conduct of domestic and foreign affairs by the present
10
government.
Japanese "mediation" brought about a settlement of
the dispute between Indochina and Thailand, the terms of
which were highly favorable to Thailand. Japan exacted
from the two disputants assurances that they would not
enter into with any other power any agreement which
would be in opposition to Japan.
The Japanese Foreign Minister left Tokyo on March 12
FP
for Moscow, Berlin and Rome. Ambassador Grew expressed
the view that Matsuoka's conversations with Molotov would
APR 21.19
be more significant than those with German and Italian
leaders, as the primary purpose of his trip was thought
to be the reaching, with German support, of a political
understanding

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
understanding with the Soviet Union. The Japanese Am-
bassador in Moscow expressed doubt whether Matsuoka
would be successful in persuading the Soviet Foreign
Commissar to reach a political agreement.
Little progress was made in economic discussions
between Japan and the Soviet Union.
Approximately 7,O00 Japanese troops which had at-
tacked and pillaged Chinese supply routes along the
coast of southern Kwangtung Province were withdrawn.
Dr. John Earl Baker, an American, was appointed by
the Chinese Government to take charge of traffic on the
Yunnan-Burma highway.

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
One.
Situation in Japan.
The Ambassador at Tokyo reported growing dissatis-
faction in Japan with the existing Government under the
leadership of Prince Konoye because of oppressive control
over the distribution of consumers' goods, liquidation
of the political parties, the extreme political objectives
of some, and the cupidity of others, associated with the
Imperial Rule Assistance Association, the indispositions
of Prince Konoye in times of crises, and the exposed and
extremely difficult international position of Japan. This
dissatisfaction, the Ambassador felt, mignt in the near
future force a change in the Cabinet, Prince Konoye being
replaced by some person possibly of the type of Baron
Hiranuma, who hes acted as a check in tne Cabinet to those
extremists who have exerted on the Prime Minister a sinis-
ter influence. Tne Ambassador concluded that he did not
expect to see any notable change in Japan's objectives but
that there might be anticipated an increase of caution in
Japan's conduct of international affairs.
Two.
Mediation Conference between Indochina and
Thailand.
The Ambassador at Tokyo on March 1l reported that the
exchange of notes embodying the Japanese "mediation"
ar
settlement of the dispute between Indochina and Thailand
was initialed that afternoon. He was informed that the
Japanese

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Japanese note stated in translation "We propose for your
unconditional acceptance the following terms"; while the
Frencn note cuntained the statement "We cede at your in-
sistence". The agreement was unaerstood to provide for
the cession by Indochina to Thailand of the Province of
Battambang, a large part of the Province of Siem Reap, and
the Provinces of Melouprey, Pakse and Luang Prabang. It
was stated that most of this territory wos to be demili-
tarized and that Japan guaranteed the execution of the
settlement. It was reported that the French Government
undertook not to enter into any agreement with any other
power as a result of which Indochina would be placed in
opposition to Jaoan. It was stated in press despatches
of March 12 that Tnailand, as well as Indochina, undertook
not to enter into any agreement or understanding directed
against Japan.
The French emphasized that the settlement was reached
only under duress. Despite Thailand's very considerable
territorial gains, the Thai Government, according to the
American Minister at Bangkok, was not particularly happy
over the conduct of the conference by the Japanese and was,
like the French Government, subjected to Japanese pressure.
The American Ambassador at Tokyo reported that he was in-
formed by his French colleague that the chief Thai delegate
had remarked to him that the settlement was "devoid of
all common sense".
Three.

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Three. Visit of JapaneseForeign Minister to Moscow,
Berlin and Rome.
The Japanese Foreign Minister left Tokyo on March 12
for Moscow, Berlin and Rome. Japanese official announce-
ments and the Japanese press emphasized that the trip was
for the primary purpose of establishing close personal re-
lationshins with German and Ialian leaders, ignoring the
significance of Mr. Matsuoka's stop-overs at Moscow on
his way to and from Berlin. The Ambassador at Tokyo sug-
gested on March 12 that there was little justification for
the view that the Foreign Minister's journey was for the
purpose of concluding a prearranged agreement with the
Soviet Union and that it was more likely that Mr. Matsuoka's
principal objective was to enlist German support in induc-
ing the Soviet Union to reach a political understanding
with Japan. The Ambassador believed that the visit to
Rome was only a matter of formal courtesy.
The American Ambassador at Moscow was informed by his
Japanese colleague on March 10 that the Japanese Minister's
visit to Berlin was secondary to his call at Moscow where
he desireu to persuade Molotov to agree to a Soviet-
Japanese political understanding; that Matsuoka had no
important matters to discuss with German or Italian lead-
ers; and that he doubted the likelinood of Matsuoka's
being able to talk Molotov into an agreement.
Four.

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
Four. Soviet-Japanese Relations.
According to the American Ambassador at Moscow, his
Japanese colleague stated on March 10 that commercial dis-
cussions between Japan and the Soviet Union had made
progress but were then being obstructed by the "pettiness"
and "lack of understanding" of the Jaoanese Foreign
Office and that the question of a fisheries convention
was still at a stalemate.
Five.
Japan's Program of Southward Expansion.
The Japanese Anbassador to the Soviet Union informed
the American Ambassador on March 10 that he believed that
because Germany did not wish to go to war with the United
States, Germany would not attemot to drive Japan into
armed conflict with this country.
.General Tatekawa added
that in his opinion Japan did not intend to expand further
further
south than Indochina, observing that/Japanese action would
be precipitated only by interference on the part of the
United States with Japan's access to oil.
The French Foreign Office informed the American Ambas-
sador at Vichy on March 6 that it was felt that the Japa-
nese were displaying considerable hesitancy over any bold
expansion southward; that until a Soviet-Japanese political
agreement should be reached Japan would not risk an impor-
tant southward move, and that because of less friendly
relations between the Soviet Union andGermany as a result
of

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.99)
-7
of recent Balkan developments Japan could not anticipate
much effective supporting pressure from Germany on the
Soviet Union.
Six. Limitation of Rubber Shipments to Japan.
The American Consul at Hanoi reported on March 10
that, according to official French information, an agree-
ment was being negotiated among Indochina, malaya and the
Netherlands East Indies to limit to Japanese domestic needs
exportation of rubber to Japan; that Indochina was expected
to export about 25,000 tons of rubber to Japan during 1941;
and that the other two colonies would reduce their ship-
ments to Japan so that the total would ot exceed the
established domestic needs of Japan.
Seven. Jeopardization of merican Industrial Interests
in Japan by New Japanese Legislation.
The American Ambassador reported on March 10 that Amer-
ican industrial interests in Japan were jeopardized by recent-
ly enacted legislation extending government control over
industry. The Ambassador pointed out that these American
interests were unable because of Japanese legislation to
liquidate their assets and transfer them to the United States
and were therefore in danger of losing all or most of their
investments in Japan.
8. Japanese Bombings of American Property.
The Consul General at Hankow was informed on Maroh 1l
394.115
that

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
that the Seventh Day Adventist Mission at Yengcheng,
Honan, was on January 28 damaged by Japanese aerial
bombardment.
Eight. Sino-Japanese Hostilities.
The force of approximately 7,O00 Japanese troops
which landed early last week along the southern Kwangtung
coast was reported by the Consul General at Hong Kong
to have met little Chinese resistance and to have had as
one of its principal objectives the pillaging of the sup-
ply routes in that area. Japanese sources quoted by the
Consul General at Canton mentioned the capture of rice,
petroleum products, military supplies and wood oil. The
793.94
Consul General reported that the Japanese South China
Expeditionary force on March 9 announced that the troops
which had landed along the southern Kwangtung coast had
been withdrawn.
Nine. Yunnan-Burma Highway.
Dr. John Earl Baker, an American formerly connected
with the Red Cross, was stated in the press to have been
appointed by the Chinese Government to take charge of
893154
traffic on the Yunnan-Burma Highway.
The Consul at Rangoon reported on March 1l that the
new

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.98)
-9
new bridge across the Mekong River which had been badly
damaged by Japanese bombing was reopened to traffic on
March 3. He stated that the Chinese Government goods
shipped from Burma into China by the Southwest Trans-
portation Company from December 30, 1940, to February 28,
1941, amounted to more than 22,000 long tons.
Ten. Relations Between the Chinese Government and
the Chinese Communiste.
The Ambassador at Chungking reported that General
Chiang Kai-shek addressed the People's Political Council
on March 6,with regard to demands presented by the Com-
munist delegates to the Council, stating that the Govern-
ment's policy was that the army should be nationalized;
that there could be but one national political authority;
and that if the Communists "obey orders and stop attack-
89300B
ing other army units, our Government would be lenient ...
but should they disobey orders, violate discipline and
obstruct the armed resistance ... the Government ...
cannot but suppress them in accordance with law".
Eleven. Relations between Thailand and the Soviet
Union.
The American Ambassador at Moscow reported reliable
7492
information

information that the Thai Minister to Germany called on
Molotov on March 12 and exchanged notes establishing
diplomatic relations between Thailand and the Soviet
Union.

Strictly Confidential Memo for President Roosevelt.
The Japanese Government cannot admit, through of-
ficial channels, that American economic pressure and
defense preparations under President Roosevelt have
been so politically successful that the Japanese now
would welcome an opportunity to change their interna-
tional, and modify their China, positions.
The domestic position of the present Japanese Gov-
ernment is like that of the Bruening Government in Ger-
many in 1931. The Japanese would rather lose the war
in China than lose the domestic war to their own Extre-
mists. But, the loss of the China War and the imminence
of an American War, would put the radical nationalists,
00
civil and military, in complete control. If the Conserva-
tive authorities, including Prince Konoye, Mr. Matsuoka,
Count Arima, General Muto, etc. and the Emperor, can win,
.00/1033
by diplomacy, a safe economic and international position,
public opinion in Japan would restore the Conservatives
to complete control.
For such a reversal, the Japanese majority needs,
no less than China, the help of the United States.
Failing this, they foresee the possibility of a union
3
of their own Extremist elements with the Radial foroes
in China; a union comparable to, and an appendage of,
the compact of Russia and Germany in Europe. They feel
that if some constructive cooperation is not realized
DFC8
with the United States before March or April, the
Facist element will take control in both China and
Japan, no matter whether England or Germany wins in the
E
1944
Spring offensive.
Such an eventuality would surely close the door
S
for the Allied cause in the Far East.
in
JU
his own words, "is riding the horses until he can stop
them."
, in his own words, said that "to
H
call the present war in China a Holy War is a blasphemy,"
S
and "to call the Treaty with Wang-Ching-Wei an equal
Treaty is a lie."
said he would probably be
killed if we revealed his statements to certain
Japanese.
We found the Japanese offioials virtually despair-
ing of any possibility of reestablishing cordial rela-
tions with the United States. President Roosevelt's
polioy,

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.99)
1491
-2-
policy, and the Italian losses in the Mediterranean have
created a remarkable opportunity for solidifying the Far
Eastern situation in our own favor, and the Japanese are
apparently now following a plan of procedure for coopera-
tion with the United States. Mr. Matsuoka designed his
speech of December 19th as an indication of this inten-
tion.
The Japanese feel that their alliance with the Axis
will have to be nullified realistically before it can be
broken legally and officially. The Japanese authorities
are ready (though they dare not admit their readiness at
the peril of their lives) to substitute the United States
for Germany, by an agreement which would embrace the
following aspects:
I. LEGAL (for Japanese public opinion)
A. The Japanese Government could maintain that as
they accepted the Axis Alliance to maintain world
peace by restricting the European War vis-a-vis
the United States (but much more Ruesia) they
could apply the same principle to Germany and
threaten Germany with Japanese involvement if
she extends the War beyond its oresent confines.
(Germany then would be doubly hesitant to de-
clare war on the United States.)
II. POLITIC
A. Acceptance of the cooperation of the United
States in a settlement of the China War on
the basis of the secret Truce Terms offered
last October by Chiang Kai Chek. With some
guarantee of politic-economic order in China,
and the removal of China as an immediate mili-
tary menace, or a politioal menace through a
European 'sell-outi, China and Japan could
then unite to fight Communism in China and in
the Far East. This would take Germany, now
acting through Russia, out of China.
B. A recognition of a Far Eastern Monroe Doctrine
based on the aforementioned China settlement,
the Japanese-American guarantee to check any
third power attempting to alter the political
status of the Philippine Islands, Hong Kong,
Singapore or Malaya, and the establishment of
autonomous Governments in Indo-China and the
Dutoh

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.99)
1492
-3-
Dutch East Indies, in order to remove these
areas as potential war spoils, and in order to
forestall the demand of Japanese Extremists
for forcible action. (These autonomous Gov-
ernments would agree to respect all existing
investments, etc.) Actually, in the Dutch
East Indies, Queen Wilhelmina could be accepted
as a Sovereign.
C. Just prior to our abrogation of the Commercial
Treaty, the Japanese and British had virtually
agreed upon a Treaty reopening trade in the
Yangtze Valley. The British, therefore, would
have no objection.
D. No territorial aggrandizement in China proper.
III. ECONOMIC
A. Japan (and with her, the Far East) is drifting
into a commodity economy which will produce a
low standard of living which she does not like,
but cannot avoid without American assistance.
American assistance (cfr. additional memoranda)
could be so given as to guarantee the political
agreement and set up an economy in the Far East
so totally variant from the German that the
Germans could not do business with it. By ear-
marking, but leaving in the United States, a
heavy gold credit, with interest payments, for
substantiating the currenoies of Japan and
China, the United States would put the Far East
on a money economy like our own, and hold over
both China and Japan the threat of withdrawal
for any failure to comply with the political
provisions of the joint agreements.
B. Japan would grant a complete Open Door provided
she received similar treatment from other Far
Eastern countries. She would write a Reciprocal
Trade Treaty with the United States allowing free
entry of certain basic commodities, heavy machin-
ery, etc. Cotton and agricultural surpluses
would be similarly accepted and could actually
be bought by both China and Japan under the
monetary arrangement above mentioned.
Because

=== Page 107 === (confidence 0.99)
1493
-4
Because of the domestic situations, any such ar-
rangement would have to be presented to the Japanese and
Chinese people as a fait accompli. Meantime, merely to
indioate that such a settlement is possible is to put
power in the hands of the Conservative element in Japan
and give them confidence to proceed.
A representative of President Roosevelt could be
introduced, with the full knowledge of Mr. Grew, to work
out, with the utmost speed and secrecy, in cooperation
with the controlling elements in Japan, including the
Emperor, such an agreement as would bring some order in
the Far East, and put within the power of President
Roosevelt the opportunity to immunize the Pacifio for
at least three years.
The Japanese people who now despair of American
friendship would welcome this as the greatest boon to
their national life and security, for which the Japanese
would sacrifice anything except their Far Eastern posi-
tion. The representative of the President should be
someone whom he knows and trusts int imately; someone who
will be apprised fully of American aims in the Far East;
someone who is keenly aware that the Germans will attempt
ruthlessly to prevent any American-Japanese agreement;
and someone who will not attract attention as an official
member of our State Department.
If President Roosevelt acts to investigate this
possibility, we would he willing to cooperate with his
representative for the safeguarding of the Japanese of-
ficials, and the verification of their statements.

Tokyo's despatch no. 5528, April 21, 1941.
On April 10, 194l the Japanese Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs, according to the Japan Times
and Advertiser, rebuked the Japanese public
for adopting a hostile attitude toward
foreigners and urged that police officers be
courteous to foreigners and not irritate them
unnecessarily. Several vernacular newspapers
editorially supported Mr. Ohashi's stand.
The Ambassador expresses the hope that
these efforts will be effective in reducing
the "tendency towards xenophobia" formerly
encouraged by the Japanesd press and by acts
of the Japanese Government.

With reference to the Embassy's despatch no. 5468
dated March 26, 194l, reporting efforts of the Japanese
Foreign Minister, Mr. Matsuoka, to curb the anti-foreign
agitation in Japan, I have the honor to inform the Depart-
ment that in a review of Japanese foreign affairs, Mr. Chuiohi
Ohashi, Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs, on April 10, 194l,
made the following remarks on the subject of the attitude
towards

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.99)
1509
-2-
towards foreigners, as reported in the JAPAN TIMES AND
ADVERTISER:
"10. I am told that there is a tendency
for a section of the Japanese public to
adopt an attitude, not becoming a great
nation, of hating foreigners as a whole.
Restricting foreigners is a thing quite
different from hating foreigners as a whole.
There is a need of giving full protection
to law-abiding foreigners irrespective of
their nationality. It is contrary to
Bushido to harass good, helpless foreigners
who happen to reside among us. I have a
wish to see that police officers are courteous
toward foreigners whom they have to question
in the performance of their duties and do not
irritate them unnecessarily."
The most interesting feature of Mr. Ohashi's remarks
on this subject is that by implication he charges the
Japanese police with improper treatment of foreigners and
specifically urges that in the future the police exercise
care and consideration in their dealings with foreigners
resident in Japan. A number of vernacular newspapers
editorially supported Mr. Ohashi's plea for a more con-
siderate attitude towards foreigners, on the ground that
discourteous treatment of foreigners residing in Japan was
contrary to the Japanese spirit and traditions. There is
L/
enclosed a summary of a NICHI NICHI editorial as published
in translation in the JAPAN TIMES AND ADVERTISER on April 13,
1941.
It is hoped that the apparently genuine efforts of
the Japanese Foreign Office will not be without effeot in
reduoing the tendency towards xenophobia which had previous-
1y

=== Page 129 === (confidence 1.00)
1510
-3-
ly been encouraged by the Japanese press and by the
actions of the Japanese Government itself. Mr. Ohashi's
reference to the activities of the Japanese police would
appear to indicate that these efforts are something more
than a mere gesture on the part of the Japanese authorities.
Respectfully yours,
710
Joseph C. Grew.

Tokyo's despatch no. 5551 of April 24,

The Embassy reports that according to
the new rules and regulations announced on
Aplil 12 the Secretariat of the Imperial
Rul.e Assistance Association will now consist
offour bureaux, the Diet Planning and
Policy Bureaux having been abolished and
the dangers of a political conflict with
the Diet thereby removed.
The Embassy states that it is now clear
that the Imperial Rule Assistance Association
1s to become little more than a "spiritual
mobilization" society and declares that the
present reorganization symbolizes the failure
of the Konoye cabinet to establish success-
fully a new internal structure. The Embassy
believes that the significance of this failure
lies in the fact that there is still in Japan
resistance to any movement which would pre-
vent the formation of political groups and
their participation in political affairs but
warns at the same time that there should not
be read into this failure of the association
any positive significance, whether with regard
to the possibility of a resurgence of liber-
alism as a factor in Japan's diplomacy or to
the

threaten the life of the nonoye Cabinet since the closer
relations established with military ana business circles
through the new ministerial appointments in carly april
strengthened the Cabinet's internal position. While the
significance of its failure as originally conceived is
believed to lie in the fact that there is still in Japan
resistance to any mov ement which would prevent the formation
of political groups and their participation in political
affairs.
Before the conclusion of the Diet session a fundamental
reorganization of the I.R.A.A. appeared necessary if it were
to continue in existence. It had been attacked principally
on the grounds that it usurped prerogatives of the Diet,
that its constitutional position was uncertain, and that
its denomination as a "public association" did not clarify
its actual status. It was announced that the reorganization
woula

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
would take the form of a drastic change in personnel and
a simplirication of the organization's structure. Count
Arima, who first headed the Association, resigned and 400
employees submitted their resignations en bloc. It was
then announced that Prince Konoye would retain the title
of President, that Lieutenant General leisuke Yanagawa,
Minister of Justice, would become Vice President and that
Mr. Sotaro Ishiwata, Finance Minister in the Hiranuma
Cabinet, would te appointed Chief of the Secretariat. At
the conference of Prefectural Governors, held in Tokyo
during early April, it was announced that each Governor
would head the branch of the Association in his own prefecture.
The form and functions of the numerous bureaux and
sections provided for in the original rules of the Associa-
tion were inadequately defined. Controversy was especially
heated over the Diet, Planning and Policy Bureaux. It
was at first thought that the Diet Bureau might exert a
strong influence on the deliberations of the Diet itself and
fear was expressed that it might be used to dictate policy
and to control the members of the Diet. Establishment of
the Planning and Policy Bureaux was opposed on the ground
that the formulation of political policy could not con-
stitutionally be invested in an organization standing out-
side the legal structure of the Japanese Government. As
a consequence, these three bureaux have been abolished and
the danger of a political conflict with the Diet thereby
removed. With the abolition of these bureaux the Associa-
tion has been stripped of the political power whioh it
might otherwise have exercised.
Aocording

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
According to the new rules and regulations announced
on April 12, the Secretariat will now consist of four bureaus:
General Affairs, Organization, East Asia, and Central Train-
ing Section. The General Affairs Bureau will handle problems
of personnel and administration. The Organization Bureau will
disseminate information regarding the objectives and activi-
ties of the Association and will act as liaison between the
local councils and the central organization. The East Asia
Bureau will coordinate various Asia promotion organizations
and will cooperate with the Government in connection with
the execution of national policies concerned with East Asia.
The Central Training Section will train association offi-
cials. A Research Commission will be set up to investigate
and report upon matters specified by the President or the
Vice President. The reorganized plan calls for no c hange in
the local cooperative councils which have continued to function
since the inauguration of the I.R.A.A.
The necessitated reorganization of the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association symbolizes the failure of the Konoye
Government to establish successfully a new internal structure.
It was at one time thought that this failure might mean the
resignation of the Konoye Cabinet. However, the appointments
of Army and Navy officers and of Mr. Ogura of the Sumitomo
interests to ministerial posts warded off the fall tat seemed
impending. The Imperial Rule Assistance Association is still
proolaimed as an organ uniting the people of the nation in
servioe to the Throne but is conceded now to be little more
than an association to carry out national spiritual mobiliza-
tion.
Although

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Although the political parties had been abolished,
factions representing varying shades of political thought
were active during the Diet session. The Chuotei group,
made up of former party leaders, openly opposed the appro-
priations to the Imperial kule Assistance Association.
Mr. Seigo Nakano resigned his position as permanent director
of the Association and announced the formation of the Tohokai
party under his leadership. Another group styling itself the
Imperial Hotel Club and including members of the old Minseito
and Seiyukai parties opposed the extension of political power
to the Association and has made active efforts to establish
a new political party. Prince Konoye has been approached to
head an organization to be known as the Diet Members Club but
he has stated that he favors such an organization only if it
is to be purely a social club to which all members of the two
Houses of the Diet may belong.
A meeting to discuss the formation of the Diet Members
Club was held at the Imperial Hotel on April 16. The press
commented on the lack of interest in the venture indicated
by the fact that out of 30o Diet members invited, only 90
attended the meeting. The following resolution was drafted:
"We, Diet members in attendance at this meeting, pledge our-
selves to work for the establishment of a Diet Members Club
comprising all members of both Houses".
The establishment of a purely social and politically
insignificant Diet Members Club will not preclude the forma-
tion of political groups. While the policy of the Government
toward Nakano's new Tohokai has not been enunciated, it is
likely that existence of the party will be permitted. The
factions whioh grew up during the past Diet session, repre-
sonting varying shades of political opinion and made up of
politioians

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
politicians from the old parties, are likely to continue or to
resolve themselves into actual political parties. This dev-
elopment the Government may not oe able to prevent, although
it is probable that any such parties will announce their de-
termination to function in harmony with the original prin-
ciples of the I.R.A.A.
The failure of the Inerial Rule Assistance Association
to assume a political role has significance only in a nega-
tive direction. The reorganization above described signal-
ized the failure of plans to create a political inst rument
outside the framework of laws which create or recognize
agencies of government, and to employ that instrument to
bring about a radical alteration of the social, economic,
and political systems. There should not be read into the
failure of the Association any positive significance,
whether with regard to the possibility of a resurgence of
liberalism as a factor in Japan's diplomacy or to the
possibility of change in fundamental national policies in
the field of foreign relations. Whether these two possibilities
exist is a question which does not fall within the purview of
any discussion of the Association, and must be answered in
the light of other considerations.
The failure does suggest,
however, that the right to discuss policy and to oppose meth-
ods still exists and that the traditional impulse to organize
political groups for the representation of opinion is still
present.
Respectfully yours,
800
Joseph C. Grew.

DIVISIGN OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS

Sent to Tokyo, doadon,
Mr. Secretary:
and Chuagking.
The Situation in the Far East
HES
General Summary
94.00
No indication has thus far been given as to the
894.00/1044
course of Japan's foreign policy following the conclusion
of the Soviet-Japanese "neutrality pact" and consideration
of the report of the Japanese Foreign Minister to his
Government upon his return from Europe. Mr. Matsuoka
arrived in Tokyo on April 22 and in his public statements
indicated that he had been strongly impressed by the
rapidity with which the Axis and Soviet leaders reached
decisions and acted.
Certain recent developments, outlined in section one
below, would seem to suggest that Japan is for the time
being concentrating its attention on an attempt to bring
FILED
to a conclusion the Sino-Japanese hostilities. The 
Ambassador at Tokyo reported that the moderate elements
PS/CMR
in Japan still exerted aome influence but that the hands
of the extremists were being strengthened by the reverses
which the British have been suffering in the eastern
Mediterranean area.
According

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
According to the Ambassador at Moscow, the Japanese
Ambassador there stated that Soviet-Japanese trade and
fishery negotiations continued but were not expected to be
concluded for two or three weeks and that the Soviet Union
was endeavoring to obtain through Japan commodities from
the Western Hemisphere and south Pacific for its own and
probably German consumption.
The Ambassador at Tokyo reported that Germany has
been shipping important quantities of manufactured goods
to Manchuria (presumably ultimately destined for Japan),
including fifteen motor torpedo boats and three submarines.
The Embassy at Berlin reported that Germany expected to
obtain considerable economic benefits from forthcoming
German-Japanese economic negotiations.
The Japanese on April 19 landed troops on the coast
of Chekiang and Fukien Provinces and captured Ningpo and
Foochow on April 2l. These operations extended the Japa-
nese blookade of the central and south China coast:
communication channels to the interior.
Approximately 2,O00 American troops despatched from
the United States arrived in Manila on April 22.

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
One. Japanese policy following conclusion of the
neutrality pact.
Following his return from Moscow on April 22 the
Japanese Foreign Minister gave no immediate indication
of what Japan's foreign policy will be save for a state-
ment that, like the Axis, Japan too "must be quick in
action in order to cope with the momentous need of the
times". Press despatches from Tokyo reported that Japan's
"new foreign policy" is being conducted with the Tripartite
Pact as its "immutable basis" under the double motto of
"peace in the Pacific" and "the construction of a new
order in East Asia".
The following developments would seem to suggest
that Japan is for the present concentrating its attention
on an attempt to bring to a conclusion the Sino-Japanese
hostilities: (1) the launching of military operations
along the Chekiang and Fukien coasts, discussed in
section four below; (2) the continued blockade of the
Kwantung coast; (3) the opinion of high Chinese officials
as reported by the Assistant Military Attaché at
Chungking on April 16 that they feared that one to two
hundred thousand Japanese troops would be withdrawn from
Manchuria for the purpose of conducting operations against
the Chinese; and (4) the emphasis given in the Japanese
press

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
press to the necessity of concluding the "China Affair".
With regard to Japan's policy of southward expansion,
the Ambassador at Tokyo reported on April 17 that despite
the pact with the Soviet Union there continued to be evi-
dent opposition to Japan's assuming the initiative in a
program of southward expansion. He was on April 18 in-
formed in strictest confidence by Baron Hiranuma (the
strongest personality in the Japanese Cabinet) through an
intermediary that at its last meeting, presumably on
April 15, the Cabinet had adopted a resolution that only
peaceful means were to be used in the advance to the south,
but that should foreign naval vessels be disposed in a
manner to constitute a blockade of Japan or should severe
economic pressure be exerted on Japan, the policy embodied
in this resolution would not be operative. It is, of course,
a matter of speculation whether the foregoing information
accurately represented the Japanese point of view or was
furnished Mr. Grew for ulterior purposes.
The Ambassador at Tokyo reported that military
developments in Europe and their effect upon American
policy continued to guide to a marked degree the Japanese
Cabinet in its pursuit of policies certain to involve
Japan in new hostilities and that British reverses in the
eastern Mediterranean area had encouraged pro-Axis
elements in Japan.
Two.

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Two. Russo-Japanese neutrality pact.
The Ambassador at Tokyo reported April 17 that the
Japanese press had been instructed by the Government that
it should not (1) give the impression that Japan is
enthusiastic about the pact, (2) mention the probable
opposition to the pact of certain anti-Soviet groups in
Japan, (3) mention Soviet weaknesses which caused that
country to conclude the agreement, and (4) mention condi-
tional provisions exchanged for the conclusion of the
pact. This last instruction was considered by the
Ambassador as perhaps signifying the existence of secret
commitments.
According to the American Ambassador at Moscow, his
Japanese colleague informed him on April 16 that upon his
(Tatekawa's) recommendation the question of aid to China
by the Soviet Union was not raised by the Japanese during
the negotiations for the pact as he had advised against
disoussing this subject because he saw no reason for
paying a price for a promise which he felt sure would not
be honored.
Three. Soviet-Japanese commeroial and fishery
negotiations.
The Ambassador at Moscow reported on April 17 that
he had been informed by his Japanese colleague that trade
negotiations between Japan and the Soviet Union were not
expooted

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
expected to be concluded before two or three weeks; that
the Soviet Union had agreed to take silk to the value of
one million yen and was to deliver to Japan comparatively
small quantities of platinum, manganese and oil; that the
Russians had pressed for tin and rubber which the Japa-
nese Ambassador had repeatedly told them were not
available to Japan for exportation; that Japan will attempt
to buy commodities in the Western Hemisphere and in the
south Pacific on behalf of the Soviet Union which may
finance the purchases; that some of these purchases may
ultimately be destined for Germany (Tatekawa stated that
Germany was seeking to obtain the entire Far Eastern soy
bean crop); and that as a condition for making a long
term fisheries convention the Soviet Union was seeking a
l00 percent increase in the fishing lot rentals.
Four. Sino-Japanese hostilities.
The Assistant Naval Attaché at Shanghai reported on
April 19 that Japanese troops had landed on the Chekiang
coast south of Shanghai and on April 21 that the important
port of Ningpo had been captured as well as other coastal
towns in Chekiang Province. The Japanese army spokesman
at Shanghai was quoted in press despatches dated April 23
as having stated that Japanese operations in Chekiang had
been completed. The Consul at Foochow reported on
Apr11 19

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
April 19 that the Japanese had effected landings on the
Fukien coast near Foochow and were advancing on that
provincial capital. On February 21, he reported that
Foochow had that day been occupied by the Japanese.
Apparently the local Chinese military offered only
slight resistance to the invasion.
Five. German-Japanese relations.
The Ambassador at Tokyo reported on April 17 that
members of the German Gestapo were becoming increasingly
active in Japan, reporting to the Japanese police the
names of allegedly anti-Axis Japanese and thereby causing
anxiety among certain quarters.
According to information received by the Ambassador
at Tokyo, considerable quantities of German manufaotured
goods had during the first three months of 194l entered
Manchuria. Among these goods were 15 torpedo speedboats
and parts, 43 locomotives, 3 submarines, and more than 72
carloads of aircraft manufacturing machinery.
The Embassy at Berlin reported on April 23 that the
forthcoming German-Japanese economic negotiations were
expected in Berlin to be of an extensive nature covering
European areas under German domination and Asiatic areas
under Japanese domination and providing for an exohange of
mutually benefioial commodities and manufactured goods.
S1x.

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.90)
-8-
Six. Reenforcement of American garrisons in the
Philippines.
Approximately 2,O00 American troops despatched from
the United States arrived in Manila on April 22.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
June 23, 1941
MEMORANDUM
Recently Captain E. Jackson of the S. S. Clevendon, an
American ship presently chartered by the Alaska Steamship Lines,
furnished the following information to a representative of this
Bureau at Seattle, Washington.
Captain Jackson stated that a year ago last March he
was in the Orient on the said ship unloading coal at the Amagasaki
Japanese Government Electric Plant, located halfway between Kobi
and Osaka, Japan. While unloading coal he related that members of
the crew including himself were held on strict surveillance by the
Japanese until they had searched the ship, making a special examina-
tion with the view to the location of firearms, and that all cameras
were withheld.
He indicated that the Amagasaki Japanese Government Electric
Power Plant consumes 2,O00 tons of pulverized coal a day. The power
generated at this plant supplies towns in the vicinity as well as
the shipyards. The destruction of this plant by any means would stop
production and effect hardship upon the population residing between
Osaka and Kobi, Japan. He indicated that there was little or no
fire protection in this plant and that coal was dumped in heaps, bins,
and under sheds which might create fires by spontaneous combustion.
The personnel of this plant was malnourished, it being the assumption
of Captain Jackson that the workers had been conscripted to work in
this yard.
It might be observed at this point that Japan is plenti-
fully supplied by nature with coal. The military group in Japan had
conscripted miners and others necessary in the personnel of the plant
which made it difficult for the Japanese to secure coal from their
owm mines. The discovery of the shortage of coal by the Japanese did
not occur until some hardship was felt with the result that large-
scale purchases of coal were made in Canada and in this country.

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
According to Captain Jackson, the food supply in Japan
during the year 1940 was limited. He indicated that the civilian
population was unable to secure the normal quantities of rice for
consumption and that the better qualities of rice were transported
by the Japanese for the use of the armies in China.
He indicated that there was very little leather avail-
able in Japan, most of this material having been commandeered by
the government for the use of the military and naval personnel.
It was difficult to purchase leather shoes in Japan during the
early part of 1940.
Captain Jackson indicated that the water supply for Kobi,
Osaka, and the vicinity, by reason of the absence of rain and be-
cause of the presumed deterioration of pipes and of the reservoir,
was limited to the extent that the population depending upon the
reservoir--the commmity and the vicinity mentioned--could only
draw water between the hours of 10:00 a.m. and noon daily. Difficulty
was experienced by the ship in securing enough water to take it to
the next port of entry.
In the early part of 1940, according to Captain Jackson,
an unnamed Japanese freighter put into the port of Hong Kong, sup-
posedly to undergo repairs. Little effort was made on the part of
the Japanese crew to effect the repairs alleged to have been needed.
Several high naval officers of the Japanese Navy were on board this
ship which had been planted in Hong Kong for the purpose of making
observations as to the activities in that port. The British authori-
ties at Hong Kong made every effort to have the ship clear the said
port and finally were able to have it leave by declaring the ship
seaworthy.
Captain Jackson further indicated that it was highly
probable that when and if relations between this country and Japan
become more strained the subterfuge of planting freighters in the
ports of this country for repair with the objective in view of spying
upon the movements of American naval ships and other vessels will be
attempted by the Japanese.

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.96)

=== Page 235 === (confidence 1.00)
July 25, 1941
MEMORANDUM
A dam, 120 kilometers from the mouth of the Yalu River,
and 80 kilometers above Shingishu was completed in 1939 at Suinho
in Korea. This dam measures 174 meters in length, 23 in depth,
and 38 meters in height. The power house is equipped with four
turbine generators, each of which has a maximum capacity of l00,000
kilowatts. Nearby the dam are two cement factories at Shokori and
Heijo, which draw power from the dam.
The Dairen to Harbin section of the South Manchurian Rail-
way is in good condition, however, the roadbed from Harbin to Manchuli
is in poor condition.
The Trans-Siberian Railway from Manchuli westward cannot
handle more than one train in each direction per day and each train
is normally made up of thirty wagons, each of which has a maximum
capacity of thirty tons.
The Seishin-Rashin to Harbin railway carries comparatively
little traffic. The German engineers are reportedly working on the
Harbin to Manchuli section and, up to the end of 1940, about 100
wagons a day were operating over this line. It was reported there is
virtually no traffic over the Harbin-Suifenho-Vladivostok section and
traffic on the Trans-Siberian from Vladivostok to Manchuli running north
of the North Manchurian border is fairly heavy.
In North China and Japan a shortage of cotton is developing
due to the activities of the Chinese Guerrilla forces who are seizing
most of this commodity before it reaches North China or the post of
Tsingtao for shipment to Japan.
Practically no shipmentsof peanuts are being made and the
shortage of this commodity in Japan has been accentuated by the culti-
vation of peamuts in Japan being curtailed in favor of other crops.

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Information concerning the Mitsubishi Heavy Industries, Air-
craft Plant, Nagoya, indicates that up until a few months ago there
were only fifteen German engineers in that plant, whereas now there
are at least sixty, and that there is always disaffection between
Japanese and Germans.
Production has attained a maximum of ten planes a day of
all types but lately it has been curtailed due to the shortage of
material. It was also reported that this plant had a British plane,
probably a "Hurricane" and was reported to have completed one similar
but that the Germans were dissatisfied and were making changes in it.
It was also reported that in February, 194l, fifty-five
Japanese were arrested in the plant for sabotage and that a great
amount of this type ofactivity took place.
It has been reported that a very extensive arsenal, manu-
facturing small tanks on a large scale has been built on the north
shore of Uchiura Bay in Hidaka Prefecture, Hokkaido and is believed
to extend five miles.
Information was further furnished to the effect that prepara-
tions are being made to heavily fortify the hills near Otaru, Hokkaido
and many military roads into the hills have been constructed but up
until December, 1940 no guns were mounted.
The Yawata Iron Works, Kokura is said to have 300,000 tons
of scrap iron in storage, enough for their requirements for eighteen
months and now employs 25,000 persons.
The branches of the Tachizawa Iron Works are reportedly
situated as follows:
1. Fuse factory, 65 Chodo Itchome, Fuse, Osaka Prefecture
2. Wakae factory, 549 Wakae-Mura, Naka Kawachi Gun, Osaka
Prefecture

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.95)
-3 -
3. Tamashima factory, 226 Higash, Shin-Machi, Tama Shima
Machi, Asakuchi-Gun, Okayama Prefecture
4. Tsuruhash, factory, Ikaino Nichome, Higashinari Ku,
Osaka City.

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: Apr11 17, 1942
SUBJECT:
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Paul H. Lacques of the legal firm, Bigham, Englar,
Jones and Houston, 99 John Street, New York City.
Mr. Schmidt
COPIES TO:

1—1493
On April 16
/Mr. Lacques said that he had telephoredto inquire whether
the Department could give him definite information of the
894.00
dates when the port of Kobe, Japan, had been closed to
foreigners during the spring and summer of 1941. Mr. Lacques
said that according to the New York Times of July 15, 1941
O
the port of Kobe had been closed for 10 days. He added that
C
he was particularly interested in information of the dates
when the port of Kobe had been closed prior to July 28, the
date when, he said, "freezing regulations" went into effect.
Mr. Schmidt informed Mr. Lacques that while he was
personally aware that the port of Kobe or at least certain
sections in that general area, including some of the piers,
had in the past from time to time been closed to foreigners,
he was unable at the moment to give Mr. Lacques any definite
information in regard to the dates of such closure. Mr. Schmidt
said that he would be glad to see what information he could
obtain for Mr. Lacques later.
Mr. Lacques requested that in order to facilitate re-
ceipt of the information, the Department telegraph him at
0.
his expense in New York.
刀
Mr. Sohmidt assured Mr. Lacques that he would do what-
ever he could to comply with the request.
After

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
After a careful search of the Department's files
Mr. Schmidt was unable to find any precise information
giving the dates of the closure of the port of Kobe,
Japan.
On April 17 Mr. Schmidt talked with Mr. Houston in
New York (Mr. Lacques was out of town for the week-end)
and informed him that the Department did not seem to have
available the precise information which Mr. Lacques had
requested. Mr. Schmidt added that it was reported, how-
ever, that the port of Kobe had been closed from time to
time during the past few years at the discretion of the
Japanese authorities. Closing the port had usually occurred
during times when Japanese troops were moved in and out of
Kobe. Mr. Houston was also informed that a report from
Japan indicated that the Japanese authorities had put
into effect a system of general passes for entry to the
docks at Kobe and that these passes had in some instances
been refused to American citizens and employees of American
firms. Those individuals were informed that it would be
necessary for them to obtain specific passes to visit
particular ships.
Mr. Houston expressed his appreciation for the in-
formation conveyed to him.

 Mr. L. . Chamberlain, Manager of the Osaka Branch of

Mr. Chamberlain called on May 29 upon Mr. Hamilton
and talked afterwards with Mr. Jones and with Mr. Coville.
He stated that he had no specific errand which brought him
to the Department, but that in a golf game Ambassador Grew
4.00
89
had suggested to him that, if he should find himself near
Washington, he call at the Division of Far Eastern Affairs
to give the Department the benefit of his recent information
about Japan. Mr. Chamberlain had left Japan in the latter
PS/LD
part of April and had been serving as manager at Osaka of
the National City Bank of New York's branch there. He
stated that he expects to return to Japan after a short.
period of leave, and that upon his return he will go to
Tokyo (to relieve Mr. John Curtis), and that it is in a
FILED
rather

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
rather optimistic frame of mind that he looks forward
to returning to Japan.
Mr. Chamberlain stated it as his belief that the
state of mind in Japan, so far as it affects probable
relations with the United States, has markedly improved
in comparison with six months ago and that a conflict will
be avoided. He gave it as his opinion that there has been
a recent sharp increase in the sense of weariness among
the Japanese people over the China hostilities, and growing
conviction that continuation of the hostilities on the
present basis would be both endless and fruitless. He
described the severity of various consunption restrictions
 in Japan and indicated that the scope of such restrictions
has effectively served to aggravate the people's discourage-
ment over the hostilities in China.
On the subject of the opportunities for continuation
in Japan of the activities of American banks, Mr. Chamber-
lain stated that unquestionably those opportunities are
waning at present but that he sees no reason to suppose
thatthey should not later return with more normal trade
relations.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
DATE: July 16, 1941
SUBJECTY
Possible composition of a new Japanese cabinet.
VI
PARTiciPAnTS: Mr. Takagi, Third Secretary of the Japanese Embassy.
Mr. Schuler.
千
1941
Division of
COPIES TO:
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
MR. WELLES
JU1A19.41
894.00/1092
Department of State
throichi
1-1408
During the course of a call this morning Mr. Takagi
asked if I had heard that the Japanese cabinet had resigned,
to Vhich I replied in the affirmative, and inquired whether
the Embassy had any news. Mr. Takagi said that the
Embassy was completely without information on this develop-
ment. In response to my query as to who might be the
next prime minister, Mr. Takagi said that he thought
that General Minami might come into power. By way of
explanation he said they tried almost everyone else. He
felt that the Foreign Ministry might go to Shigemiteu, who
JUL181941
is due to arrive in Japan this week. Commenting on
Mr. Matsuoka's position, Mr. Takagi said that he had
folt that Mr. Matsuoka's position was one of deadlook for

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
some time and that the only way that the deadlock could
be resolved was by some form of action or by Mr. Matsuoka's
retirement from the cabinet. Mr. Takagi was careful
to add that what he had said was purely speculation,
and that the Embassy had as much difficulty as foreigners
in discovering what Japanese policy was.
