=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
FE/
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
OF STATE
SE
BCB
NDER
1194
July 18, 1941.
JULY
MR. WELLES
NEW JAPANESE CABINET
Of the fourteen members of the new cabinet, seven
are civilians, four are generals and three are admirals.
OBF
Eight members, including Prince Konoye, were in the
me
preceding cabinet. One new member, Home Minister Tanabe,
is a Hiranuma protege. Of the remaining five new members,
General Yanagawa, Vice President of the Imperial Rule
0B
Assistance Association, is the only big figure. The repre-
sentation of big business and industry in the cabinet--
preceding representation minister without portfolio Ogura,
894.00
head of the Sumitomo interests, and Communications Minister,
AC
Murata, head of the Osaka-Shosen Kaisha--is further
strengthened by the appointment to the Agriculture portfolio
of Mr. Hiroyasu Ino, President of the important Japan
211941
894.0
Marine Products Company, and Admiral (retired) Sakonji,
President of the North Sakhalin Oil Company, to the Com-
merce portfolio.
Analysis of the changes in the cabinet brings out in
1093
relief two important developments: the repudiation of
PS/LDP
Matsuoka

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
Matsuoka and the appointment of a Nomura man* in his
place, and the revival of the influence in the government
of the Imperial Rule Assistance Association group.
With the repudiation of Matsuoka, with the strengthen-
ing of big business representation, and with greater power
for the totalitarian Imperial Rule Assistance Association,
one gathers the impression that the chief policy of the
new cabinet will be the strengthening of the defense and
internal economic and political structure of Japan; and
conversely, the avoidance of Matsuokan adventures or
entanglements that will diffuse the strength of the nation
and distract and divide its thought.
The purpose of this effort to unify thought and
strengthen the nation's economy implied in the cabinet change
would seem to be to improve Japan's national defense posi-
tion while prosecuting the China campaign more vigorously.
The complexion of the new cabinet is definitely conservative
and will undoubtedly restore some degree of confidence to the
anxious people of Japan. In view of the swing of the
pendulum to moderation which it represents, one may even
expect an effort from it to restore Japan's international
relations to normal. It is also probable that a strong
effort will be made to adjust relations with the United States.
Amotnoy
OBF
*Admiral Toyoda is brother-in-law of Admiral Takeda, closely
associated with the Mitsui interests.

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
The foregoing view 1s supported by the war weariness
and lack of enthusiasm for new wars which some observers
believe now characterize the Japanese people.
Another view is that the new cabinet has been
"renovated" and strengthened with the purpose of
efficiently carrying out orthodox Japanese concepts of
national welfare and expansion. Flamboyant talk of the
type indulged in by Matsuoka is not to be expected. How-
ever, Japan's deep-seated antipathy to and fear of
communism may be expected to be a dominant factor in the
attitude and acts of the new government. This would in-
clude definite possibility of an attack on Russia, al-
though such attack may well depend upon Germany's
attaining substantial successes against Soviet armies.
In this connection it may be mentioned that two of the
new members of the Cabinet drawn from business life are
heads of corporations dependent upon Siberian resources.
Moreover, elimination from the Gabinet of Matsuoka,
regarded as the person principally responsible for
Japan's entering into the neutrality pact with the Soviet
Union, tends to overcome a psychological barrier to
Japan's attacking the Soviet Union.
3.2.
FE:Langdon:OJL/HNS

27, 1941.
We are not informed regarding the author-
ship of this broadcast, but, from the internal
evidence, it is clear that the author knows a
good deal about Japan, Japanese personalities
and Japanese politics.
8
b
In the light of what he says, especially
on the last page (3), and of other evidence or
testimony to the same general effect, it is
altogether reasonable to believe that threat
of assassination hangs over the heads of any
Japanese high officials who oppose the militant
militaristic leadership [Incidentally, Konoye
is not really a leader--he merely has influence
(more negative than positive) because he is
Konoye]; therefore, if and in so far as he
advises or insists on a moderate or conservative
polioy and procedure, Konoye has been and is in
danger of assassination; hence, the gesture of
the proposal that he sail from Japan to meet
with the President of the United States in con-
ference does not necessarily create that danger
for him; in fact, that gesture, if consummated,
might be a way of escape. At any rate, a jump
from frying pan into fire does not prove that
he who makes that jump is 
exhibiting extraordinary courage.
Query arises whether, toward giving support
to the efforts of whatever there exists of
"moderate elements" in Japan toward, in turn,
bringing about accession to effective power in
Japan of a rightly minded control leadership
(in place of the militant militaristic element),
894.00
the

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
1556
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON POLITICAL RELATIONS
-2-
the kindest and the soundest course for
foreign countries, especially the United
States, to pursue would not be that of bend-
ing every effort toward ensuring a thorough
defeat and a complete discrediting of the
armed efforts of the military militaristic
leadership. Any kind of a "peace settle-
ment" or adjustment on any kind of compromise
lines which leaves the armed militant
militaristic leadership in control or even
in a position of influence would tend to
perpetuate a political philosophy and con-
comitant practices the outstanding features
of which are use of force, direct action,
resort to assassination, et cetera. So long
as even the roots of a militaristic philosophy
continue to flourish and have a popular place
in Japanese political and social life, the
nation will not be secure against the polit-
ical machinations of the chauvinistic leaders
in the armed forces, and officials will not
be secure against the pistol shots or the
sword cuts of fanatical self-chosen or
group-chosen patriots who, frustrated in
the field of intelligence and reasoning,
resort to methods of assassination. For
Japan to enjoy political health, may it not
be, is it not, essential that the cancer of
militant militarism which is deeply imbedded
in the Japanese body politic be destroyed and
eliminated?
S1CH

AUGUST 16, 1941 - 6.30 A.M., P.S.T.
NOTE:
THE FOLLOWING ARE SOME HIGH LIGHTS ON THE ATTEMPTED
ASSASSINATION IN TOKYO OF CABINET MINISTER BARON HIRANUMA.
THE ASSASSIN'S BULLET WHICH STILL THREATENS TO END THE
ED
CHECKER CAREER OF KIICHIRO HIRANUMA MAY PLAY A DECISIVE ROLE IN
HISTORY. THE STRICKEN BARON IS A FAR MORE IMPORTANT FIGURE IN
JAPANESE POLITICS THAN IS GENERALLY BELIEVED. AN ANALYSIS OF THE
PRESENT JAPANESE CABINET WILL REVEAL THAT IT MAY BE TERMED THE SECOND
HIRANUMA CABINET JUST AS ACURATELY AS THE THIRD KONOYE CABINET. BOTH
HIRANUMA AND PRINCE KONOYE HOLD THEIR POSITIONS ONLY THROUGH THE
COURTESY OF THE JAPANESE MILITARISTS. NOW HIRANUMA HAS BEEN ATTA
APPARENTLY THERE ARE THOSE WHO OPPOSE HIS POLICIES. IT IS IMPORTAT
AT THIS TIME TO DISCOVER WHAT HIRANUMA'S POLICIES ARE AND WHAT GROU
WOULD BE MOST LIKELY TO OPPOSE THEM.
UNTIL BARON HIRANUMA, AFTER GREAT STRUGGLE AND POLITICAL
MANEUVERING, ACHIEVED THE PREMIERSHIP OF JAPAN IN 1939 HE WAS SOM
THING OF A LEGENDARY FIGURE IN THAT COUNTRY.
IN INFORMED FOREIGN
QUARTERS THERE AND IN SUCH LIBERAL CIRCLES AS EXISTED IN JAPAN HE
RECOGNIZED AS ONE OF THE COUNTRY'S BAD MEN. OTHERS BELONGING TO
CATEGORY ARE GENERAL JINZABURO MAZAKI, RETIRED ARMY OFFICER WHO WA
A1
IMPLICATED IN THE MURDERS OF FEBRUARY 26, 1936, FUSOMOSUKE KUHARA,
INDUSTRIALIST WHO PUT UP THE MONEY TO FINANCE THE MURDERS IN THAT
REBELLION, ADMIRAL NOBUMASA SUETSUGU, JINGOISTIC NAVAL OFFICER, GENERAL
D
SABAO ARAKI, ONE TIME WAR MINISTER AND IDOL OF THE YOUNG OFFICERS
CLIQUE, AND TOSHIO SHIRATORI. AS FAR AS RESPECTABILITY IS CONCERNED

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
IT IS SOMETHING WHICH FEW WILL ACCORD THE STERN FACED BARON.
THE
FUNNY THING ABOUT HIRANUMA WAS THAT ONCE HE CAME INTO POLITICAL
POWER HE RENOUNCED THE VERY ELEMENTS WHICH HAD MADE HIM POWERFUL.
A FASCIST, UNTIL HE GAINED POLITICAL CONTROL, HE BECAME CONSERVATIVE
IN OFFICE. A BELÉIEVER AND AN INSTIGATOR OF DIRECT ACTION AS LONG
AS HE WAS A LAWYER AND A JUDGE, HE FOLLOWED A COURSE OF INACTION
WHEN HE HEADED THE CABINET.
JUST WHAT MADE PREMIER HIRANUMA A
TURNCOAT MAY NEVER BE KNOWN.
PERHAPS HE RESENTED THE GROWINGLY
HIGH HANDED GERMAN ADVISORS WHO WERE NOT ONLY MONOPOLIZING THE
IMPERIAL HOTEL BUT WERE EDGING TOWARD THE IMPERIAL PALACE.
AT
RATE HE MODIFIED HIS FORMER VIEWS. PERHAPS THROUGH THE INFLUENCE
OF FINANCEIERS LIKE SEIHIN IKEDA, ONE TIME MANAGING DIRECTOR OF THE
VAST HITSUI INTERESTS, HIRANUMA SOUGHT TO AVOID A CLASH WITH THE
UNITED STATES AND GREAT BRITAIN.
THE PAST IS RELATIVELY UNIMPORTANT
THE POINT OF INTEREST TODAY IS THAT HIRANUMA STILL HOLDS THE VIEW
THAT ANY JAPANESE ACTION WHICH WOULD BRING WAR WITH AMERICA
OR
BRITTIN WOULD BE FATAL TO JAPAN. IN THE PRESENT TOKYO CABINET
FINANCE MINISTER OGURA, HOME MINISTER TANABE AND LIEUTENANT GENERAL
YANAGAWA ARE RECOGNIZED AS HIRANUMA'S MEN.
HIS POWER IS OBVIOUS.
TO ANY JAPANESE SEEKING TO MAKE A SOUTHWARD PLUNGE OR A NORTHWARD
DRIVE HIRANUMA MUST BE ANATHEMA.
SO THE BARON HAS NOW BEEN DISPOS
OF. THE ASSASSIN'S HAND IS NOTHING NEW IN JAPANESE POLITICS. IT
HAS BEEN A RECOGNIZED METHOD FOR CERTAIN ELEMENTS IN THE ARMY AND
NAVY TO FORCE THE ISSUE.
THE ASSASSINATION OF PREMIER HAMAGUCHI
PAVED THE WAY FOR THE SEIZURE OF MANCHURIA.
THE MASS ASSASSINATIONS
IN FEBRUARY 1936 SET THE STAGE FOR THE INVASION OF ALL OF CHINA THE
FOLLOWING YEAR.
THE SHOT FIRED AT BARON HIRANUMA WILL BE THE PRELUDE

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
8
TO WHAT? ONE THING IS CLEAR. PRINCE KONOYE IS IN A TIGHT SPOT.
IT MAY BE ASSUMED THAT THE PRESENT PREMIER FAVORS A "WAIT AND
SEE" POLICY FOR JAPAN. IN THIS REGARD HIS VIEWS PROBABLY COINCIDE
WITH THOSE OF BARON HIRANUMA.
HERETOFORE THERE HAS BEEN THE IDEA
THAT PRINCE KONOYE IS OF TOO EXAULTED A LINEAGE TO BE IN DANGER OF
HIS LIFE, BUT IF A BARON IS ATTACKED IS A PRINCE ENTIRELY SAFE?
THE KONOYE CABINET MINUS HIRANUMA WILL DO CANNOT BE FORETOLD, BUT
THERE IS LITTLE EVIDENCE THAT PRINCE KONOYE HAS EITHER THE ABILITY
IF THEY SAY SOUTH,
OR THE DESIRE TO OPPOBE JAPANESE EXTREMISTS.
KONOYE IS LIKELY TO AGREE, IF THEY SAY NORTH, HE IS LIKELY TO
CONCUR. THE ATTEMPT ON THE LIFE OF BARON HIRANUMA HERALDS ACTION
IN THE PACIFIC.

September 11, 1941.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE CHIEF OF STAFF:
Subject: Political Developments in Japan.
1. A United Press dispatch from Tokyo dated September 1l, 194l,
gives the following information:
"Emperor Hirohito today took direct command of Japanese
Army Headquarters and moved to assure close Army collaboration
with Premier Fumimaro Konoye's Government, which appeared to be
trying to keep Japan out of war even if that meant drifting away
from her Axis ties."
2. Major developments are reported as follows:
a. Establishment of a new Defense General Headquarters
under General Otoso Yamada. Yamada is personally responsible to the
Emperor and becomes virtual Generalissimo of the Army superseding
previous emphasis on General Staff control.
b. Appointment of Fumio Goto, former Home Minister, as
chairman of the Central Cooperative Council of the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association, replacing Admiral Suetsugu.
c. Entertainment of the Cabinet at luncheon by the Emperor,
"in appreciation of its ontstanding services to the State."
3. A proper evaluation of this news is impracticable at this
early date but a definite trend seems indicated - a trend away from
the Axis and toward better relations with the United States and Great
Britain. The new system is interpreted as an effort to strengthen
the eivilian government, check militaristic domination of Imperial
Poliey, and erect a barrier to possible dissatisfaction among the
militarists with the future course of events.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.98)
1561
25
4. General Yamade is a conservative, of great energy end ability.
It is reported that he stands high in the Emperor's favor. Goto is
likewise a conservative and replaces one of Japan's worst jingoists.
The action of the Emperor in taking direct command of the Army and his
giving prestige to the Cabinet by inviting it to luncheon, and publioly
thanking it is unprecedented.
5. Barring a massacre of the conservatives by the militarists,
an event deemed unlikely in view of the Emperor's action, it is probable
that Japan will find a peaceful way out of one of the greatest crises
in her history and seek a means to realign her foreign policy in an
anti-Axis direction.
SHERMAN MILES,
Brigadier General, U. S. Army,
Acting Assistant Chief of Staff, G-2.
Distribution:

SUBJECT:
REVIEW OF AN ARTICLE IN THE AUGUST ISSUE OF
CONTEMPORARY JAPAN ENTITLED "THE THIRD KONOYE

894.00/1105
Por Dotriburlon Clack

The Honorable
101941
The Secretary of State,

Sir:
PS/MW
As of possible interest and as an example of an attempt-
ed rationalization, from the Japanese viewpoint, of the recent
reorganization of the Japanese Cabinet, I have the honor to
submit the following observations on an article by Mr.
Toshishighe Yomota appearing in the August issue of
cumman.
CONTEMPORARY JAPAN entitled "The Third KonOYe Cabinet
wormed". The author was formerly political editor of
the

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the YOMIURI SHIMBUN and now is an editorial writer of that
newspaper.
Following a brief account of reasons for the resig-
nations of each of the four previous cabinets which have
held office since the beginning of the current Sino-
Japanese conflict, the author advances the following
explanation for the fall of the second Koncye cabinet and
the formation of the third Konoye cabinet:
"The resiguation was to enable the succeeding
cabinet to cope efficiently with the unexpected
change in the international situation. In this
connection it must be remembered that Yosuke Matsuoka,
the Foreign Minister in the last Cabinet, was one of
its key members, ranking in importance and influence,
perhaps, next to the Premier himself. He certainly
gave an important reorientation to foreign policy
by concluding the Three Power Pact with Germany and
Italy, the Japan-Soviet Neutrality Pact, the Basic
Sino-Japanese Treaty and by his mediation in the
border dispute between Thailand and French Indo-
China. His record forms a brilliant page in the
annals of Japan's wartime diplomacy. But Mr.
Matsuoka is absent from the third Konoye Cabinet,
the portfolio of foreign affairs having been
given to Admiral Teijiro Toyoda, Minister of
Commerce and Industry in the second Konoye Cabinet.
It is, therefore, crystal clear that foreign policy
was among the important factors which decided the
cabinet change, and a section of the public jumped
too hastily to the conclusion that a reorientation of
Japan's foreign policy might be one of the results.
But this is at best a superficial view."
The foregoing remarks appear to confirm the more or less
general belief that the appointment of a new foreign
minister was the most significant change brought about
by the fall of the second Konoye cabinet. The author,
nevertheless, is generous in his praise of the accomplish-
sosukee
ments of Mr Matsuoka during his tenure of office and afrirms
that his retirement does not portend a change in the course
of Japanese foreign polioy.
To

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
To reconcile his admission that foreign policy con-
stituted an important factor dictating the cabinet change
with his contention that the retirement of Mr. Matsuoka will
not alter the course of Japanese foreign policy, the follow-
ing explanation is offered:
"Japan's national policy has long been formulated
as directed toward a complete settlement of the
China affair and the establishment of a lasting peace
in East Asia through a new order in this part of the
world.....Japan's foreign policy, whether in regard
to Europe and America or to East Asia, will continue
to be conducted upon the basis of those fixed prin-
ciples, and cannot, therefore, be affected in the least
by cabinet changes or the coming and going of foreign
ministers. Changes, if any, in Japan's foreign policy
can only be those which may result from a new Foreign
Minister's different attitude toward diplomacy or by
his modifications in method of conducting foreign
policy. Just as Yosuke Matsuoka, the retiring Foreign
Minister, had his own ideas of foreign policy and of
how it should be conducted, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda, his
successor, must have his own ideas and his own methods.
Changes in method we shall certainly see, and these
cannot fail to have their effect on Japan's diplomacy,
for it is impossible to imagine that Japan's diplomatic
measures, even if based upon an immutable national
policy, can be rigialy laid down in advance when such
sudden and unexpected developments of the international
situation o ontinue to occur. The most recent changes
in the international situation are indeed of such im-
portance that they presuppose changes in diplomatic
formulae".
An e xamination of the foregoing thesis seems unmistakably to
point to the conolusion that, although Japanese policy remains
"immutable", Mr. Matsuoka's "attitude toward diplomaoy" or
"method of conducting foreign polioy" had become outmoded.
A significant hint that henceforth Japan's Axis con-
neotions may not necessarily exert a controlling influence
on

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
on the nation's foreign policy and that, despite the
fact that Japan's policy is "immutable", there still remains con-
siderable room for the exercise of judgment on the part of
Japan in the charting of its course, may be read into the
following statement:
"Still, at the present moment when the interna-
tional situation is in process of radical c hange, Japan
mey be called upon to review the situation in which it
finds itself and take full cognizance of its destined
course. Following the conclusion of the Tripartite
Anti-Comintern Treaty with Germany and Italy, the ad-
vocacy of 'Axis' diplomacy as the essence of Japan's
foreign policy has been in the mouths of all, an ad-
vocacy further stimulated by the conclusion of the
Three-Power Pact, so that the signatories of the Pact
have come to be regarded as sharing in a common des-
tiny. Undertaking, as they have done, to fulfil a
common mission in the reconstruction of world peace
and to direct their foreign policies in the same
general direction throughout Europe and Asia, it may
be said that Japan, Germany and Italy are jointly
pursuing an 'Axis' diplomacy and share a common destiny.
This is a matter of principle. It is, however, an
entirely unwarrantable deduction from this principle
that any one of the signatories should pursue the
national policy of one of the others as if it were
its own or that any one of the signatories should
blindly follow one of the others in its conduct of
diplomacy. When this is considered, sections of the
Japanese public must be considered as open to the
criticism of having been unduly swayed by their ideolo-
gical sentiments and leanings and led to take up a
position of considerable difficulty by their advocacy
as an end in itself of a diplomatic policy which is
but a means to an end."
The author does not venture any opinion as to whether Mr.
Matsuoka should be numbered among those "open to the oriti-
cism of having been unduly swayed by their ideological
sentiments and leanings" but, inasmuch as the only oriti-
cisms direoted at him, though only by implioation, relate
to "attitude" or "method", such a possibility, in the ab-
once of other conorete criticism, is not excluded.
A

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
A rather surprising element is injected into Mr.
Yomota's discussion by his remarks in staunch defense
of international good faith:
"It may be a trite saying, but it is a funda-
mental truth, that the secret of success of a
country's diplomacy lies in its devotion to,
and its scrupulous observance of, international
good faith. Without international good faith,
there can be no constancy in international rela-
tions, nor any moral law in diplomacy. In the
world of today, however, the general tendency
appears to be toward complete disregard of in-
ternational good faith and morality in interna-
tional relations, and even to destroy the plain
dictates of international law without scruple, by
distorting it so t hat it is held to allow exactly
that which it so definitely prohibits. Japan, as
the advocate of the 'Imperial way' in diplomacy,
cannot allow itself to be swayed by such interna-
tional anarchism......
"An illusion, based on wishful thinking apparently,
possesses various foreign Powers, including Great
Britain and the United States, which surmise that
Japan's foreign policy will undergo some change as
a sequel to the recent cabinet change. This misunder-
standing shows their failure to grasp the fact that
Japan values international good faith and honour as
dearly as its national existence."
The validity of the foregoing argument appears to be
open to the criticism that it attempts, quite inexplicably,
to identify international good faith with consistenoy in
pursuing a fixed foreign policy while negleoting to
emphasize such manifestations of international good faith,
as that term is more generally understood, as the faithful
observance of obligations and engagements among nations.
Respeotfully yours,
710
iginal and 3 copies to Department.
opy to Division of Commercial Affairs

Pasadena, California
October 7, 1941.
MEMORANDUM ON JAPAN
(Private and Confidential)
GENERAL CONDITIONS (as observod in Kobe)
Despite vigorous governmental restrictions on
prices, the over-all cost of living was continuing to
rise gradually up to the end of August. Serious short-
ages had developed to com licate the price controls:
meatless days were frequent, eggs virtually unobtainable,
sugar and flour rationed to fanilies at a few days' supply
per month, even the staples, fish and rice were compara-
tively scarce. During August it became increasingly dif-
ficult to obtain vegetables, as the farmers were reluc-
tant to market their produce in the city at the control
prices. All these food difficulties naturally weighed
most heavily on the native population. Foreigners' ser-
vants were able to shop around, frequently at illegal
prices, picking up meat, poultry, etc. for the Frigidaires,
so that from their standpoint vegetables were the imme-
diate problems. Until the freezing regulations became
effective on July 28, sugar, flour, salt etc. could be
imported from Shanghai by parcel post.
Due to the shortage of gasoline private cars had been
practically non-existent for sone time. However, effec-
tive September lst, even taxis and busses, other than the
20% already equipped with charcoal burners, were to cease
operations, thus virtually signalling the end of civilian
motor transportation.
Clothing for the masses was entirely ersatz, only
the well-to-do being able to afford the scarce old-stock
materials. Leather and rubber were no longer obtainable.
Matches, coal and charcoal were thinly rationed. The
coming winter should be very trying from this angle, as
well as food. All canned goods in the country, except
for stocks already in the hands of retailers, were requi-
sitioned by the government as a reserve food supply.
RESTRICTIONS APPLICABLE TO FOREIGNERS
Prior to the freezing order effective July 28, foreig-
ners had been able to obtain remittance permits in moderate
amounts for payment of insurance premia and support of
family abroad. Outstanding permits lapsed automatioally
on July 28th and it was considered unlikely that further
exchange would be granted for such purposes, except in spe-
cial cases where applicants could prove a complete lack
of funds abroad, a very dif icult matter indeed.
From the middle of July telephone calls between towns
were permitted in Japanese only. Looal calls could be made
in any language but foreigners' lines were tapped almost
continuously. Censorship of mail addressed to foreign
points and points within Japan was begun at the same time.
The normal overnight service between Yokohama and Kobe then
became anything up to a fortnight.

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Several important covers despatched by one of the British
banks to its Hong Kong office on July 9, were held in the
local Post Office until August 25, when they were returned
with the explanation that there had been no boats sailing
direct to Hong Kong. As the usual mail route to that port is
via Shangha1 (three boats per week), this was considered to
be another method of "pinpricking".
Strict police supervision over foreigners has long been
the rule in Japan. This surveillance increased noticeably
during the summer, many individuals being shadowed constantly.
An extreme, but not unique, case was that of a British ship-
ping firm manager whose shadow took up residence across the
street, searched his home and office premises at intervals
and twice arrested his secretary for questioning.
After the middle of July travel by railway throughout
the Country was subject to official permission which was
granted or withheld in line with troop and naval movements.
Foreigners were barred from the Inland Sea, almost all
of which is a fortified area and the military highway to the
south.
Up to the end of August there had been no reported in-
cidents of anti-foreign violence or persecution such as the
anti-British demonstrations of 1940, but merely a rather
chilly atmosphere resulting from the tense international situa-
tion which was played up in the vernacular press as an anti-
dote to the lack of encouraging news from China. The sizeable
Axis Community received no preferential consideration from any
but official sources, and there were many indications during
recent months that Japanese in responsible quarters strongly
resented the manner in which some 15oo Naz1 agents with
diplomatic credontials were throwing their weight around in
government and press circles. The fact that such a proud
people would submit to being pushed around to this extent is
in itself a real indication of weakness, coupled with help-
lessness in the face of the pro-Nazi army group.
DEPARTURE FROM JAPAN AFTER JULY 28.
A large number of "designated foreigners" (i.e. British,
Dutch and Americans) were booked to sail for San Francisco
on the Nitta Maru from Yokohama August 7. When that sailing
was cancelled, owing to the difficulties encountered by the
Tatuta and Asama, all prospective passengers immediately en-
deavored to leave for Shanghai to connect with President
boats. While there was no mention of the term "hostage" in
Japan, it soon became obvious that these would-be "evaouees"
were being delayed by every possible means short of outright
detention. Between July 31 and August 23 one Dutch and three
Japanese ships left Kobe for the south by the outside route.
A number of Netherlanders, and three or four others managed
to leave by the Dutch boat on July 31, with no funds and very
little baggage, before the authorities could get their bearings.
The following regulations then came into force:-

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
In order to leave the country "designated foreigners"
were required to have:
1. Permit from the minister of Finance to purchase
S.S. ticket (application to state name of ship and
sailing date).
2. Ditto for S.S. Company to sell said ticket.
3. Police permit to leave the country.
4. Permit from the Minister of Finance to carry out
more than ¥ 50. in Japanese currency, or equivalent
in Letter of Credit, draft or check.
5. Ditto to carry out more than five pieces of lug-
gage containing only clothing and personal effects
of nominal value.
Failure to obtain any one of the first three permits
prevented departure. Since the military secrets regula-
tions forbade advance announcement of steamer movements,
it was impossible to apply for permits in time. More-
over, under government orders the steamship people con-
sistently maintained that their ships were fully booked.
This situation gave rise to protests from the foreign
governments and finally led to the departure of over lo0
British and American "hostages" on the Tatuta Maru from
Kobe to Shanghai August 28. The permit system was strictly
adhered to, which gave the authorities power to decide whom
they would allow to leave, but announcement of ship and
sailing date was made several days ahead in this instance.
Customs inspection commenced on the afternoon of the
27th and was very strict. A few persons succeeded in ob-
taining permission to take out household belongings, but
the majority carried only handbags containing clothing and
personal effects.
Embarkation on the 28th was made by tender from Wada
Point about five niles from the center of town, only Japa-
nese being allowed to board at the dock. Baggage which
had passed customs the previous day was also loaded along-
side, so that no one could tell whether or not his belong-
ings were aboard. Foreign passengers, including some 350
Polish refugees, assembled at Wada Point between lo and
1l a.m. for currency inspection and a sun bath on the open
jetty until 1 p.m. when the Tatuta appeared, anchored half
a mile off shore and a tender plied back and forth until 3.
Passport inspection took place on board, followed by lunch
consisting of three sandwiches and tea per person. Bag-
gage had been dumped below and was 90% inaccessible during
the voyage. Many passengers were entirely without luggage
until the day after arrival in Shanghai and some, including
American Naval Attaches from Tokyo, eventually discovered
that their effeots had never been put on board.

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
The prohibition against boarding at the usual dock
was an uncalled for expression of authority calculated to
inconvenience the evacuees, as no troop movements were
in progress at the time to justify exclusion of foreigners
from the wharf. The 30 odd men from the American Embassies
in Moscow and Tokyo were treated indiscreetly and will not
hesitate to broadcast their opinions where they count the
most. Several British and Canadian businessmen underwent
nervewracking and humiliating experiences in securing per-
mits anc passing customs.
The atmosphere throughout was extremely frigid, the
general impression being that they were reluctantly forced
for policy reasons to allow certain foreigners to leave,
but determined to make the oper tion as difficult and un-
comfortable as possible.

CONFIDENTIAL
de
October 16, 1941.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE CHIEF OF STAFF:
Subject: Fall of the Japanese Cabinet.
1. A United Press dispatch states that the three
894.00/11
months old Konoye Cabinet resigned on October l6th un-
der nationalistic pressure for termination of Japanese-
American peace negotiations.
894.0
2. This resignation was the logical result of
Foreign Minister Toyoda's failure to secure a relaxa-
tion of the economic pressure on Japan by the U. S.
Government.
3. It is impossible to predict the next move on
2
the part of Japan until the composition of the next
2
cabinet is known. It is highly probable, however, that
the trend will be toward the Axis, with the Army, rather
than the Navy, exercising the controlling influence.
This Army element will not be slow to take advantage
of any weakening of the Siberian Army brought about by
Russian reverses in Europe.
SHERMAN MILES
Brigadier General,
U. S. Army,
Acting Assistant Chier
of Staff, G-2.
PS

PS
M.Cer

ADDRESS TO THE
ECONOMIC ROUND TABLE OF SAN FRANCISCO
BY G. C. MERRILL
OCTOBER 21, 1941
There are possibly some groups before which you might
pome as a high authority on international relations; but after seeing
all the maps on the wall and hearing masterly critiques based upon them,
I cannot pose; but must be content with recounting a few personal notes
and letting you do the predicting.
My experience in the Orient consisted of five years in
Japan and one year in the Philippines working for the State Department
in a junior capacity. Mr. Grew was the Ambassador to Japan, as he still
is, and Mr. MoNutt was the second American High Commissioner to the
Philippines under the Tidings-McDuffey Act, providing for Philippine
independence in 1946. Admiral Yarnell was the Commander-in-Chief of
the Asiatic Fleet and Mr. Nelson Johnson the Ambassador to China.
Mr. Grew, Admiral Yarnell or Mr. Johnson would be the
men to talk to you about the broader political problems of the Pacific
area. I will have to limit myself to relating some of the incidents
which I saw myself and to say what these smaller things illustrated
as fundamentals which may continue to have a bearing on events in the
Far East, particularly Japan's actions. The most practical use I can
be to you is to give you some background in the light of which press
dispatches, which you may read from day to day, will occasionally have
more meaning
While I tell you of some of the small things that
happened in my experience there, may I ask you to work out in your own
minds the converse situations. That is, will you picture Orientals in
this country, rather than Americans in Japan, and see whether you oan
imagine parallel occurrences here. We have in this country Japanese
newspaper men, business men and officials and I do not believe examples
can be found of our Federal Government handicapping, molesting or irri-
tating them as private or corporate persons by reason of polioy.
When I first arrived in Yokohama in 1933 there were
two drug stores catering to the foreign residents. A few months later
ome remained. It was the one that was run by a Japanese. The foreign
1.

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)
cist, a Britisher in this case, had packed up and gone home.
hat was a squeeze play, plain and simple.
Once we were invited to swim at the beach house of a
European diplomat. Facing his own house from the beach, the Minister
photographed his own guests with his own house as a background. The
inevitable plain-clothes man appeared from nowhere and confiscated the
camera. On the map this beach house was located in a fortified area.
Diplomatic immunity or no, the diplomat never saw his film again. The
camera was reluctantly returned some weeks later with a figurative "So
sorry"
That little plain-clothes man is a permanent fixture
and as ubiquitous as a sea-gull wherever the white man comes and goes
in Japan. When we paid a visit to Nagoya and the American Consulate
there he met us at the station. He just turned up from nowhere as we
were checking a suitcase at the station. Since, by this time, we were
used to this quaint custom, we gave him our itinerary for sight-seeing
and the visit to the Consulate down to the minute. After you live in
Japan too long they say you almost feel guilty if you depart from the
schedule for five minutes as we did when we stepped into a department
store to buy an umbrella. After all that might have crossed up the
authorities. But since we had been honest and comparatively accurate,
we had only one more glimpse of him. He darted out from behind a
pillar to say goodbye as we left on the train. You see it was his duty
to cheok our departure. That could not be left open on the record.
A slightly more serious question required official
attention. A fine old Kentuckian who had lived in Yokohama for at
least thirty years was suddenly placed under detention on suspicion of
espionage. You may have a little difficulty believing this story, but
I handled the case from beginning to end and incidentally restored to
the records the American citizenship of this old gentleman. (It had
lapsed, since the Yokohama earthquake of 1923 had destroyed the reoords).
Now let me tell you the grounds upon whioh they based the charge of
espionago
First, this gentleman had formerly operated one of the first
2.

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.99)
ographer's shops in Japan. As he put it, "It was safer for me to
t out of that business and they told me so. Since about 1915, then,
I have supported my wife and myself by raising chickens and by sending
specially illustrated envelopes to stamp collectors in America. I get
the collectorst cancellations from the Post Office in out-of-the-way
places. A Japanese artist does a water color on the envelope from my
old photographs which I took in my sea-faring days. I have one of al
most every island in the Pacific and I charge the American collector
ten cents for the finished collectorst "Cover". I've been doing this
for years, but one particular collector in Kansas City wanted all I
could muster for him so I requested the Post Office in the Bomin Islands
to return me a cover with the special collectors' cancellatioh, and them
I dug up an old photograph of the Bonin harbor which I had taken in 1911
with the express permission of the mayor. Now the Bonin Islands are a
strategic zone and very secret in these days. The Yokohama Post Office
turned my envelope over to the police and they have been quizzing me
for three days; but I think they will let me off if I sign a confession."
I have given you a few among dozens of instances of
persecution or bureaucratic irritation. They happened every week even
when Japan was definitely at peace - that lull between Manchuria and
the Maroo Polo bridge incident. And yet here in this country we are
sorupulous in our offioial attitude toward representative Japanese and
their freedom to come and go. Only very lately have we come to realize
that there is only one way in whioh to prevent the molestation of
Americans in Japan - that is by applying in our own country the rule of
an eye for an eye and a tooth for a tooth. But we are hindered in the
application of this in small things as in large by our own apathy and
1nsistence on decency in the treatment of others, not to mention our
Don't let me leave you with the impression that these
comparatively minor cases were important in themselves. They could be
forgotten as they are here at home when they are read on the fourth page
of our newspapers. But the person who has lived in Japan knows two
thinge whioh prevent him from forgetting. The constant reourrence of
3

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.98)
type of case has a purpose. It is partly simple bureaucracy, inev-
able in any country, but it differs from the red tape of other countries
in the purpose and constancy with which it is applied. This constant
pressure against the white man and his business, especially since the
Japanese invaded Manchuria (and the western powers were powerless to
prevent that invasion) this pressure on the Americans and the Englishmen.
in particular, privately and officially, went hand in hand with intense
nationalism and imperialistic ambitions. Never for one moment did the
Japanese officials neglect to impress the occidental with his inferior
standing in the Orient and wi th the fact that his own Government's pro-
tection was no longer sufficient. The meticulous attention to detail on
the part of the Japanese in the application of these policies showed it-
self in the smaller cases to which I have referred just as it showed it-
self in the larger cases which were handled by the Ambassador and came to
your attention in the American Press.
These more important cases arose first in Manchoukuo
where today there are empty plants, offices and residences built for
American firms who counted too heavily on their own Government's ability
to maintain the "Open Door." For the Japanese Government Manchoukuo was
a good place to experiment with these decrees to exclude foreign businems.
Since we did not recognize the Puppet State we could not deal with ita
authorities directly. The decrees worked so well that they began to be
applied to Japan
Continuously while I was there, the managers of the oil
companies, American and British, as well as the American, British and
Netherlands Embassies, spent day after day and month after month trying
to hold the Japanese government to moderation in applying decrees which
bordered on confisoation.
At the same time, a gramaphone company was transmuted
into a Japanese firm from a foreign firm. There was compensation of some
sort although the American staff, with one exception, packed up and came
homo to look for mow jobs. But the result was achieved. This company
beoame Japanese in capital and management at a convenient interval before
the outbreak of the Chinm War.
4.

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.99)
You read in the headlines about a year ago that the
ternational News correspondent in Tokyo was held in jail for a matter
of months beoause he had shown what they referred to as an unfriendly
spirit. I do not believe any Japanese newspaper correspondent here 
and they are much more numerous than American newspaper men in Japan
has ever been jailed because of dispatches to his Tokyo newspaper re=
flecting unfriendly views toward our foreign policies.
While I was there another newspaper man, long a resident
of Kobe, was refused re-admission after having gone home to England and
returned to the very gates of Japan.
This man had had the courage to
conduct a constantly critical and very able analysis of Japanese politics
as long as they let him stay.
More recently the principal, if not the only, American
language newspaper owned and published by an American who had resided
most of his life in Japan, has simply folded up. It does not matter
how or what the consideration. The result is exclusion - pursued th
greater doggedness, small step by step, in contrast to our own blunt
single error of 1924, the Oriental exclusion from the quotas. Incidentally
this was a piece of unnecessary discrimination in the wording as finally
enacted, and gives us an eternal example of what happens to foreign
affairs when Congress takes the iniative. (Parenthetically the same might
be said of the Neutrality Act. It simply tied our hands and achieved
nothing. It definitely gave the "Go" signal to Hitler.)
Letme once more ask you to review this long chain of
private and corporate squeeze plays which have driven American business
out of Japan, Manthoukuo and that part of China which is now controlled
by Japan, and to consider what the effect on Japanese individuals and
business in this country would have been had we applied the same pressure
and perhaps inaugurated an imperialistio polioy and dominance of this
hemisphere. Must we not oonsider whether our own puritan restraint and
weakness for fair play has in the world as it operates today prepared
the way for our own downfall? It is not necessary to view our Orientall
problems through the eyes of those Americans who live in the Orients:
but it is well to remember that when treaties are broken and aggressiom
5.

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.98)
sed on nationalistic ambition, the old language of diplomacy is
longer effective and more than ever actions speak louder than words.
Behind the constant Japanese pressure for the exolusion
of foreign business there lie at least two fundamental motives; nation
alistic ambition as I have mentioned and a basic resentment at the past
controls exerted over China, and Japan by the western powers including
Germany. Hand in hand with this ambition and this well-cultivated re-
sentment there go an unexpressed sense of inferiority with its counter-
part of Oriental "face," as well as an unrelenting campaign to establish
precedents breaking down treaty relationships and shaving off one by one
the heretofore accepted rules of International Law.
What the Japanese have never understood and do not to-
day understand is that our American advocacy of legal procedure in inter-
national relations which we have so often publicly expressed is genuine.
Their failure to understand this is not entirely their fault. Law is a
thing completely strange to them. Emerging suddenly from feudalism and
isolation, as the Japanese did, into the hard glare of the nineteenth
1
century commeroialism of the world, they had no opportunity to weigh the
reasons for the development of constitutions, statutes and legal procedure.
This is even less surprising when we think of the poss-
ibility that Roman and Napoleonic Law has perhaps never really been com-
prehended by the Germans. In this light perhaps the Japanese feudalistic
oode of life, going hand in hand with amazing industrial and trade ex-
pansion and military conquest, appears less contradiotpry.
The Japanese tell their own people that the comforts,
luxuries and indulgences as well as the laws and legal procedure of the
western world, American puritanism and the negative ethios of Christian-
ity can never be applied to their life without undermining the Japanese
stoioism so necessary to the fulfillment of their imperial destiny It
1s in this way that they have sold their own people on a low standard of
living, so-callod.
Their failure to understand the law as we see it explains
to some degree their opportunistic diplomacy and military policies as
well as many of their domestio problems.

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.99)
Domestically in Japan the vast majority of legal suits
re settled by compromise. A disputed property line, a suit for damage
by an automobile, blackmail of a young bachelor by a Tokyo barmaid,
in fact, any allegedly unfulfilled contract - all of these are almost
forced by the local prosecutors to be settled without reference to the
statutes and almost invariably on the basis that the party who can
afford to pay loses.
If you shoot a burglar in your own house you, not the
burglar, are the culprit before the law.
Then add to the avoidance of the law, its circumvention,
or (more correctly) a failure to grasp its purpose in the community
two further essentials in the Japanese book of rules. These are prece
dent and "face"
To discuss precedents:- One of the last things a Japanese
subordinate would do is what one of our Consuls did in the troublous
days in Central America. He telegraphed Washington for permission to
raise the American flag over the Consulate because a revolution was
brewing and the Consulate was near the center of the likely rioting
Washington cabled back: - "No precedent". The consul then raised the
flag over the building and cabled Washington: - "Precedent established."
In the Japanese book of rules this would be a crime
obliging the Consul to cut out his entrails. It would be insubordi-
nation, formerly referred to in the pre-New Deal Era, I believe, as
rugged individualism. Second, it would not be in the Consul's written
Regulations.
It would espeoially mean that such a Consul, if Japanese,
was not goose-stepping. The precedents are set only on orders from the
topo
In 1933, two weeks after I arrived in Japan, I was struok
with the similarity of the public character of the Japanese and that
oharacter of the Germans which is essentially regimentation, national
ambition, and the devotion to both on the part of all the people. I
never had ocoasion to revise that conolusion.
7.

=== Page 366 === (confidence 0.99)
A word or two more on the question of the Rule of Law:-
he Japaneme Constitution is so elastic that Dictatorship has crept in
through the provisions originally made for demooratic evolution. The
framers of that document were sound men. They knew that to ask a
people to proceed from feudalism to representative government in a
generation was, after all, not realistic. They were patriots trying
in their own way to make provision for the success of parliamentary
government and the development of an onlightoned public opinion. But
to permit of leadership in the interim they restricted the franohise
and left the final say with the Emperor. The Emperor was then the
astute and liberal Emperor Meiji but for whose wisdom, the late Prince
Saionji would have been left without further influence deplorably early
in his career.
I have mentioned Saionji because among other things
he was one of those rare Japanese who knew full well that "face" is a
moral escape from the rigors of ambition or embarrassment.
The importance attached to "face" simply demonstrates the
ever presence in the Japanese mind of the matter of prestige both per-
sonal and public. Is it not correct to say that half of the time em-
barrassment arises from undue seeking of prestige? If so, we see a
little more clearly why the loss of "face", a drastio occurrence in
the life of the Japanese, is part and parcel of their ambition.
In 1936 on the morning of February 26th there was &
remarkable snowfall in Tokyo and Yokohama. There was an eerie silence
over the comings and goings of plain men. The streets were muffled
by nature. On that day there occurred a move which invoked a train
of disaster some Japanese would like to erase from their memories and
from history. They murdered their Prime Minister - they thought. But
it turned out to be a olose relative who resembled him. They tried to
murder Prince Saionjiibut his thoughts were a lap ahead of theirs.
They murdered
few others; but most espeoially they killed their
Finance Minister, Baron Takahashi. With him there disappeared the
8

=== Page 367 === (confidence 0.99)
Today we see the result.
Perhaps we have found a olue to the policies of Japan.
Since that fatal day every cabinet which has been formed has gone far-
ther and farther toward support of "military extremists". There seems
to me to be no other brief summary or description than the single word
"ambition"; but that does not explain the methods which have been used
Much has been learned in the Orient from the diplomatic
methods of the European Dictators to whom they have had to bow.
In 1936 Japan signed the Anti-Cominterin Pact with
Germany. In 1939 one of her cabinets tumbled because the same Germany
closed a deal with Russia for the division of Poland and the Baltic
countries. Yet today there is a new triple alliance of Rome, Berlin
and Tokyo and once more the fingers are crossed with reg rd to Russia.
So European events have run hot and cold over the progress of Japan's
Asiatic ambitions end we must admit that there have always been European
events that have dominated the course of affairs in the Orient. Today
that is still true
German raiders have been reported in the Pacific
flying the Japanese flag and yet Japan is very anxious over the course
of her relations with Russia and she is not unaware of the strings
attaohed to, or the price she may have to pay for, cooperation with
Germany
Let me quote a January issue of the publication of the
Institute of Pacific Relations, San Franoisco Office - I cannot improve
on the comment:- I quote
"Japan's (eventual) decision (in relation to the Axis)
might have a powerful influence on the development of hostilities be-
tween the Axis powers and Great Britain, but it would mean absolutely
nothing in terms of Japan's own program for Asia. Japan's polioy really
1s immutable' - it is a permanent commitment - and the Axis powers are
simply one of the instrumenths to be used in advancing it. Repudiatiom
of the Japan-Axis pack would not signify that Japan had repudiated the
New Order in Greater East Asia; it might indicate that the New order
was being returned, temporarily, tè the icebox"
9

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
"If the logic of events is crowding Japan toward 
lean-out decision, it applies no less to other nations fringing the
hostilities of Europe and Asia. A MOVE TO CAST JAPAN'S LOT WITH THE
TOTALITARIAN POWERS MIGHT PUSH THE UNITED STATES BEYOND ITS PRESENT
ANOMALOUS POSITION TO A MORE POSITIVE SUPPORT OF THE STATUS QUO."
Since you may have a question with regard to present
events let me quote from another important article in "Foreign Affairs"
in which Professor Griswold of Yale maintains that events in the Orient
have been and are still today dependent upon and secondary to those in
Europe. He says:
"The decision of the American Government has been made
to conoentrate whatever energies and resources it can spare from its
own defense program on assisting England to withstand the German siege.
This does not mean that the United States has turned its back on the
Far East. Far from it. It does mean that no major decision of Far
Eastern policy is taken in Washington without a preliminary appraisal
of its oosts or benefits to the British war effort.
"How much latitude this rule permits for American
diplomatic action in the Far East is a question compounded of many
elements: the relative effectiveness of the Chinese and Japanese armies
in their present theatre of war; the relative naval and air strength
that the British, Dutch, Australians and New Zealanders could muster
against the Japanese in East Indian waters; the 2,920 miles from
Yokohama to Singapore and the 6,107 miles from Singapore to Pear1 Harbor;
the relative indispensability of East Indian and Malayan rubber to the
United States and of American, cotton, iron, steel, oil and tools to
Japan. But these are as chips on the gaming table in comparison to the
basio will of the American people regarding the role they intend to play
in world politios. There is no doubt at all as to what role they would
like to play. If all they had to do was to pull a lever, they would
immediately bring peace and justice to both Europe and the Far East,
which, practically speaking, would mean a free and independent England,
France and China, the demobilization of the Axis legions and a universal
restitution of human, i.e. civil liberties. How far they are prepared
10

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.97)
to accomplish this end in the difficult byways of world politics
Jutside their own hemisphere is another matter."
There is one more story I have just learned of an
official who was just recently detained one month in Japan with 57 pieces
of official and personal baggage from the American Embassy in Moscow.
one
worthy Hoover Republican, I understand, was amazed at this American's
annoyance with the Japanese over this affair. But it's typical. Not only
by treaty: but as a matter of national interest - until this world was
turned upside down - immunity and courtesy were extended to diplomats and
diplomatic passports. This is another instance in which the converse case,
a Japanese diplomat returning through The United States from Europe 
could not happen under our State Department which does not yet badger
To
individualsofficials, properly documented, just to see how to much high-

handedness his government will knuckle under.
In conclusion I shall say very little. The broader
questions of today may, and doubtless will, be raised in the next half
hour. But I will venture one broad proposition.
As was said on Admiral Yarnell's program Saturday evening,
when Japan joined the Axis it was to fight and conquer, sooner or later.
She has not swerved for a moment from her aims in Asia. "Chinese co-
operation" in the "Greater East Asia Policy" means conquest and domination.
When they talk about "moderates" in the Cabinet, it means men who prefer
only to stab later, rather than sooner, at the rubber and tin and chromium
at Singapore, the Philippines and Alaska. They have thumbed their nose
at us for at least nine years; they have put their hands behind their
baoks at intervals; but they are consumed with ambition and the time is
ripe, or soon will be, for them to strike at a falling, if not fallen,
Russia, divert American aid from the enemies of Hitler and split our
ooean defenses which may well be those of the last opponent of the Axis
domination of Europe and the Atlantic.
Do we want to wake up to this sort of a situation just
when that half of Hitler's armies, whioh are not at work consolidating
his hold over the Crimea and its resources, is turned southward or west-
ward again from Central Europe?

1. System of Government.
a. Form of Government: Extent of Suffrage. Japan is a consti-
tutional monarchy, the imperial constitution having been voluntarily
promulgated by the Emperor, Mutsuhito (Meiji), in 1889. All of the
new political machinery was taken from or influenced by Western methods.
The franchise has been extended twice since the 1889 Constitu-
tion anû since 1925 embraces all mon over 25 who have a permanent resi-
dence. But the ordinary man attaches little value to his vote and can
be easily persuaded to sell it to a politician more adept in such
matters.
b. Stability of Government. The Japanese Government is one of the
most stable in the world. This is due to the belief that all Japanese
are related by blood to the Emperor who is their father and the repre-
sentative of the Supreme Being on earth and who reigns over his people
as a spiritual leader. He is supposed to be the most perfect person
because he is most directly related to the Supreme Being. and he is
therefore more just, more ethical, and more pious then any living being,
and so the nost fit person to rule. Those related nore closely to the
ruler are supposed to be more ethical and nore pure than those indi-
rectly related. The basis of Japanese governnent is the assunption that
all Japanese are the obedient and living people and children of their
fathers, the sovereigns, and "rejoice that they are so wisely governed
by a line of Emperors unbroken for ages eternal" - ("The Inperial Way",
or "Kodo"). Because of this, society is more inportant than the indi-
vidual, all men are not considered equal and the governing nan is
superior to written law. Law is considered as a nonentary decision
and is dependent entirely on the will of the Enperor.
Because of Japan's adhorence to the "Inperial Way", a national
revolution scarcely seens inninent. Nor will it adopt conplete
fascisn, any form of communisn, or even entiro totalitarianisn. Shades
of socialisn and believers in it are known to have oxisted in the
governnent, but they have existed only behind the facade of the
traditional nanes and custons. More frequently the radicals forn
secret societies just as other groups have done in the past, and they
are still in existence. Actually, secret societics are legally banned
and their members are liable for arrest.
In 1926 the Conintern organized the Japan Cormmunist Party, but
it was never given legal recognition in Japan. With the enactnent of
the Peace Preservation Law in 1928, a thorough and systenatic hunt
for conunists was initiated and the conunist novenent was soon driven
underground, never to emerge again.

c. Organization of Government and Method of Operation.
(1) Theory and Practice of Governmental Administration.
Although the Japanese constitution invests the Emperor with all
executive, legislative, and judicial powers, he does not rule in the
actual sense of the word. He is the facade for the civil govornment
and reigns in the spiritual sense over his children. In practico ho
dispenscs power to the various organs of the government on the advice
of the Premier, the Lord Keeper of tho Privy Seal and the Minister of
the Imporial Housohold, and makes appointments in the same fashion.

for the enactment of important business,the last two men vicld consider-
able influence; the Minister of the Imperial Household may prevent or
delay audiences with the Emperor, and the Lord Privy Seal can refuse
tho seal. The result is that should either of those two men disepprove
of cortcin acts thero is no legal procedure to put them into effect
beccusc thoy can cither refusc audicnce with the Emperor or refuse the
soal. Therefore one source of influence upon tho government oxtends
from the Imperial Houschold. Another is from the Premior and the

of the Cabinot is inpossible and it is noccssary to forr: tho Cabinet

the arny and tho navy. United, the latter also forn a consultetive

nane of tho Enperor. In brief, the influence that an organ can assuno

Boccuse of tho various bodies of governnont which are able to
assunc power, dissension can hardly be avoidod. Since the boginning
of tho China Incidont tho ary and navy, which nay bo jointly referred
to as tho nilitary group, have overshadowed the ther organs. Inasruch

and councillors and power lios largely with influence, assossinations
have boen tho order of the day and the ilitary have ridden into

The press in Japan exerts considerable power around commer-
cial districts such as Tokyo, Osaka, or Kobe, but a large part of
agrarian Japan is indifferent to politics. The Japanese express
their dissatisfaction in the form of riots, strikes and ethical in-
difference rather than in attempts by the people to control the
government.
(2) Branches: Correlation of Powers.
(a) Executive. The Emperor. As already mentioned, the
Japanese constitution invests the Emperor with all executive, legisla-
tive, and judicial powers. He has the supreme command of the army and
navy through the chiefs of the General Staffs, and the two service
ninisters, who are responsible directly to him. The Imperial prero a-
tive over the supreme comaand is outside the scope of parliamentary
responsibility. In the exercise of his prerogative, the mperor is
assisted by the board of field marshals and fleet admirals, the "Gensui-
fu"; and by the High War Council. the "Gunji Sangiin". For the
management of domestic affairs he operates through the Privy Council.
The Imperor's authority over general affairs of state is
very considerable. His power to convene, prorogue, and dissolve the
Diet is limited only by the stipulation that the body must meet at
least three nonths during each year. Through his ministers of state,
he has charge of the initiation of bills in the Diet. he sanctions and
orders the promulgation of the laws passed by the Diet and issues
emergency ordinances. He can legislate together with the Diet, but
his imperial ordinances become inoperative if not approved by the Diet
at its next session. Presunably, he also could refuse to sanction a
law passed by the Diet, even though it had been initiated under his
directions; but his assent has never been refused. He has conplete
charge of appointments, pardons, and similar matters. nd the Diet
can do little, in a direct nanner at least, to curb his absolute con-
trol of foreign affairs, even to the making of wars and peace and the
conclusion of treaties. In this connection, however, it shculd be
noted that though the declaration of war and the making of peace are
placed under the control of the mperor, the military authorities may
force war or peace by sending an aggressive expedition in the one case,
or by withdrawing troons in the other, for they alone are competent
to "advise" the mperor in such a matter. Only the mperor has the
right to amend the constitution. It should also be noted that, in
practice, the Emperor exercises this authority over the general
affairs of state only with the vice of his ministers.
The Lord Privy Seal. The theoretical function of the Lord 
Privy Seal (also called Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal), is to advise
the Emperor on the formation of a new cabinet when the old one falls.
He is also the first one consulted by the Emperor in every cabinet
change, or when other important decisions are about to be considered,
and exercises in general the functions formerly discharged by the
Genro. Though the Lord Privy Seal is an officer of ministerial rank,
he is not a member of the Cabinet, being directly appointed by the
CONFIDENTIAL

Emperor and holding office at the Emperor's pleasure.
The Council of Elder Statesmen (Genro - meaning "old and

Restoration originally. They held great power in that no critical
stops were over token without consulting thom. The Gonro might give
counsel contrary to that presented by the Cabinet and in such cases the
opinions of tho Eldor Statesmen would prevail. Tho powers of the

body of officials, consisting of the Lord Kceper of tho Privy Seel,
all ox-premiors and the Presidont of the Privy Council has already
detormincd the leadership of the last three cabinets.
The Privy Council. It was established in 1888 and

liost of its actual work is donc through sub-committoes.

The nore astuto nenbors f the Council often wiold a

tho reconmondations of the Privy Council or to resign. To potition
the Enperer to ignore the advico of the Council would bo to throw

b. Political Parties and Groups.
(1) Immortance of Party Organization in Political Life of

e. Education. A comprehensive code of education was issued after
the Restoration in l867 requiring every child to attend school from
the age of six to twelve for the purpose of fostering a national spirit.
Thanks to the system of compulsory education the Japanese poople are
among the most literate in the world. All social classes are officially
on the same levol; all childron are taught that nationalism, or loyalty
to the Emperor is the groatest virtue a man or woman can attain, for
nothing is highor than tho Emperor.
"Dangerous thought" is controlled in education by merely not
teaching anything which is not approved by the Minister of Education.
When young men are sufficiently educated in the "Imperial Way" (Kodo),
they are sometimes encouraged to road the newer doctrines, but it is
assumed that at this stage they will be so completely loyalist that
this literature will only increase their belief in Japanesc governing
theory and increase their distaste for countries not sharing it.
In regard to present trends in education, Prince Konoye
announced recently that thero will be even greater regimentation of
public thinking and that he forcseos "an educational system in which
there will be established ethical principles of the nation stressing,
above all, service to the State and eradicating all selfish and mate-
rialistic thoughts".
f. Foreign Influenco in Internal Politics. The rigid nature of
the Japanese State precludes any direct foreign influence in internal
politics at presont. Novortheless, through thc channols of Western
languages, most of which are studied in Japan, the people have become
acquainted with the ideas of the West. Formerly, thero was some

3. Intornational Political Situation.
a. Bearing of Intornal Political Situation on International
Policies. The military today holds a very strategic place in the
internal political situation in Japan. With its influence it is able
to shape the policy of the whole governmental system. Previously, for
years Japan reduced expenditures for the army and the navy and was

b. Foroin Policics. Japan's forcign policies revolve around the

In regard to tho first problen, Prince Konoye's foreign progran

The origin of the Tripartite Pact of September, 1940, is found
in this shift of Japanese attention from China to the areas in South-
eastern Asia. Japan needed the support of Germany, the conqueror of
France and the Netherlands, to further its design on the French and

c. Foreign Relations.
(1) Alliances and Ententes. Japan formally joined the Axis
powers on September 27, 1940, in a Three-Power Alliance. In this
pact Japan, Germany and Italy agreed "to assist one another with all
political, economic, and military means, when one is attacked by a
power not at present involved in the European war or in the Sino-
Japanese conflict". This plainly means that if the United States goes
to war on the British side, Japan goes to the assistance of the Axis.
Hungary joined the Axis on November 21, 1940, followed by Rumania and
Slovakia. Japan also has treaties with its two puppet states of
Manchoukuo and Nanking, providing for military cooperation, but in view
of the complete dominance of Japan over its satcllitos, these treaties
can hardly be regarded as alliances or ententos.
Sino-Japaneso Relations. Japan's relations with China must
be divided into two parts. The first is political and economic in
character and concerns that part of China occupied by Japanese forces
beginning in July, l937, and nominally ruled by the puppet government
of Wang Ching-wei at Nanking. The second part comprises the military
operations carried on by Japan against unoccupied China and the
Nationalist Government at Chungking. The Japanese plan to bring tho
Chungking Government to its knees and unite its territorios with
those of Japanese-occupied China and absorb all China into the "Greater
East Asia Co-prosperity Sphore".
Occupied China. Strictly speaking, there are no Japanose-
occupied "areas"; there are only Japanose-penetrated "linos" and
"points" - broken "lines" and isolated "points". The Japanese have
invaded the following areas: North China (Hupei, Hunan, Shantung,
and Shansi); Central China (Hupei, Hunan, Anhwei, Kiangsi, Kiangsu,
Chekiang); and South China (Fukien, Kwantung, and, until Juno, 1940,
Kwangsi). In these aroas there are altogether 1,O33 districts (hsien).

There is no evidence that any annexation of Japanese-occupied

The Japanese have, however, made several efforts in the past to set up

Japan had been pursuing a policy of "divide and rule".
The Liang regime failed to obtain the cooperation of the
Chinese in the occupied areas and led the Japanese to make an attempt
to have a more reputable Chinese leader to head tho Nanking regime.
The two most outstanding Chinese who had not fled with Chiang Kai-shek

Unoccupied China. The maneuver phase of the hostilities in
China may be said to have ended at the close of l938 after the fall of
the Wuhan cities and Canton, but there were, nevertheless, several large
scale operations during the succeeding three years.
Japanese military operations during January, 1940, were
involved with continual fighting over most of the fronts in China, but
were featured by three outstanding developments: the campaign in
Suiyuan province; the operations centered around Nanning; and the
"strategic" withdrawal from northern Kwantung. Beginning in January,
1940, and continuing to the present (August, 1941), systematic and
intensive bombing operations were carried out by Japanese airplanes
directed against Chungking and many other inland cities. In the hope
that a final crushing blow could be dealt to the Chungking Government,
the Japanese embarked on a series of destructive air raids on the
provisional capital from August 8 to August 13, 194l, during which
period Japanese navy planes bombed Chungking 49 times. Much property
damage was done (including a near-hit on the American gunboat Tutuila,
moored in the Yangtse) and there was considerable loss of lives, but
the morale of the Chinese remains high and their determination to fight
to the bitter end remains unshaken. The Chinese, on their part, in-
tensified the activity of their guerrillas, and also retaliated with an
outbreak of terrorism against Japanese officials or puppet regime
officials in the foreign settlements in Shanghai and Tientsin.
Land operations during the greater part of l940 were on a
small scale and indecisive in results. The absence of any largo-scale
military activity during that period indicated that the Japanese were
concentrating on political rather than military maneuvers. It will be
remembered that this was the time when the Wang regime was being
primed for action designed to win over the Nationalist arnies by
blandishments and promises.
When this venture ended in failure, the Japaneso then turned
their attention toward the south coast of China, hoping to cut off
Chungking's supply routos. Operations beginning in the spring of
1940 ended in the occupation, in April, of Ningpo, Foochow, and other
points on the Chekiang and Fukien coasts. This largely completed the

At present there is little military activity in China, as the

Japan's expansionist venture in China rounded out four years
on July 7, 1941, and has cost Japan more than 25,000,000,000 yen
(about $6,000,000,000) or a sum onc and a half times the Japanese
income in 1936. Within the last month, there are indications that
the new Foreign Minister Toyoda has sent out feelers to England and
America for suggestions leading to a liquidation of this costly and
apparently futile Japanese invasion of China.
Relations with France. The Japanesc are fully aware that
France, as a result of Germany's victory, is in an even weaker position
than Groat Britain in its inability to back up a firm stand in tho Far
East. The Japanese, in consequence, rogard the French colonial
possessions in the Far East as ripe for the picking and have pursued
a steady policy of lopping off the French possessions one by one. The
occupation of the Island of Hainan by Japan, regarded as being within
Franco's "sphero of influence" was followed by a Japaneso occupation
of the Spratley Island archipelago in the faco of French protests.
Even before the war tho Japenese regarded French Indo-China
as being within the Japanese economic orbit. It was obvious that the
fall of France would bring about a Japanesc advance in Indo-China.
Thereafter the Japanese, under cover of one pretext after another,
steadily encroachod on tho helpless colony. Claiming that North Indo-
China was necessary as a base from which to bomb the Burma Road, the
Japanesc forced the French colonial government to agree to Japanese
military occupation and tho use of French air bases. Following this

The announcement on August 22, 1939, of the conclusion of the
German-Soviet non-aggression pact was received in Japan as a stunning
blow. For a while relations between Germany and Japan were cool and
Germany was accused of treachery. Later, relations fell back into a
comparatively normal state and Japan decided to return to the fiction
of the Anti-Comintern Pact with both Germany and Italy.
Germany's failure to end the European War in 1940 led to a
political rapprochement with Japan and a political mission was sent by
Berlin to Tokyo to strengthen the Berlin-Rome-Tokyo Axis. An Imperial
Conference held on September 19, 1940, sanctioned the conclusion of a
defensive alliance with Germany and Italy. This alliance, known as
the Tripartite Pact, envisaged action against a probable American
intervention in the Far East. The two European partners of the new
Axis committed Japan to a course which would ensure the continuance
of the main part of the American fleet in the Pacific and thus
diminish the possibility of an American participation in the war
directly against Germany. In return, Germany and Italy promised full
support of Japan's policy in regard to French Indo-China and the
Netherlands East Indies, and also to induce Russia to cooperate in
relieving any Japanese anxieties in the North.
The conclusion of the Tripartite Pact was followed by much
German activity in Japan and led to some resentment among the Japanese
at the sudden foreign influence in their domestic affairs. The
activities of German commercial agents in Manchuria and Japanese-
occupied China are in particular much resented by Japanese commercial
firns apprehensive as to the future of Japan's own economic stake
there in the event of a German victory.
It is not surprising that there should be once again a change
in Japan's relations with Germany when the latter invaded the Soviet
Union. The leading exponent of a pro-Nazi policy in Japan, Foreign
Minister Matsuoka, was dropped fron the government and there are indi-
cations that the new governnent will favor a policy of greater freedom
of action, irrespective of comnitnents under the Tripartite Pact, and
one less integrated with the policy of Gernany.
Relations with Great Britain. To understand Anglo-Japanese
relations it is essential to realize that the outbreak of Japanese
hostilities against China in July, 1937, marked the beginning of an
intensive and deternined canpaign to drive foreign interests out of
China. The result was that Great Britain, in the position of being
the largest stockholder, was forced to assune the brunt of the pressure.
Until September 1939, the British were resisting grinly and
stubbornly, but after the outbreak of the European war the position
of England and France rapidly changed, and, threatened in the West,
neither country was in a position to assune a firm policy in the East.
The Japanese lost no time in pressing their advantage.
Tho keynote of British policy since July 1937, had been to
support by indirect neans the Chinese National Governnent. Until the

vitally effects the latter's Fer Eastern aggressive policy in the

of Great Britain today as compared to that of a year ago has enabled

Broadly speaking, Great Britain has elected to take parallel
action with that of the United States. Britain and its dominions
joined in imposing drastic financial and cconomic pressure on Japan

The outbreak of the European war presented Japan with an
opportunity to put into action what it had advocated in words, namely,
the inclusion of the East Indies within the orbit of nations in the
"Greater Enst Asia Co-prosperity Sphere". Japan's "Southward Advance
Policy", hitherto vaguely construed to mean economic penetration in
any region south of Japan is now generally regarded as meaning the
ultimate conquest of the Netherlands East Indian archipelago. The

the islands of Waichow and Hainan, as well as the Spratley Archipelago,
in 1938 and 1939, and the establishment of Japanese naval and air bases
in French Indo-China in 194l, indicate a definite Jepanese policy lead-

tion of the Netherlands East Indies.
The paucity of men and equipment that the Dutch have at their
disposal in the East Indies obviously will not deter the Japanese from
seizing the Islands and it follows therefore that only fear of British
action and American opinion has kept Japan within bounds.
To date Japan has not attempted to challenge the British and
Americans by a grab of the archipelago and has confined its actions in
the field of diplomacy. On February 12, 1940, the Japanese government
suddonly abrogated the Dutch-Japanese Arbitration Treaty of April, 1933.
Despite the Japanese disclaimers, the abrogation has gonerally been
regarded as a move on Japan's part to warn tho Dutch that it will toler-

negotiations for a now trade pact. A Japanese economic mission arrived
in Batavia in 1940, and made proposals, which, it turned out, called
for a privileged position in regard to the export of certain materials,
such as oil, scrap iron, nickel ore, tin, rubber, and rice. The Dutch
regarded the Japanese demands to be of such a naturo that, if acceded
to, they would place the Netherlands East Indies in the status of a
Japanese colony. The Dutch proved to be skillful negotiators and,
encouraged by the arrival of Anerican war materials, rejected not only
the Japanese proposal for a monopoly on theso naterials, but the
Japanese contention that the Enst Indies belong to the so-called "Co-
prosperity Sphere". Believing that Japan was trans-shipping goods to
Germany, the Netherlands Indics reduced its export allotments to Japan
in February, 194l. Trade negotiations botween the two countries were
concluded in June, with the Dutch rejecting the Japanese demands for
larger allotmonts of rubber, tin, nanganese, copra and several other
products. The Dutch have also refused to sell aviation gasolino or as
much crudo potroleun as was desired by Japan. Thus after 18 months of
negotiations tho Japanese failed to bring the Dutch around to their
point of view and departed in anger in June, 194l, fron Batavia, leav-
ing the Japaneso consular officials in the city to continuo the nogo-
tiations.
The prospect for these Japanese negotiators is not bright.

Relations with Soviet Russia. The record of Sovict-Japanese

The conclusion of the German-Soviet nonaggression treaty in

Relations with Thailand. The preoccupation of the two neigh-

is realized that the Thai people would be unable to defend their

To propitiate the Japanese, the Thai government announced, on
