Relations with the United States. Relations between the United
States and Japan since 1937 showed a steady downward trend which began
with the shelving of the Japanese proposal for a modus vivendi to take
effect upon the expiration of the Commercial treaty of 19ll in January,
1940, and which reachod its low ebb in the current freezing of Japanese
assets in America and the embargo on oil. The troubled relations be-
tween the two countries are due to two basically opposed points of view
involving principles as well as self-interest, and the outlook for
compromise or reconciliation is not bright.
Briefly stated, Japan's aim is to create an economic empire in
the Far East which will be solf-sufficiont and capablc of resisting the
application of sanctions by any power or group of powers. In other words,
Japan looks forward to an economic order which will be independent of
the British Empire or the United States. In pursuing this aim the
Japanese have not had scruples in discarding or ignoring treaties,
agreements and the established rights of third parties. The Far Eastern
policy of the United States is based on two principles, the sanctity
of treaties and the equality of economic opportunity in China. That
of Japan, on the contrary, is to upset the status quo. As a result,
American relations with Japan during the past two years have been
characterized by a steadily incroasing pressure upon Japan. On July
26, 1939, without warning, the United States informed the Japanese
Government of its intention to terminate the Treaty of Commerce and
Navigation of l9ll. This action constituted the first blow struck in
defense of American policy. However, by a temporary arrangement, com-
mercial relations remained as before, without benefit of treaty. The
Japanese have tried with some minor success to secure other markets
and sources of raw materials, mainly in South American and southern
Europe, in an attempt to becone independent of the United States.
Japanese-American relations have also been marred by continuous
American protests in regard to loss of American life and damage to
American property caused by the current Japanese military operations in

China. In March 1940, the United States Government, aroused over

The stiffening attitude on the part of the United States

The outbreak of Nazi-Soviet hostilities on June 22, 1941,

in southeastern Asia. The absorption of Indo-China and plans for a

enforcement of the Open Door policy, by economic means at least,
appeasement having notably failed to deter Japanese aggressive moves
on the Asiatic continent. Acting in prompt retaliation to the freez-
ing of Japan's assets by the United States the Japanese Government

Ministry. The application of the freezing order to Japan, a nation
largely dependent upon the United States for many raw materials and
finished products (over 70 per cent of Japan's gainful trade is done
with America and Britain), had a far greater effect than did its appli-
cation to the United States, which was economically dependent upon
Japan only for silk.
Other Relations. Through its possession of the northern part
of the strategic island of Timor, Portugal was affected by Japan's
"Southward Advance" and granted Japan commercial and air rights on
this island in August, 1940. The Japanese are in a good position to
exert pressure on the Portuguese colony of Macao, which is completely
at Japanese mercy. The Japanese have established a consulate at Dilli,
the capital of Portuguese Timor, and also stocked an airdrome there
with a large quantity of high-octane gasoline, and there is reason to
believe that Timor, lying as it does directly across the air route
from Australia to Singapore, will play its part in the current Japanese
bid for dominance in the East Indian Archipelago.
General Conclusions. The exigencies of its war offort have
made of Japan a collective dictatorship without a dictator. A dictator
on the order of Hitler or Mussolini is out of question in Japan where
all persons are bound together under the Emperor. If there have been
trends toward a totalitarian set-up in Japan and a corresponding de-
cline in democratic institutions, they have not troubled the Japanese
people at all, for thoy feel that this leaning toward totalitarianism
is only temporary, and is a result of the war with China. Furthermore,
the total trend is merely a reversion to old, accustonod forns of
repression.

Despite all the talk about a partyless government and a
political dictatorship, little progress has been made in this direction.
Although all political parties were dissolved last summer, the new
national structure, the Imperial Rulc Assistance Association, failed
to win over completely the army and had to be reorganized along less
ambitious lines. Originally, the idea was to form a single party, and
make the State and the Party one and the same thing. The army vetoed
this, apparently from fear lest the head of the single party might in
time become the permanent power holder. After its reorganization in
April of this year the national structure has turned out to be merely
another movoment for the moral mobilization of the people.
Japan's main problen today is not political but economic and
financial. Unless it solves the problen of inflation and public morale
at hone it faces national ruin. Totalitarian-like laws have been
passed in August, 194l, which have made the lives of all Japanese
subjects the charge of the Emperor (or his advisors) - their wealth
likewise. All domestic issues are subordinate to the chief issue in
Japan today, namely: to build up a so-called high-degree national
defense state in view of the menacing attitude of the democracies.
The unfavorable world conditions for foreign trade, econonic
pressure by the United States and England, and the tremendous costs of
waging war on China, have conbined in interfering with Japanese solu-
tions to ward off insolvency. There is a general trend toward infla-
tion, a significant decline in the absorption of national bonds, and
an increase of 50 per cent in the national debt, a nuch heavier tax
burden, a mounting shortage in nearly all connodities and a decline
in labor efficiency. All these factors lure the Japaneso to seek re-
lief in territorial expansion, especially in those areas where vital
war naterials are to be found.
Since the European war, Japan has been afforded nany opportuni-
ties to dislodge European interests in the Far East and substitute its
own "New Order" in eastern Asia. Originally this neant nerely the con-
quest of China, but after the Nazi invasion of the Netherlands and
France, this vague formula was understood to include within its scope
Indo-China, Thailand, and the Netherlands East Indies. To assure it-
self of Axis diplonatic support Japan, in Septenber, 1940, concluded
a Tripartite Pact with Gernany and Italy, in which it gained the co-
operation of the Axis Powers in Japanese plans to oust the denocracies
fron eastern Asia. The "New Order in East Asia" policy was renaned
the "Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere" and envisaged a close
bond between all the nations in Eastern Asia, with Japan playing the
dominant part. In Aoril, 194l, Japan's policy of seeking a free hand
in East Asia was inplenented by a Neutrality Pact with Russia. Al-
though this agreenent fell short of Japan's hopes that Russia would
cease rendering aid to China, it did release Japan fron anxiety as to
Russia's intentions as to the Southward Advance policy.

Since the conclusion of the Tripartite Pact Japan has advanced

using this as an opening wedge, the Japanese have within the last few
weeks absorbed the southern part. Japanese expansionist policy has
always been opportunistic and it doesn't take too much imagination to
foresee further advances into Thailand, British Malaya, and even the
Netherlands East Indies unless a firm stand is taken by the affected
nations. The policy of the United States is founded upon the mainte-
nance of the Open Door (equality of economic opportunity for all in
China) and the sanctity of treaties, both of which principles have
repeatedly boen violated by the Japanese. America's method of apply-
ing economic sanctions against Japan has not proved to be a serious
deterrent to Japan's southward advance policy.
Japan today stands on the brink of two divergent policies. One
beckons it toward the dazzling vision of tropical lands, with their
almost fabulous wealth of minerals and food products and is chiefly
advocated by the navy circles, whose minds are on the oil reserves of
the Indies. The other policy, fostered by the rival army group, is
directed toward the acquisition of maritime Siberia, possibly as far
wost as Lake Baikal, a conquest which would not only remove an ever-
present dread of air raids from the north but also would give valuable
fishing grounds, timber lands, coal and iron ore fields, and some oil,
not to mention the rich prize of Outer Mongolia. The problem that
faces the Japanese is further complicated by the fact that both the
Dutch in the south and the Russians in the north, are not without
assurance of aid from the British and Americans. A move in either
direction would lead to a collision with the two greatest ses powers
in the world, upon which, until the freezing order of this month,
Japan depended for 77 per cent of war materials to carry on war. To
add to Japan's cup of bitterness, it has no definite assuranco that
Germany, in the event of victory over the Soviets, would allow the
Japanese undisputed sway in China or in the East Indies, where German
interests were strongly represented before the European war. Japan is,
in brief, a hungry, and unwelcome guest in a world of power politics,
economic empires, and contending forces possessing resources far ex-
ceoding those at the disposal of the Japanose.
An inkling that Japan is beginning to roalize that it is being
encircled by the demoeracias may be seen from Japan's own plaintive
references to the ABCD (America, Britain, China, and the Dutch) powers
within the last few weeks. With access to the resources of the above-
named countries now cut off by their ombargo on Japanese trade, Japan
for the first time since the China Incident began four years ago, will
have to support its war effort by its own resources or those within
areas occupied by its forces. Unless Japan succeeds in holding out
against the democracies on the basis of these resources, it is not
difficult to foresee military and economic collapse and a decline from
the rank of a first-class power to that of a second or even third-rate
nation.
CONFIDENTIAL

Subject: Japan'. New Promier.
Department of State
The Umited Press reports that Lieutenant General Hideki
Tojo, War Minister in the late Konoye Cabinet, has been designated
Premier and ordered to form a new Cabinet.
2. General Tojo was born in 1884, the son of a Samurai. He
has held several high offices in the Army. notably that of Chief of
Staff of the Kwantung Army, Vice Minister of War and Inspector
General of Aviation.
894.00/11
3. He has been called the father of modern Japanese Army
strategy and is known to be anti-foreign, with a partioular dislike
for the Russians, and an open admiration for German methods. He
areated a sensation in 1938 when, as Vice Minister of War, he pre-
dieted that Japan would have to fight Russia as well as China. He
also warned that Americe would have to be watched. when the Axis
Alliance was signed in September 1940 he said that the road Japan
would follow had been "definitely decided" and there was no turning
34
back. General Tojo is regarded by his associates as a man of un-
shakable determination. He cites reverence snd filial piety as the
two most important attributes of a Japanese soldier. He has little
patience for arguments or other people's views.
894.cc
4. Any cabinet selected by General Tojo may be expected to
have Axis leanings, but will be otherwise anti-foreign and highly
nationalistic.

has the confidence of his fellow generals as a middle-of-
the-road man. In Tokyo last spring he was understood by

No. 53.
October 27, 1941,
On September 30, 1941, JOIIN THOMPSON, San Francisco,
CALIFORNIA, U.S.A. made the foliowing statement to DR. OSWALDO
ARANHA, Minister of Foreign Affairs, RIO DE JANEIRO, BRAZIL,
and was obtained by us from a most secret source:-
894.00/1143
He stated that owing to the amazing Fifth Column
activities of the Germans in Japan, the Japanese press and
much of its officialdom has been corrupted. Japanese return-
ing from Japan and others passing through before the embargo
all confirm this.
He continues:
"Today I had another talk with a Japanese who is
somewhat close to his Government. He tells me that Japan
will definitely break away from the Axis but it will take
time. The Emperor has taken hold and it will take time even
for him to make the change.
The first steps taken were to the effect that the
Emperor took control of the army in Japan proper. This
should have the effect of definitely squashing any efforts
on the part of the super-military in sympathy with the
Nazis to stage a revolution like the one they had staged in
1936.
Three Japanese newspapers all in the pay of the
Germans have been forced to suspend publication. Admiral
Suettsugo, the Number One naval firebrand, advocating imme-
diate war on the democracies has been ousted from his post
as head of the super-nationalistic party. And finally, Mr.
Nakano, the man who aspires to become the Hitler of Japan has
been put in jail quietly and with great secrecy. The anti-
Axis forces now have control in Japan proper. There is a
possibility of a minor revolt by the Axis adnerents, but I
am told that Prince Konoye and the Emperor have taken steps
to handle such a situation.
S
The Japanese armies in China are still independent
/MC
of control by Tokyo, it will become gradually more difficult
for the armies on the continent to get supplies from home
unless they "play ball with Tokyo."
S
All of this, while encouraging to peace in the
Paoific, is certain to be a long drawn out process and will
require a lot of tactful handling on the part of Washington
and Tokyo.

=== Page 447 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Another difficulty in the way of continued peace
out here is the Russian situation. Many of the Japanese
military chiefs in China and Manchuria tnink that they can
beat the weakened Russian garrisons in Siberia. The German
advisers and instructors, who are with the Japanese forces,
are continually urging the Japanese to jump and attack the
Russians. So far, nothing beyond a steady mobilization of
Japan's best fighting forces and its best military equipment
on the Siberian border has taken place. If the Russians
should be decisively defeated by tne Germans, there is no
question that the temptation for the Japanese to rush right
in and grab things will be too great for any force to hold
them back.
With regard to the Russian situation, from all one
gathers here in the way of uuthentic information and fron my
previ us contacts with Soviet diplomats and military, the
Russians are not going to give up. Stalin feels that if he
makes peace with the Nazis his fate will be worse than that
of Petain in France.

NUV 5- 1941
The following facts about Japan:
1.) Tojo is Minister of War as well as of the Interior
under which resorts national defense;
2.) One of his first acts was a clean-up of the Police
in Tokyo;
5
3.) The choice of the other members of the cabinet who are
not politically prominent is to be considered as a
superfluous administrative complement of the cabinet;
seem to prove the truth of the following theory: that differences
of opinion about foreign policy have not been the real reason
for the resigning of the Konoye cabinet. This resignation, how-
ever, has been the immediate consequence of the attack on Minister
attack on
Hiranuma. The murder of Hiranuma can be considered as part of a
large plot to which belonged many of the so-called younger officers
assailant
and leading men of the Police of Tokyo. The mrderr was a member
of a s ecret society, called "The Black Dragon". Their aim was
a government which would immediately start military action
against Russia and the United States. The Prime Minister and
his ministers realized that they were not the people to guarantee
control of the younger officers and that only a military man of
high authority could control the increasing revolutionary spirit.
P
S
The new cabinet, nevertheless, is more or less a compromise.
1
The Fascist element has almost found the realization of its de-
工
sire, i.e. military dictatorship. One may have doubts as to
whether they will accept Tojo as a stepping-stone towards the
dictatorship they want. Any sign of rapprochement on the

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.97)
1580
-2-
part of the democracies will give strength to the extremists
because it will be explained as weakness on their part and
as a proof that the extremists are right and that Japan
can strike wi thout danger. Therefore, the best guarantee
against Japan's entry into the war remains the undiminished
maintenance of economic measures.
Washington, D.C.,
November 4, 1941.

December 3, 1941
MANCHURIA
Living Conditions Under the Japanese
The first time the Japanese Special Police came to the house was
in 1932. There has been a closer check-up since 1936, but since 1938
the Japanese Police have kept a very strict check on all foreigners
residing in the country. The Special Police visited my home on an
average of three times a week for the last three years. The Military
Police called usually once a month. The Military Police always stayed
two hours on a single visit. They did not ask any questions but by
staying so long they gained a cross-section of what was going on during
the day. On the surface of things, the Military Police were very
courteous but this illustration will show how they were working under
the surface. A Chinese graduate of the Imperial University in Tokyo
moved to Ssupingkai where I was living. He was working in the Meteor-
ological Bureau. He discovered the church, and being a Christian, came
out to the evening meetings. His home was just a five minute walk
beyond mine so it was only natural that we should walk home together.
He also felt the need of educated companions. All of a sudden he
stopped coming out to the church. It was about two months later that
I saw him and said: "Where have you been? I haven't seen you for long
time". "Oh", he said, "you must have heard how the Military Police
investigated me for having contact with foreigners and going out to the
church".
Special Police
The Special Police usually stayed anywhere from one half hour to
four and a half hours on one visit. Their questions covered a wide
range. They went into the most minute details of our life history
going as far back as when our grandparents went to school. They asked
questions about our salary, where the money came from, and organization
of the church. They asked for the names and addresses of friends we
wrote to in Manchuria. We had to give details as to our Chinese workers'
life history, salary, etc. At times they wanted to know the names of the
wealthy Chinese church members. As a general rule, we had plain clothes
men at the meetings, and very often police in uniform. We had to give
them an outline of our addresses and the number of people present.
All foreigners residing in Manchuria had to have a residence
certificate. These were renewable once a year and had to be carried
with us wherever we went. The last three years we were required to report
to the Police Station whenever we left the city. We had to tell them what
train we were going on, where we were going, where we would be staying
on reaching our destination, what our business was, and when we would return.

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Usually the police would call at the house before we left; we would be
checked up on the train again, the police sometimes copying dowm the
details from the residence certificate several times on one journey.
On reaching our destination, the police would visit us again. In this
way, they knew exactly where every foreigner was and what he was doing
at all times. From a Chinese friend who was the bank manager, I discovered
that the Japanese Police looked over our bank accounts once or twice a
month. One afternoon a Japanese Police rushed into the house almost out
of breath. Before he was even sitting down, the first question he asked
me was "What is the financial condition of the church in Iceland?" The
reason for this question was to find out how wide my connections were and
how much I actually knew. I have been asked many times what I thought of
the war between Japan and China. This was asked, of course, to try and
trip us up. They copied in their notebooks most trivial details, as for
example, where I bought a bouquet of flowers and how much I had paid for
them. The Japanese are suspicious of everyone and everything. The thing
which gave me most difficulty on returning home to Canada while crossing
the Japanese border was a sheet of music "Wings of the Navy" because it
had a picture of an aeroplane on the front sheet.
Radio
To have a shortwave radio in Japanese controlled territory was a
punishable offense. The police visited the house to check up on the
radios more than once.
Natural Resources
Since the autumn of 1939, coal has been rationed. The amount of
coal allotted to us was not sufficient but even then we were not able to
buy up to the full amount specified on the rationed card. This condition
prevailed in spite of the fact that I lived only fifty miles from one
coal mine and one hundred and thirty miles from another one. It was
almost impossible for the Chinese to buy flour and foreigners had great
difficulty in securing sufficient flour for bread. Many other items such
as matches, cigarettes, cotton, bread, milk, sugar, etc. were scarce,
and it was almost impossible to buy leather shoes. For the masses of the
Chinese people, Kao Liang was plentiful but in one case I heard where even
this coarse grain was being rationed and that they were being given part
of the ration in Kao Liang and part chaff. Building materials were very
scarce as the steel and cement are being requisitioned for war production.
Russia
The Japanese have, of late, been nervous of Russia's strength.
Russian reconnaissance planes were reported to have been all over Manchuria
at the time of the Nomanhan battle in 1939. My Japanese teacher was called
up for a month's military training and was about to proceed to the battle-
front when the armistice between Russia and Japan was signed. I said to
him, "The situation for Japan is not dangerous, is it?" because there were
reports in the newspapers giving fantastic numbers of Russian planes shot
down, but he was very frank and said with reference to the Japanese situ-
ation, "They say it is very dangerous".

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Attitude of the Manchurian People Towards Japan
The 43,000,000 people in Manchuria are overwhelmingly Chinese.
They are completely subdued but not sympathetic towards Japan. They
are under a great many restrictions one of which is that they must have
a permit from the police to move even to another part of the same city.
The Chinese themselves say that there is one representative of the
Chinese Government in each large center waiting for an opportune moment
to lead the people in an uprising against the Japanese but rules for
registration, etc., are so strict that it would be extremely difficult
to get Chinese into the country for guerrilla warfare at the present
time.
The Koreans
The Koreans living in Manchuria are also subdued and afraid of
the Japanese as are the Chinese but more of the Koreans are sympathetic
towards Japan as they get precedence over the Chinese and are used by
the Japanese as special police. However, the majority of Koreans are
not sympathetic towards Japan. The White Russians living in Manchuria
are afraid they will be conscripted into the Japanese army and forced
to fight against the Soviet Russians.
Mongolians
I have not had sufficient contact with the Mongolians living in
Manchuria to find out their attitude, but in 1939 when the battle of
Nomanhan was going against the Japanese, some of the Mongolian troops
in the Japanese army revolted. They looted a little village called Liu
Hu and then appeared before the city of Tungliao shouting "Japan is
finished".
Vulnerable Points in Manchuria
Manchuria is, comparatively speaking, well supplied with railways.
There are many large railway bridges which would be important and vulnerable
targets from the air. Some of these are at Cheng Chia T'un, Chiang Ch'iao,
Harbin, Antung, T'u Men, along with numerous other smaller branches.
Coal Mines
There is a coal mine at Hsian approximately fifty miles east of
Ssupingkia. The large open-cut mine at Fushun, about thirty miles near
Mukden, would be especially vulnerable from the air.
War Industries
There is a very large and important smelter at Anshan situated on the
railway between Mukden and Dairen. At Pen Hsi Hu there is a coal mine and
a large steel plant. At Ssupingkia, there is an oil refining plant (though
many of the Chinese think that they manufacture poison gas there rather than
refining oil.)

=== Page 463 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Barracks
Japanese soldiers are scattered throughout the whole of the
country but there are important barracks at Mukden, Cheng Chia T'un
and Kung Chu Ling. A new and large barracks is being built west of
Ssupingkai and is scattered over a distance of five miles.
Homing Pigeons
The center of the homing pigeons for the Japanese army in Manchuria
is situated at Kung Chu Ling, 153 miles north of Mukden.
Naval Base
The naval base at Port Arthur is very closely guarded as are
Rasshim and Seishin in Northern Korea.
Railway Junctions
Some of the most important railway junctions in Manchuria are
T'u Men and Antung on the border of Korea and Manchuria. Kirin, Hsin King,
Ssupingkai, Mukden, Cheng Chia T'un, Tsi Tsi Har, Harbin, are very important
railway junctions. Pai Ch'eng Tze is a very important railway junction and
military center. T'ung Liao and Wang Ie Miao are also important railway
centers and their destruction would have a tremendous effect on the Mongolians
as these are centers where the Mongolians call to trade.
Attitude of Japanese toward Foreigners
Japanese officials are suspicious of all foreigners residing in
Manchuria. Those who understand Japanese are considered to, perhaps, know
too much. They are suspicious of those who don't study Japanese as they
feel they are not sympathetic toward Japan. The Japanese officials were
not definitely anti-Christian but they were suspicious of the missionaries
because of their close contact with the people and were afraid of them
becoming too enlightened. They were also afraid that the Chinese would be
giving out information to the foreigners. There were a few anti-British
demonstrations in Manchuria during the summer and autumn of 1939 at the
time of the Tientsin crisis.
Attitude of the Soldiers
Before 1930, the Japanese soldier was definitely anti-American and
pro-British but they have now dropped their pro-British attitude. All the
Japanese soldiers are not absolutely sympathetic toward the conflict with
Japanese airman who was proceeding to the Chinese front to take part in the
spring offensive. He told me he did not like to do it but that there was
no help for it.

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
Attitude of the Chinese toward Foreigners
The attitude of the Chinese living in Manchuria toward foreigners
is extremely cordial but many of those who hold important positions in their
community are afraid to be seen too closely connected with foreigners.
Attitude of Koreans
The attitude of the Koreans is also friendly but since the anti-British
attitude of Japan has become more pronounced, they are more cautious in
having any outward connection with foreigners.
Attitude of Japanese people toward Foreigners
The ordinary Japanese are still courteous toward the British and
Americans but have become more timid in having relations with foreigners.
Only on a very few occasions have I met with any disagreeable incidents.
One evening while I was in the telegraph office in Ssupingkai, the operator
ventured the opinion that he hated the British. Last February while
travelling from Peiking to Manchuria, on crossing the border at Shan Hai
Kuan, the Japanese Military Police were extremely rude and appeared to be
trying to stir up trouble.

=== Page 470 === (confidence 0.99)
December 3, 1941
MEMORANDUM
A confidential source has furnished the following information
regarding miscellaneous Japanese matters.
Imagawa Yoshitochi, Director of the Yokohama Specie Bank,
Batavia, is reported to have stated that the economic retaliatory
measures taken by England, America and the Netherlands East Indies
were a heavy blow for Japan's economic position, but in view of the
good relations existing with Thailand, Indo-China, Manchuria and
occupied China, Japan would quickly recover from it.
NB
The main point seems to be whether the oil supplies would
remain available. The Japanese Ambassadors to America and England
allegedly received instructions to do their utmost to prevent the
stopping of these supplies. In case they are unsuccessful, Japan
assertedly will not hesitate to immediately take the necessary steps
to occupy Borneo. Should this occur, England and America would only
protest but not intervene, provided the Japanese did not attack the
other islands.
It is reported that among the instructions issued by General
NB
Terauchi Shoichi to forces in Hainan Island were that black-out
exercises were to commence on September 10, 194l. Further, five small
arms factories were to be completed by the end of October, 194l.
On August 24, 1941, 500 cases of bombs, 1,200 cases of
shells and 300 cases of aircraft spares were landed at Yu Lin Kan
from Japan.
In connection with the growing political tension between
Japan and the Netherlands East Indies, 180 Japanese women and children
of Batavia, Semarang and Soerabaya are reported to have embarked on
August 1, 194l on the "Haruna Maru" for their home country. In
conversations heard during this embarkation, it appears that these
families do not intend to return to the Netherlands East Indies "before
the war is over".
In Semarang it is rumored that the German women from the
Netherlands East Indies who intend to return to Germany via Japan
and Russia are working in Japanese munitions factories.
According to a well informed Japanese, the army, as well as
about 50 ultranationalistic, pan-Asiatic organizations brought heavy
pressure upon the Japanese Government and the navy to attack the oil

=== Page 471 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
tankers from America en route to Vladivostok. According to the report,
the navy supposedly positively refused, with the excuse that it was
not up to her to force Japan's national policy although she was
prepared to carry out with all her might any policy decided upon by
the Emperor. It is asserted that the Japanese navy seems as yet
opposed to provocating America, seemingly aware that it is no match for
the combined American-Allied Forces in the Pacific.
The army is firmly determined to continue to assist Germany
in accordance with the Tripartite Pact by binding the Allied Forces
in southeast Asia through its occupation of Indo-China, which country
Vichy handed over to the Japanese on Hitler's oruer, and the concen-
tration of large military forces, estimated to number one million, at
the Manchukuo frontier in order to lighten the pressure on Germany's
east front. The army was reportedly convinced that the Russian
resistance would soon be annihilated and therefore expected to occupy
Siberia and the Maritime Provinces at an early date. It appears,
however, that both the army authorities and the majority of the
Japanese people are beginning to realize that Russia's army will not
quickly be destroyed, and as a result, the army is holding back for
the present.
Admiral Sankichi Takahashi, Cabinet adviser and one of the
most powerful navy leaders, recently published an article in which
OBF
he doubted whether Germany would win the war. This, according to the
informant, should be considered as a hint that the conclusion of the
Tripartite Pact was a blunder.
The dispute between the army and navy is alleged to have
strengthened the position of Konor and the entourage of the Emperor,
giving the present Cabinet a chance to maintain itself and to with-
hold the army from bringing about an armed conflict. However, the
source of information observed, all this by no means indicates that
Japan will directly turn its back on Hitler or will she be inclined
to negotiate about the withdrawal of its troops from Indo-China.
This is considered impossible in view of the fact that the Axis Pact
was announced by Imperial Decree, and further because of the vital
importance of the economic and strategic stranglehold on Indo-China
by Japan
Another question which assertedly must be reckoned with is
the mission of Ambassador Yoshizawa and other officials to Indo-China,
which actually prepared Yoshizawa's appointment to the position of
Governor General of that country.

=== Page 472 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
It is reported that General Wu Yi Ping who returned
on September 14, 194l from a special visit in Tokyo in behalf of
the Nanking Government stated:
"Konoye Cabinet was experiencing much difficulty as a
result of increasing criticism from extreme right-wing
elements who were secretly demanding energetic measures
be taken to conceal Japan's position in the Tieh Yo.
"If it was true Premier had strengthened his position
by obtaining support of the so-called 'Court groups' it
was doubtful if this support was powerful enough to keep
despotism of Hightists in full check. Main argument of
the latter is that Japan, in order to gain any real results
from conversations with America and democracies in general,
will have to make definite concessions, not only in both
China and Far East questions in general, but most probably
in connection with her future relations to Axis.
"Equally the Government understands that such concessions
(particularly in relation to the China incident, which was
started by the Army extremists) may lead to open in-
subordination of extreme Right groups and adoption of a
provocative policy by them, either in north or south, which
will finally identify Japan's fate with that of Germany.
"Cabinet's policy is therefore to draw out conversations
with America to the utmost possible limit and to profit by
any easing in the Far East situation to strengthen its own
position.
"Cabinet is also exercised as to the final effects of
British and American economic policy and while it is putting
on a bold front at present it realizes that the Japane se
people are becoming anxious as to the future. This
anxiety has been further increased by the inability of
Germany to force a quick decision against Russia and thus
help to remove the menace of the Soviet forces in the Far
East.n
It has been asserted that Admiral Decoux has issued instructions
to the Japanese press to cease attacks on Great Britain and the United
States. This supposedly was confirmed by the editor of "Breton" who
stated these instructions were given to a press meeting on September 20,
1941.

=== Page 473 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Ishibashi Tsuneyoshi and Tobishima Sadashireo, representatives
of the Osaka Mainichi and Tokyo Nichi Nichi, were reportedly forced to
leave the Netherlands East Indies. No reporters of these newspapers
will be allowed to enter that country in the future. This measure
supposedly was enforced because of the systematic anti-Dutch press
campaign carried on in these papers, and further since Tsuneyoshi
assertedly made photographs in forbidden areas and violated the alien
registration ordinance.
It is reported that wheat is rapidly becoming a staple food
in Japan. Rice is rationed but wheat is still an open comnodity. In
1933, the Japanese Government formed a five year plan for the increase
of the wheat crop, setting the goal at 9, 000,O00 koku. This figure
was attained in 1937 and immediately a new plan was made to increase
the crop to 15,000,000 by 1942. By 1940 the output had risen to
13,120,000 koku and it is estimated 15,000,000 koku wil1 be produced
in 1941.
In order to handle this crop, all Japanese flour mills with
a production in excess of 200 barrels daily have been consolidated
into an "All Japan Flour Industry Guild". The total capacity of
production for all flour mills reached 60,080 barrels daily in
March, 194l. However, the mills have a rated capacity of 67,563
barrels daily which means 80,000,000 bags of flour a year.
The Japan Iron Sand Mining Company (Nihon Satetsu Kogyo KK)
which has deposits in Iwate and Aomori Prefectures is reported to have
been extracting vanadium and recently succeeded in obtaining cobalt
as a by-product of refining. Approximately l00,000 tons of iron sand
are handled annually from which an estimated 500 to 600 tons of cobalt,
which can be used in the manufacture of nickel, et cetera, is anticipated.
The Japan Iron Sand Mining Company is capitalized at yen
35,000,000, of which yen 17,500,000 is paid up. This company has mills
which refine the crude metal conglomerate, obtaining vanadium, titanium,
cobalt and tungsten. It is said that the mills are expanding every
month.
According to a report, a photograph taken in 1939 at Maizuru
shows a group standing at the main entrance to the shipyard in that
city, and in the background the tops of six large traveling cranes, and
four large constructions of the steel scaffolding type can be observed.

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.98)
-5 -
It has been reported that work on a seaplane training
station at Fukuyama, Hiroshima-ken, on the Island Sea, was to
commence November 12, 194l. A landplane training station is already
located there.
It is also reported that the Sumitomo Chemical Company
plant at Uiihama, Ehime Prefecture, has been working on the latest
type of poison gases since June, 1940. The chief chemist at this
plant is a German loaned to the Sumitomo Company from the I. G.
Farbenindustrie. It is asserted that these poison gases, of
arsenic derivatives the raw material for which comes from Besshi
Copper Mines waste gases, are being manufactured for the Japanese
army.

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.99)
holosure to despatch no. 634 from Jemes B. Stewart,
American Consul General, Zürich, Switzerland, dated
December 23, 194l, on the subject of: "Book on Japan
for possible use by the Committee on Publio Informa-

JAPAN'S STRENGTH AND WEAKNESS
Are the Japanese Healthy or Overstrained?
This is the question which the well-known travel
authoress, Lily Abegg, places at the head of a chapter
in her book "Yamato - The Belief of the Japanese People
in their Divine Mission". Lily Abegg's answer to this
question is all the more interesting since the author-
ess does not belong to that class of travoling authors,
unfortunatoly all too prevalent, who, after completing
a sojourh of a few weeks, or a visit to a few first-
class hotels, bars, and places of amusement, a trip to
a few internationally famous sights, and, if lucky, in-
terviews with a few prominent persons, immediately starts
to write a"profound and fundamental work" concerning a
country. Lily Abegg, in contrast to suoh authors,
really knows the country about which she writes. She
was born there, has spent a great part of her life there,
speaks the Japanese language practically as her native
tongue, and is still today one of the permanent colla-
borators of one of the biggest German newspapers, so
that her statements can scaroely be suspeoted of being
tainted with anti-Japanese feelings, as might easily be
the case with an American author writing about Japan.
Nevertheless, whoever expects that in fundamental
questions Lily Abogg might reach different conolusions
from those of American authors familiar with Japanese
life, will be disappointed. Just as is the oase with
the more oonscientious of American observers of Japan,
she sees, side by side with their enormous accomplish-
ments, the grave weaknesses of this people. Thus, in
answering the question: "Are the Japanese healthy or
overstrained?" she writes as follows:
"It is not an easy task to write concerning the
health, spiritual or physioal, of a people so alien
to ourselves, and at the same time so differently
conditioned as the Japanese.
"The fishermen and peasants along the coasts of
Japan, handsome sunburnt men, look like athletes
oven though most of them are of small stature. If
we move away from the coast, however, and approach
the mountain districts, we meet athletio figures
more

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
more and more seldom, instead there are puny, even
weakly farmers who give the impression that they can
hardly bear the burden of their work. The more we
penetrate into the northern districts, the more dismal
the sight becomes, until we finally enter the notorious
slum districts where there are impoverished creatures
suffering from the effects of years of malnutrition.
In the large cities, especially one does not obtain a
partioularly favorable impression of the stature and
health of the Japanese people. These indoor people
are pale and nervous, as in other large cities in the
world, equally pale and narrow-chested are the small
artisans squatting all day long on their Japanese
straw mats.
Invalids and Hypoohondriacs
"Soon it becomes apparent that most city dwellers
constantly complain of some ailment or indisposition,
these may be grave illnesses such as tuberculosis or
rheumatism, or merely minor ailments such as headaohes,
fever, nervousness, et cetera. Most of them carry
along medicines or pills, and the physician is ofton
and gladly.consulted.
"A foreign visitor concludes that all this strikes
him as remarkable merely beoause he did not expect this
of the sons of the Far East, since he was led to believe
that they could bear any hardship. However, he soon
discovers that nowhere else does one hear so much about
the improvement of public health, the problem of nutri-
tion, or the necessity of athletio training, as in
Japan. Furthermore, the visitor learns that the Japan-
ese consider the strong physique and good health of the
white man as something enviable and exemplary. After
the first encounter with foreigners, which occurred
during the past eantury, the ambition of every Japanese
was "to beoome as big and strong as the foreigners".
The Japanese has always looked upon us as being stronger
and healthièr. He is probably right. One can confi-
dently assert that the general level of Japanese health
is below that in European countries.
"For several centuries now a majority of Japan's
population has lived in cities. Especially during the
Yeddo-Bra from l600-l853, the internal peace of the
united Japanese empire had vouchsafed a flourishing
period for commeroe and for professional artisans.
Tokio, the seat of the Shogun, the reigning prince,
was the world's largest city during the eighteenth
oentury.

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
century. Today it is the third largest. Undoubtedly,
as in other places, this urban existence has had its
debilitating and weakening effect.
"As in other countries, the robust, invigorating
movement comes from the rural population. Many gener-
als, high officials, economic leaders, and other promi-
nent personalities in Japan were farmers' sons, and
are not descendents of the Samurai families, as is
often mistakenly assumed, especially as regards officers.
During the past few decades much healthy farmer blood
has come to enrich the oities. Many a Japanese who is
today manager of a tortile mill, or head of an export
firm, had a grandfather who stood in the rice fields.
Industry owes a large part of its man-power to the
rural districts.
The Plight of the Japanese Farmer
On the whole, therefore, Japan would not find it-
self in a fundamentally different situation from that
in any other country if it had healthy peasants and a
sound agricultural system. But today this is not the
case. The awful plight of the Japanese peasant
throatens not only public health, but the very existence
of the nation. The farmer's lot was never an easy one
in Japan. He was always obliged to work cruelly hard
for a pittance. But during the last decades espeoial-
ly, he has been driven by donditions to the verge of
disaster. The chief fault lies in the lease system
whioh obliges the peasant to surrender from 40 to 70
peroent of the land's yield, for only in the rarest in-
stances does the Japanese peasant own the land he tills.
In addition, there is the decline of the silk-worm cul-
tivation and the artificial stabilization by the Govern-
ment of the price of rice. It was the peasant who had
to pay for the country's economio ascent, and he has
paid for it wdllingly, obediently, and faithfully, even
to the verge of starvation.
"What is to beoome of a nation that so neglects
the very source of its strength, its peasant population?
For the present this piracy oontinues, as does the pira-
tieal exploitation of the intellectuai classes. The
ambitious Nipponese Empire with its world-conquering
projeots here shows festering spots which may some day
become the nuoleus of a fatal malady.
"The malmutrition of the rural classes, aside from
its direot physical effect on the population, predis-
poses them to infeetions, particularly to tuberoulosis.
Tuberoulosis and rheumatism are the two most prevalent
diseases in Japan. There are foreign physicians who
estimate

=== Page 481 === (confidence 0.99)
estimate that at least 70 percent of the entire
Japanese population has a tendency to tuberculosis.
"The prevalence of rheumatism, from which prao-
tically every Japanese suffers, is undoubtedly con-
nected with the damp and windy climate, and the in-
adequate living quarters in the swampy districts
among the rice fields. Naturally, the fear of
catohing any sort of cold, and of rheumatism, is wide-
spread among the Japanese. They therefore indulge in
what we would consider to be exaggerated preoautions,
or downright molly-coddling; even in the heat of July,
with 95 degrees in the shade, the Japanese will not
abandon, by day or night, the popular red flannel belt.
He is convinoed that without it he will catch "his
death of cold". While we Europeans have pleasure in
swimming in the Japanese ocean as early as April or
as late as November, the Japanese does so only from
lato in July to the beginning of September.
"There is hardly a Japanese who does not take a
cure at least once a year. Only in recent years did
it become the habit to spend ones holiday in hiking
or athletios, but the cure is still the traditional
thing. Such a trip, mostly undertaken with kith and
kin, is considered by the Japanese as the ideal re-
laxation. At least three times a day and often more
frequently, he bathes in the hot springs and emerges,
convinoed that his health has thereby benefitted.
For, as stated, a Japanese invariably has some sort
of ailment, or at least imagines it.
"Poor oyesight and bad teeth are striking
features of the Japanese. Their short-sightedness
may be due not only to constant reading and studying,
but also the strain of generations of handwork and
oraftmanship. In no other country throughout the
world can there be found so many persons wearing
speotaoles as in Japan. During the Olympic Games
hold at Garmish-Patenkirohen, three out of the four
Japanese skaters were bespectacled, a fact not even
notioed in Japan, but which in Germany was striking
enough to be spooially mentioned in all the news-
papers.
The particularly poor teeth of the Japanese
are due to their food, and the nutrition question is
far from having been solved in Japan. Whereas
Europeans admire the frugal, modest, and cheap Japan-
ose food, the Japanese themselves are at their wit's
end in trying to solve the problem of the undermining
effects of this exaggerated frugality.
"Above

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
"Above all it is seen that in addition to these
evidences of disease and weakness which are evidenced
by all civilized nations during their course of develop-
ment, the Japanese people have not made themselves
immune to the climatic conditions, nor have they proved
capable of introducing a reasonable system of nutrition.
These deficienoies at once became apparent when the
Japanese abandoned their traditional seclusion and en-
tered into competition with other peoples inured to
the fight. Will the Japanese people be victorious
considering that they are already living on the better
part of their capital, instead of off its revenue?
"In China there are ten times as many sturdy
young men capable of being turned into soldiers as
in Japan. Sumatra and Borneo, the Philippine Islands,
and all the South Sea Islands, are populated by kindred
Malayans, all of whom excel the Japanese in natural
health and vigor. How then could this very people,
so weak and over-civilized, accomplish such feats?
The Japanese only reply to this is "Yamato Damashi"
- Japanese spirit - and one is reminded involuntarily
of General Nogi, the conqueror of the world's then
greatest military Power, who the Japanese people as
children are taught to believe was "puny, timid, and
whining".
Japan, the Land with the highest number of Suicides
"Yamato Damashi - the belief of the Japanese
people in their divine mission - without keeping this
in mind the Japanese oharaoter would become inoompre-
hensible, for it is the source of its strength and
weakness. It has often been pointed out that the
voloanic origin of the island empire shaped the Japan-
ese oharacter. As a matter of fact, its unevenness,
its hysterioal eruptions and explosions can only be
likened to the eruptions of its volcanoes.
"The high suicide rate in Japan is very striking;
the yearly average is 15,000, in other words, every
day fifty Japanese kill themselves, fifteen times as
many as in Germany!
" First there are the saorifioial deaths for the
country about which one hears most frequently in Europe.
A young man who succeeds in being received in audience
by the Prime Minister in order to hand him a memorandum
concerning abuses in the Japanese political system, em-
phasizes his aotion by killing himself with a dagger in
the Prime Minister's presence.
When

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
"When the American immigration laws became known
numerous young Japanese committed suicide in front of
the American Embassy in Tokio. At the time of the
Washington Naval Disarmament Conference young men com-
mitted suicide even before the departure of the Japan-
ese delegates, considering that even to send a delega-
tion was a dishonor to their nation. For similar
reasons Prime Minister Hara was killed at that time
by young patriots. During the Russo-Japanese war,
Japanese officers would kill themselves in the sight
of the Russians because they had not been killed by
Russian bullets during the first five minutes fight-
ing.
"Aside from these suicides arising from patriotic
motives, there are many more due to unhappy love or
family affairs, unhappy marriages, unfulfilled ambi-
tions, or poverty.
"The lack of poise in the Japanese is partioularly
oxhibited in this sentimental way of killing themselves.
On the "suioide mountain" Mihara, it has frequently
happened that tourists climbing the Mihara with the in-
tention of committing suicide, succeeded in persuading
harmless tourists to follow their example. This is
one reason why visitors to the mountain are warned
against entering into conversation with strangers.
"The voloano Mihara, into whose crater the suicide
leaps, is on an island in the bay of Tokio. In clear
weather it is possible to see the coast of Japan, the
islands, the blue sea and, in the background, the sacred
Fuji Mountain majestioally dominating everything. Con-
fronting this sublime spectacle and in a rush of emotion,
the suioide leaps into the smoking, thundering crater.
Has a Westerner, confronted by the beauty and majesty
of the Alps ever lost his balanoe so far as to make such
a jump? Some might have dreamt of it for a moment,
but the Japanese really does it.
"These overwhelming sentiments of the Japanese
stand in close correlation with his creed, his will to
self-discipline, and self-control. And such cases
demonstrate the danger of the Yamato Damashi belief.
"Just as the over rigid attitude of the Yamato
Damashi may be disrupted by sentimentalities and momen-
tary complete laok of poise, so can it be converted
into a state of sacred anger and insensate rage, at
whioh time the worst possible crimes oan be committed.
The Role of Bureauoracy
"This same Japanese whose temperament is only
controlled

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
controlled by virtue of the severe national ethical
creed is, notwithstanding, the most bureauoratic per-
son on earth. Bureaucracy rules practically the en-
tire life of the Japanese. He is bureaucratic in
his sense of responsibility, in the application of the
law, and in his social life. In no other country in
the world are there so many things that "can" or "can
not" be done; certain dishes must be eaten on certain
feast days; obligatory visits must be paid on New
Year's Day; a certain cure must be taken in the month
sacred to this custom; certain transactions must be
performed only by the head of the family, and so on,
and so on. Everywhere, and for everything, there
are fixed rules and obligations.
"A Japanese official will follow the letter of
the law more olosely than the instruotions of his
superior, with the result that new regulations are
often not carried out at first. The way in which the
Japanese clings to the letter of the law is allied to
his sense of duty and his loyalty to the State.
Furthermore, most of the laws in force today have been
adopted from abroad and their sense is often obscure
to the Japanese official. He therefore considers it
of great importance to interpret and apply them exactly.
This passion for duty can at times have disastrous con-
sequences: it has happened that officials committed
suicide in the belief that they had failed completely
to carry out their duties, while still others became
insane from the mere thought of their inadequacy.
"It is strange that most foreigners on entering
the country for the first time are struck by the seem-
ing laok of intelligence of the Japanese people, a fact
whioh is invariably contrasted with the quick sense of
perception of the Chinese. One wonders how it was
possible for these slow-minded Japanese to have acoom-
plished all they have. This lack of perception,
this "stupidity" which drives many a foreigner to the
verge of desperation, is at heart nothing but one of
the innumerable psychological peculiarities of the
Japanese. There can be no question that the Japan-
ese people possess, although not superior, assuredly
average intelligenoe. The Japanese simply does not
understand, when it suits him not to do so, and this
want of wiilingness may at times be quite unconsoious.
"In this connection little has been said conoern-
ing the admirable and likeable attributes of the
Japanese oharacter which no one has desoribed more
oharmingly than Lafoadio Hearn. There is no reason
for

=== Page 485 === (confidence 0.98)
-8
for condemning Hearn because his writings echo his
admiring affection for this people; many people have
qualities, and this is the case preeminently with the
Japanese, which appear so noble and so perfect that
only loving admiration can understand and describe
them.
"However, the task before me was not to inves-
tigate that which strikes one as agreeable or as un-
pleasant, but rather to examine some striking and
prominent oharacteristics from the point of view of
the gere ral mental attitude.
And here it must be
recorded that the spirit of Yamato, which redeems so
much in Japan can not redeem itself, since it is it-
solf a source of so many contentious and unhealthy
manifestations."

The author describes Japan's "con-
tinental policy" as having been suggested
in 190l by Foreign Minister Komura, es-
pecially noting his mention of "developing
toward" Manchuria, India and South America.
In the face of American hostility, Japan's
continental policy must be inseparable with
her naval policy which requires that western
Pacific and south China weters be made
impregnable to Japan's enemies. The article
ends with an appeal for the awakening and
resurrection of the races of East Asia and
for their union against British and American
exploitation and oppression.

(2)
That "our country's policy, which aims for the creation
of a new world order and the e stablishment of a greater East Asia
mutual prosperity sphere, has entered a new stage through the conclu-
sion of the tripartite alliance", as claimed by our Fast Asia Bu-

These three nations, which occupied opposite seats at the
Paris Peace Conference, have come to harbor the same histariaal mis-
sion, the same economic circumstances and the same political aim
and have come to band together amirtst the serious opposition exist-

was their first declaration. The creation of a new order in East
Asia, which has been given as our highest aim of the settlement of
the incident, together with Italy and Germany's new order in Europe,
has not only been presented as t he aim of the wars in Europe and
Asia, but also as the guiding principle of the creation of a new
world. Therefore, our great continental policy has entered a new
and important stage embodying a world formula.
The principle acting as the support of the creation of a
new world order has been provided by the words "to regard the ac-
quisition of appropriate territories by each of the nations of the
world as the sine qua non for lasting peace", which the drafters of
the treaty most pains-takingly wrote. This is a principle indicating
the correct basis of world partition, instead of the recognition
of possessions as legal from the fact that a nation has appeared on
the stage earlier than the other nations, and also is a principle
advocating the acquisition of appropriate territories by every nation
of the world to enable her to exist on earth and not be shut out from
space. In other words, this sets forth an idea of systematized order,
in place of the anarchical world order and world partition of today.
Although its practical applicability is limited to the greater East
Asia sphere and the European regions, the treaty is looking forward

It seemed as if it was impossible for everybody to understand
the inseparable nature of our great continental policy and the Pacific
policy at the beginning of the China incident. ht that time, our
country guarded against the aggravation of her friction with America,

tinental policy. However, as soon as a sign indicating the receding
of British influence from East Asia appeared with the aggravation of the
European situation, America self-appointed herself as the watch-dog
of Britain's Far Eastern interests and increased her diplomatic and
economic coercion against our country. Especially after the con-
clusion of the tripartite agreement, America, completely forgetting

As regards the role to be played by our navy in this inter-

explaining that Britain's foreien policy will be determined by the
movements of the Japanese and the American navies, stated as fol-
lows prior to the outbreak of the current great, war:
" The reason why Great Britain obstructed the dominition of
the European continent by one nation from 1688 to 1918 was that all
her conceivable naval rivals existed in Europe. However, from 1918,
this situation changed, for with the appearance of the Japanese and
the American navies, the great naval nations existed no longer in
Europe. Consequently, Europe was liberated from the control of Great
Britain. The significance of this change will probably be felt with
the passing of time. The hostile feeling of both or either Japan or
America will make empty the historical strategy of Great Britain to
inflict the whole pressure of her ravy upon her enemies in Europe.
Hence, it has become necessary for Great Britain to choose as to
whether she is going to maintain the balance of power of the great con-
tinent or of the world. And Great Britain, who is being confused by
the independent actions of Japan and America, will probably have to
tolerate the development of Europe. Now, supposing Great Britain were
to assume a policy leading her to fight against Germany, Italy and Ja-
pan will probably side with Germany this time, and should Great
Britain stand on the verge of destruction, America probably will inter-
vene. Howeger, this will not happen at any time before that. And
supposing America's intervention, that victory will rohably be a
more pathetic one than that of 1918n.
The important points that can be drawn from this view should

be that a tacit agreement l or America to oppeee and check our navy
has been concluded between Great Britain and America and has made
possible Great Britain's declaration of war.

Eastern policy, which is often overlooked. It pertains to the fact
that Great Britain's sea supremacy has receded from the Far Fast

The present stage of our great continental policy has

alined out at sea.
The historical conceptions of "great continental nation"

meanings ever since the l9th century, but it would be worthy of note
to those who think of the great continental policy that our country,
which has bases on the great continent and embraces the sea, is tak-
ing a form unprecedented in world history.

The Sino-Japanese treaty for the readjustment of relations,

toward the establishment of a new order in China, unfolded a new phase
in East Asia. This treaty, which does not discriminate between the win-

by the concerned authorities made public at the time of the consum-

for the expansion of her national defense industries and the building

facilities for the development and usage of the special natural re-

alliance, and work toward a mutual aim through the recognition of the
right to station troops andvessels, thus making possible the secur-

the task to defend the East Asia sohere. Half a year has passed since,

monetary system, her peace and order problem and her cultural problem
through the establishment of the state operated Nankin: Central Uni-

Japan, Manchoukuo and China and Japanese supervised properties and
Chinese warships have been returned. Hence, it is believed that the
progress of this government eventually will hasten the time of its
official recognition by Germany and Italy.
Of course, beginning with the concession problems, there are
many obstacles lying in the path of the Chinese government. Especial-

upon Britain and America, which has eaten its way deeply into the people
of China. Nevertheless, the penetration of the great Asiatic principle,

can influences, which actually can be seen, should make the people of
China realize that they are the decendents of the people, of whom their
enemies once made General Grant state, "iany of the foreigners, unable
to see beyond the present and wanting to preserve t he present condition,
are doing nothing but exploiting the things of East Asia and attempting

In August of last Autumn, the landing of our troops in

Asia, but the undisguisable loss of French influence resulted in the

Thailand and French Indo-China were Britain and America, who, with
threats and warnings, devised various political schemes. Pritain and

Indo-China and to rant her a loan of $20, 000,000. As soon as this
proposal was rejected, they inflicted economic pressure against Thailand
and America stopped the shipment of airplanes ordered by Thailand.

I regret that I cannot initial this

There is no assurance that we will be

withdrawal of American civilian personnel
from areas (all areas) under Japanese domina-
tion. It does not seem to me desireble to
muzzle for an indefinite and perhaps prolonged
period Mr. Marsh or other American officials
who may wish to talk on Japan. One criticism
that is constantly made of the Government--
and, in my opinion, with substantial warrant —
1s that, in a democracy where much depends
on having an "educated" public opinion the
Government does not adequately inform the
public of facts. Mr. Marsh has indicated
that he would be willing to revise his ad-
dress. It seems to me that it would be the
better part of wisdom for the Department to
indicate to him what it thinks should be
altered in ordeleted from his manuscript

under an injunction of being discreet for

CONFIDENTIAL
Memorandum of Conversation
October 8, 1941.
Commercial Attache GROVES
Mr. Yasuto SHUDO, Director of the
Asano Bussan Company, Tokyo.
Subjeet: Conditions in Japan
Yesterday morning (1l:45) Mr. Yasuto Shudo, Director
of the important Japanese Asano Bussan Company, called
on me at the instance of a mutual friend, Mr. Ryozo
AsAmo, whe -- with his brother -- is at the head of the
varied Asano interests in Japan; Mr. Asano was a olass-
mate at Marvard. Until two or three years ago, Mr.
Shudo was for many years in the Japanese Foreign Service,
serving as Commercial Attache at Berlin, London and
other posts and also as a member of the Japanese delega-
tiem at varions economic conferences under League
auspiees at Geneva. Mr. Shudo was my guest at lunch,
together with Mr. Calder, Assistant Commercial Attache,
and during our time together he talked quite freely of
conditions in Japan.
The eeemomie situation, he said, was becoming in-
ereasingly dirfieult with a growing shortage of many
rew materials and feedstuffs. There is extensive use
of substitute materials in all textile fabries and the
quality ef most censumer goeds has suffered appreciably.
It was evident that he felt mush anxiety about the
altimate effeet ef the situatien on the cowmtry as a
whele. He indicated that there was a great deal of
arest meag the peeple, and a wide divisien of senti-
mont ameng different groups.
The centrelling sentiment in the country falls
1nte three majer divisieas, he explaimed: (1) the
right wing er eenservative element, whieh is very
masiomalistie and pre-Axis; (2) the midale-ef-che-road
fastiem, representing largely the business semtiment

=== Page 514 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Regarding the political situation, Mr. Shudo
stated that the political unrest in the country was
in part due to the vacillating leadership of Prince
Konoye; but, he said, Japan was so woefully lacking
in able leaders that there was no one in sight to
take Prince Konoye's place at the head of the govern-
ment. He indicated that Prince Konoye was sponsoring
the effort to reach a friendly settlement with the
United States; in this connection he sent a personal
emissary -- an army colonel whose name I do not recall --
to Washington last spring, to talk with President
Roosevelt. This emissary was sent when Mr. Matsuoka,
Foreign Minister, was on his famous European trip.
When the latter returned to Tokyo, he was very displeased
over Prince Konoye's action and the revision of the
cabinet followed shortly thereafter with Mr. Matsuoka
retiring from the Foreign Ministry. The Prince's
omissary stayed in Washington and returned to Japan
only in late August.
Mr. Shudo remarked in confidence that Prince
Konoye was extremely hopeful that the discussions
with the United States would develop satisfaotorily
to a point where a personal conversation between him-
self and President Roosevelt would be profitable.
For this purpose, he said, that Prince Konoye had
made tentative plans for a personal meeting with
President Roosevelt, for which purpose he had also
made sentative arrangements for Mr. Ryozo Asano to
accompany him, Mr. Asano being a personal friend of
the President. He said that the press report of a
proposed Konoye-Roosevelt rendezvous had been of-
ficially denied in Tokyo, but the facts were as
statod.
The conclusion from Mr. Shudo's remarks was quite
ovident, -- that the Japanese Government is most anxious
to reach an amioable settlement with the United States
without resorting to more extreme measures.
H.L.G.

ENGLISH TRANSLATION OF PREMIER GENERAL HIDEKI TOJO'S
SPEECH DELIVERED AT THE HOUSE OF PEERS ON THE MORNING OF
MARCH 12 IN HIS CAPACITY AS PREMIER.)
"AS HAS ALREADY BEEN ANNOUNCED BY IMPERIAL HEADQUARTERS, THE
FATE OF THE ENTIRE NETHERLANDS EAST INDIES HAS BEEN SEALED WITH THE
SUBJUGATION OF THE ENTIRE ISLAND OF JAVA ON MARCH 9. AND RANGOON,
TBERMOSALSMPDRTANONBNARCH 8.
THEADSTIMPORTANT BASE
(IN)
"REPORTS WILL BE MADE BY ARMY AND NAVY AUTHORITIES CONCERNING
5
OTHE ACTUAL CIRCUMSTANCES OF FIGHTING. I AM GLAD, HOWEVER, TO STATE
ONCE AGAIN MY VIEWS AND CONVICTIONS AT THIS OPPORTUNITY.
I HAD OCCASION TO EXPRESS IN THIS HOUSE ON JANUARY 21, AND THEN
ON FEBRUARY 16, THE FIRM DETERMINATION OF JAPAN TO CRUSH THOROUGHLY
THE NETHERLAND EAST INDIES FORCES WHO, WITHOUT UNDERSTANDING OUR
REAL INTENTIONS, WERE CONTINUING THEIR USELESS RESISTANCE AGAINST
US, AND ALSO TO SUBDUE THE BRITISH MILITARY BASES IN THE REGION OF
BURMA, THEREBY, CUTTING OUT THE ROUTE FOR ANGLO-AMERICAN AID TO THE
CHUNGKING REGIME.
ON MARCH 1, THE. IMPERIAL FORCES LANDED IN THE ISLAND OF JAVA, THE
LAST STRONGHOLD OF THE NETHERLAND EAST INDIES, AND ITS CAPITAL,
BATAVIA, FELL ON THE MARCH 5, FOLLOWING WHICH THE STRATEGIC BASE
OF SURABAJA FELL.
RENDERED TO OUR FORCES ON MARCH 9 AND (WITH) THAT, THE

=== Page 517 === (confidence 0.99)
IN THE REGION OF BURMA, IMPERIAL FORCES, ADVANCING OVER DANGEROUS
MOUNTAINS AND RIVERS, SWEPT THE PLAINS OF BURMA, AND ON MARCH & TOOK
POSSESSION OF RANGOON, ONE OF MOST IMPORTANT BRITISH BASES FOR THE
ENCROACHMENT ON EAST ASIA, WHICH HAS HITHERTO SERVED AS THE ONLY
GATEWAY FOR AMERICAN AND BRITISH AID TO CHUNGKING. THIS RESULTED
IN THE COMPLETE COLLAPSE OF THE SO-CALLED BURMA ROUTE, BEFORE THE
MIGHT OF OUR FORCES.
"THUS, IN THE SHORT PERIOD OF ONLY 2O DAYS, WHAT THE IMPERIAL
GOVERNMENT HAD DECLARED ON PREVIOUS OCCASIONS HAS BECOME A REALITY.
THAT WE HAVE, IN SUCH A SHORT TIME, BROUGHT THE N. E. I. UNDER OUR
CONTROL AND ÓCCUPIED THE STRATEGIC REGION OF BURMA, IS SOLELY DUE TO
HIS IMPERIAL MAJESTY, THE EMPEROR, UNDER WHOSE AUGUST VIRTUE, LOYAL
AND GALLANTOFFICERS AND MEN OF IMPERIAL FORCES HAVE EXERTED THEIR
COURAGEOUS EFFORTS.
"THIS INDEED IS MATTER OF OUR MUTUAL FELICITATIONS. FOR THE
PEOPLE IN HONGKONG, MANILA, SINGAPORE AND VARIOUS OTHER PLACES, WHICH
HAVE BEEN FREED FROM THE YOKE OF THE UNITED STATES AND GREAT BRITAIN
BY THE IMPERIAL FORCES, ARE ALREADY ENGAGED VIGOROUSLY IN THE
TASK OF NEW CONSTRUCTION WITH THEIR ABSOLUTE CONFIDENCE IN THE IMPERIAL
FORCES.
"THAT IS EXTREMELY ENCOURAGING TO US. I WISH TO TAKE TTT
OCCASION TO EXPRESS OUR PROFOUND SYMPATHY WITH THE INDONESIAN AND
BURMESE PEOPLES, WHO HAVE FOR MANY GENERATIONS, GROANED UNDER THE
TYRANNICAL RULE OF GREAT BRITAIN AND THE NETHERLANDS, AND AT THE SAME
TIME, EARNESTLY HOPE THAT THEY WILL MAKE A NEW START AND ATTAIN
HEALTHY PROGRESS AND PROSPERITY, AS NEW PARTNERS IN THE CONSTRUCTION
OF GREATER EAST ASIA.
"AS A RESULT OF THE FALL OF THE N.E.I AND RANGOON, AUSTRALIA
AND INDIA HAVE COME TO BE CONFRONTED DIRECTLY BY THE MIGHT OF THE
IMPERIAL FORCES.
I WISH TO TAKE THIS OCCASION TO REPEAT ONCE AGAIN, TO AUSTRALIA
AND INDIA, THE VIEWS AND CONVICTION OF JAPAN. THE AUSTRALIANS
THEMSELVES, MUST BE FULLY AWARE OF THE FACT THAT IT IS UTTERLY
IMPOSSIBLE FOR AUSTRALIA, HAVING AN EXTREMELY SPARCE POPULATION,
DESPITE HER VAST TERRITORY, AND, MOREOVER, BEING SITUATED SO FAR FROM
US AND GREAT BRITAIN, HER MOTHER COUNTRY, TO DEFEND HERSELF AGAINST
THE MIGHT OF OUR INVÍNCIBLE FORCES.
IT IS, THEREFORE, SELF EVIDENT AS TO WHAT ATTITUDE AUSTRALIA
TAKE TO SÉCURE THE WÉLL BEING OF HER PEOPLE. IF AUSTRALIA DOES
RECTIFY HER PRESENT ATTITUDE,SHE WILL INEVITABLY FOLLOW IN THE
FOOTSTEPS OF THE N.E.I. I EXPECT THAT AUSTRALIA WILL, BY (FOLLOWIN
RELATIONS) AND OTHER CONSIDERATIONS,SEE CLEARLY THE PREVAILING
SITUATION RIGHTLY FACE WHAT PROVIDÉNCE DICTATES, AND THUS DECID
ER ATTITUDE AT THIS MOST IMPORTANT JUNCTURE.

=== Page 518 === (confidence 0.98)
INDIAN PEOPLE, JAPAN ENTERTAINS, OF COURSE,
EST PART OF ANTAGONIZING THE. I DESIRE, HOWEVER, TO
RATE CLEARLY THAT THE DETERMINATION OF JAPAN TO CRUSH
OUGHLY BRITISH AND AMERICAN INFLUENCE WILL UNDERGO NO CHANGE WHAT
VER
"BURMA FOR THE BURMESE IS ALREADY ON ITS WAY TO REALIZATION.
IT IS MY FIRM BELIEF THAT NOW IS THE TIME TO ESTABLISH INDIA FOR THE
INDIANS, WHICH HAS FOR MANY YEARS BEEN THE ASPIRATION OF FOUR HUNDRED
MILLIONS OF INDIAN PEOPLE.
"GREAT BRITAIN HAS, FOR A LONG TIME, DECEIVED AND CONTINUED
HER ARBITARY RULE OVER INDIA. BUT THE REALITY OF THE BRITISH
PROMISE MADE TO INDIA IN LAST GREAT WAR, MUST BE STILL FRESH, I
BELIEVE, IN THE MEMORY OF THE INDIAN PEOPLE.
"NOW, GREAT BRITAIN IS TRYING AGAIN TO DECEIVE INDIA WITH ALL
SORTS OF CAJOLERY. IF THE LEADERS OF IHDIA, MISLED BY SUCH
BRITISH CAJOLERY, BETRAY THE LONG-CHERISHED ASPIRATION OF THE
INDIAN PEOPLE, AND THUS FLING AWAY THIS HEAVEN-SENT OPPORTUNITY,
I BELIEVE THERE WILL BE NO CHANCE FOR SAVING INDIA FOREVER, AND
THERE WILL BE NO GREATER MISFORTUNE BEFALLING FOUR HUNDRED
MILLIONSOF INDIAN PEOPLE.
"WILL INDIA RISE AS "INDIA FOR INDIANS"TO HAVE THE HONOR OF
COOPERATING FOR THE ESTABLISHMENT OF THE GREATER EAST ASIA
CO-PROSPERITY SPHERE? OR WILL SHE PERMANENTLY STOOPUNDER THE
SHACKLES OF ANGLO-AMERICAN POWERS TO LEAVE HER NAME STAND AS A
ALAVE TOPOSTERITY?
"SHE IS NOW FACE TO FACE WITH THE TIME WHEN SHE SHOULD LIQUIDATE
HER PAST, SEE NEW SITUATION CORRECTLY AND MAKE HER FINAL DECISION.
WITH ITS CONNECTION WITH THE U.S. AND GREAT BRITAIN COMPLETELY
CUT OFF THROUGH THE FALL OF RANGOON, THE CHUNGKING REGIME HAS
BECOME COMPLETELY ISOLATED IN THE REAL SENSE OF THE TERM. WHAT THE
U. S. AND GREAT BRITAIN WOULD GIVE CHUNGKING IS ONLY USELESS
GOLD IN RETURN FOR WHICH THESE POWERS WOULD DEMAND THE FLESH
AND BLOOD OF THE CHINESE PEOPLE.
AGAINST THE LEADERS IN CHUNGKING WHO DO NOT AS YET COME TO
THEIR SENSES, ALTHOUGH THEY, TOO, BEFORE THEIR OWN EYES, SEE THE
LIVING FACT THAT THE U.S. AND GREAT BRITAIN COAX VARIOUS COUNTRIES
AND PEOPLES WITH DECEPTION, AND WITH CAJOLERY SACRIFICE ALL OTHER
NATIONS ARE DEFEATED, PAY NO ATTENTION TO THEM WHATSOEVER, I AM
MOVED WITH RIGHTEOUS INDIGNATION, WHICH NO WORDS CAN DESCRIBE.
AT THE SAME TIME, I CANNOT HELP HAVING COMPASSION ON THE CHINESE
PEOPLE, WHO AT THIS DAWNING OF A GREATER EAST ASIA, ARE UNNECESSARILY
DISTRESSED BY FOLLOWING BLINDLY THOSE LEADERS IN THEIR FUTILE
RESISTANCE AGAINST JAPAN.

=== Page 519 === (confidence 0.99)
 IS ONLY THREE MONTHS SINCE THE OPENING OF THE WAR OF
TER EAST ASIA. THE AMERICAN AND BRITISH MAIN FLEETS HAVE ALREADY
APPEARED FROM THE SURFACE OF THE PACIFIC, AND ALL OF THE ENEMIES
BASES IN THE SOUTHWESTERN PACIFIC HAVE FALLÉN INTO OUR HANDS. THE
U.S. AND GREAT BRITAIN, WHILE UNDERESTIMATING THE NATIONAL STRENGTH
OF JAPAN AND HAD VAUNTÉD THEIR UNDEFEATABLE POSITIONS BEFORE THE
WAR, AND FINALLY FORCED JAPAN TO OPEN HOSTILITIES BY REFUSING TO
LISTEN TO OUR RIGHTFUL CONTENTION, HAVE NOW EXPOSED THEIR REALITY
OF FIGHTING. THEY WON NO BATTLES AND DEFENDED NO POSITION THAT
WAS NOT WRESTED FROM THEM.
SEEING EXPLANATIONS WITH WHICH AMERICAN AND BRITISH GOVERNMENT
LEADERS TRY TO AVOID THEIR RESPONSIBILITIES FOR THIS EXPOSURE OF
REALITY, AND HEARING THEIR UTTERANCES OF FALSE PROPAGANDA, I CAN
SACRCELY FIND ANY ADEQUATE WORDS WITH WHICH TO COMMENT ON THE SHAME-
LESS ATTITUDE OF THOSE GOVERNMENT AUTHORITIES. THE AMERICAN AND
BRITISH GOVERNMENTS ARE ENTERTAINING FAINT HOPES FOR THE DISTANT
FUTURE AND ARE BOASTING ABOUT THEIR EXTENSIVE ARMAMENT EXPANSION
PROGRAMS. SUCH AN ATTITUDE OF THEIRS IS ONLY TO MINIMIZE,
PURPOSELY, THE STRATEGIC SUPERIORITY WHICH JAPAN HAS WRESTED
FROM THEM IN PACIFIC, AND TO CLOSE THEIR EYES TO LONG (CHAIN IN) AND
THE SUPERB EXECUTION OF STRATEGY, AND THE LOYALTY AND BRAVERY OF
OUR NATION, AND HIDE THEIR UNEASINESS WITH AN ARRAY OF LARGE
FIGURES.
elear as
"IT IS AS CTKAS DAY) THAT THEY ARE DESTINED TO BE

BRITAIN WHO ARE MISGUIDED BY THE AMDITION OF THEIR GOVERNMENT
AUTHORITIES, WHO HAVE VIOLATED THEIR TRADITIONS AND VENTURED INTO
HOPELESS WAR, AND NOW ARE APPROACHING, STEP BY STEP, COMPLETE
COLLAPSE, WILL DEEPLY RECONSIDER THEIR POSITION AT THIS TIME.
"IT IS TO BE GREATLY CONGRATULATED THAT OUR ALLIES IN EUROPE,
ESPECIALLY GERMANY AND ITALY, ARE STEADILY ACHIEVING GREAT VICTORIES
SIMULTANEOUSLY WITH OUR ACTIVE MILITARY OPERATIONS. IT IS OUR
INTENTION TO ENHANCE OUR COOPERATION WITH THEM AND THEREBY ATTAIN THE
OBJECT OF WAR.
FOR THE CONSISTENT COOPERATION AFFORDED BY MANCHOKUO, THE NATIONAL
GOVERNMENT OF CHINA AND THAILAND, AND OTHERS, JAPAN IS DÉEPLY
GRATEFUL. JAPAN INTENDS TO STRENGTHEN FURTHER HER COOPERATION
WITH THESE NATIONS, WHO SHARE THE TASK OF CONSTRUCTING GREATER
EAST ASIA, AND GO VIGOROUSLY FORWARD TO ATTAIN OUR COMMON
OBJECT.
"THE GLORIOUS VICTORIES WE HAVE WON ARE DUE TO HIS IMPERIAL
MAJESTY, THE EMPEROR, UNDER WHOSE AUGUST (VIRTUE), THE LOYAL
COFFICERS) AND MEN OF IMPERIAL ARMY AND NAVY WHO HAVE FOUGHT WITH
SUPERB (STRATEGISM) AND COURAGE, FOR THIS WE ARE ALL THANKFUL.

=== Page 520 === (confidence 0.97)
"IT IS TO BE NOTED, AT THE SAME TIME, THAT BEHIND THESE BRILLIANT
VICTORIES ARE GREAT CÓNTRIBUTIONS OF THÉ PEOPLE WHO ARE FILLING
THEIR RESPONSIBILITIES ON THE HOME FRONT IN THE PERSEVERANCE OF
ALL KINDS OF HARDSHIPS. JAPAN HAS THUS WON GREAT VICTORIES IN THE
OPENING STAGE OF WAR.
"SHE MUST FURTHER ACHIEVE SUCH BRILLIANT VICTORIES BY CARRYING ON
VIGOROUS MILITARY OPERATIONS TO THOROUGHLY CRUSH THE U.S. AND
GREAT BRITAIN THEREBY ESTABLISHING A NEW ORDER OF GREATER EAST ASIA
AND THE REALIZATION OF WORLD PEACE. THE WAR STILL REMAINS TO BE
FOUGHT HEREAFTER, AND THE CONSTRUCTIVE UNDERTAKING WILL HENCEFORTH
BE CARRIED ON PROPER LINES WITH MORE VIGOUR.
"WE, PEOPLE OF JAPAN, SHOULD NOT BE INTOXICATED WITH THESE EARLY
VICTORIES, BUT FURTHER ENHANCE THE INFLEXIBLE SPIRIT WITH WHICH WE
HAVE SILENTLY ENDEAVORED SINCE THE MANCHURIAN INCIDENT, AND BY
SURMOUNTING ALL KINDS OF HARDSHIPS, AND INSPIRED US TO BRILLIANT
HOPES FOR THE FUTURE, ATTAINED THE CROWNING GLORY WITH THE
