JAPAN'S CLIQUES:
THE “BATSU"
In past years Japanese writers and speakers have
frequently used the term "batsu" in referring to
cliques who have controlled governmental policy.
The word has an unfavorable connotation. It occurs
in four combinations: "zaibatsu," the big financiers
or financial groups; "gumbatsu," the militarists;
"mombatsu," the influential aristocracy; and "kam-
batsu," the higher bureaucrats. Since the outbreak
of the war with China, Japan has suppressed every
allusion to class antagonism. As a result, there are
now few references to these "batsu."
Our purpose here is to explain what these words
mean in Japan and the arguments against the "batsu"
which have been popular in the past and which may
carry conviction to Japanese minds in the future.
Zaibatsu
The word "zaibatsu" means literally the financial
clique. It is used in a collective sense similar to
that of the phrase "Wall Street" or "the interests"
in the United States.
Concentration of wealth and industrial control in
Japan gives the word particular weight. It has
been estimated that one family alone, the Mitsui,
I

=== Page 522 === (confidence 1.00)
controls 15 percent of Japan's industry. Three
houses, Mitsui, Sumitomo, and Mitsubishi, together
control 25 percent or more. If one includes the
Imperial Household, the Ministry of Finance, and
about five other groups, this control rises to some-
thing like 70 percent of Japan's industry. The
individuals and families which exercise this control
constitute a multi-millionaire class.
These great family interests are organized in both
horizontal and vertical trusts. They are controlled
by holding companies, such as the Mitsui Gomei
Kaisha, the entire stock of which is held by members
of the Mitsui family. The holding company, in
turn, controls banks and insurance companies which
add to the financial resources of the great industrial
and commercial empire.
The Mitsui family became wealthy in the seven-
teenth century through astute development of com-
mercial policy and methods in the trade between
Osaka and Tokyo. A Mitsui was the banker for the
Imperial Restoration in 1867-68, and the family
thereby gained an inside track in the later industrial-
ization of Japan. Today Mitsui controls one of
Japan's strongest private banks, insurance companies
of several varieties, mines, shipping lines, power
companies, spinning and weaving mills, and a great
commercial organization. The Mitsui Bussan, or
Mitsui Trading Company, has been said to handle
70 percent of Japan's foreign trade.
Sumitomo is an old family which became rich
through control of copper mines in the sixteenth and
seventeenth centuries. It is today heavily interested
in metals and armaments and has prospered through
the war boom.
2

=== Page 523 === (confidence 1.00)
The Mitsubishi firm is of recent origin. It is con-
trolled by the Iwasaki family, which gained a com-
manding place in the shipping world through profit-
able transport contracts in connection with the
Formosan expedition of 1874-75. As a result, Mit-
subishi today controls a considerable part of Japan's
merchant marine and is important in shipbuilding.
Beyond this, however, it has branched out into
almost every conceivable line of industry and
commerce.
Nissan, an outstanding example of the "new in-
dustrialists," is based on the fortune and industrial
interests of Fusanosuke Kuhara. It is now directed
by Yoshisuke Aikawa and has expanded enormously
in the last few years through association with the
army and through a preferred place in the industrial
development of Manchuria where it has become
the Manchuria Heavy Industry Development
Corporation.
In past years, these big financial interests con-
tributed heavily to the political parties and thus, as
every Japanese knows, encouraged political corrup-
tion. Since association with them offers almost the
only avenue to wealth in Japan, the zaibatsu are a
constant temptation to civil servants and to army
and navy leaders. Their influence in political circles,
as well as their great wealth, was widely criticized
during the 1920's. Many young army officers joined
in this criticism after the Manchurian incident in
1931. The term "Showa Ishin," or Showa Restora-
tion, was coined by the patriotic societies to signify
the desired return to the Emperor of financial control
of the country, which the zaibatsu were said to hold.
Even higher army leaders were charged with corrupt
3

=== Page 524 === (confidence 1.00)
association with the zaibatsu, and this charge played
a part in the various assassinations which took place
from 1932 to 1936.
In recent years the zaibatsu have attempted to
evade this criticism through various concessions.
Stock in many of their enterprises, which were
formerly held rather closely, have been placed on
public sale. Mitsubishi and the others have con-
tributed ostentatiously to innumerable charities and
to public subscriptions for the equipment of the
army and navy. A Mitsui Foundation has been
endowed to support charitable activities and scientific
research.
In spite of these attempts to win public favor and
the efforts of the government to soften class feeling
the zaibatsu remain targets for attack because of
their continuing influence in Japanese politics and
their war profits.
Gumbatsu
"Gumbatsu" means literally a military clique, re-
ferring to both army and navy leaders. Following
the Meiji Restoration in 1868 the Japanese Army and
Navy came under the control of Choshu and Satsuma,
respectively, the two great feudal clans which had
provided the military resources to back the restora-
tion movement. Although recruitment of lower per-
sonnel included most areas and classes of society,
control at the top was retained by members of these
clans well into the twentieth century. Satsuma con-
trolled the high navy positions and Choshu the army
generalships. Through the control of the army
which he had built up, Prince Yamagata of Choshu
became one of Japan's most powerful statesmen.
4

=== Page 525 === (confidence 1.00)
The army and navy enjoy a special position in the
cabinet because of a rule of long standing that only a
general or lieutenant general in active service may
hold the position of Minister of the Army and only
an admiral or vice admiral that of Minister of the
Navy. As a result, no new cabinet can be formed
without the support of high leaders in these two
groups, and a cabinet may easily be overthrown by
army or navy opposition.
Army recruiting has gradually been democratized,
and clan connections are no longer a major pre-
requisite for promotion. Nevertheless, it is inev-
itable that some traces of the old close control should
remain. For example, General Terauchi, who has
been influential in recent years, is the son of Premier
Terauchi, who was also a general and came from the
Choshu clan.
Control of the army and of the navy continues to be
exercised in each case by a relatively small group of
men who, whatever their origin, are almost independ-
ent of any outside control. At the present time this
group probably includes Generals Itagaki, Tojo,
Nishio, Minami, and Terauchi. Control in the navy
is probably equally concentrated, but during the last
few years it has been managed more adroitly and less
public criticism has been aroused.
The concentration of control in both services,
however, has aroused suspicion without and within
them. There have been major scandals. In 1913
it was discovered that several admirals had been
receiving sizable commissions from companies which
had been awarded contracts for battleships. As late
as 1937, Lieutenant General Uemura, formerly
Director of the Board of Ordnance, was convicted of
5

=== Page 526 === (confidence 0.99)
accepting bribes. Radical young officers within the
army have charged political corruption: In the fall
of 1935 a Colonel Aizawa assassinated the Chief of the
Military Affairs Bureau, Major General Nagata,
who he alleged had accepted material benefits from
the financiers in return for an agreement to suppress
the Young Officers' movement. The circulation of
such opinions is facilitated by the great gap between
the low salaries of the lieutenants and captains and
the liberal allowances enjoyed by higher officers and
by the fact that army secret funds are known to
afford opportunities for graft with little danger
of detection.
Mombatsu
The term "mombatsu" means literally the gate
clique and refers to those members of Japan's heredi-
tary aristocracy who have the longest family tradi-
tion and who today exercise political, social, or eco-
nomic power.
The nobility, particularly the old court nobles, hold
many positions close to the Emperor and are sup-
posed to exert covert political influence. Prince
Saionji, who died recently, was the Emperor's highest
adviser and was consulted in regard to the appoint-
ment of each new cabinet. His family has been close
to the Imperial Court since about 900 A. D. Mat-
sudaira, present Minister of the Imperial Household,
comes from an old feudal family. The nobility also
exercises political influence through the House of
Peers. The House of Peers has powers almost equal
to those of the elected House of Representatives.
Finally, their famous names and influential family
connections give the nobility a great advantage in
6

=== Page 527 === (confidence 1.00)
political life. The rise of Prince Konoye to the
premiership must be attributed largely to the fact
that the Konoye family, like that of the Saionji, has
been closely connected with the Imperial Household
for centuries.
Japanese have not forgotten that throughout their
history monoply of power came through control of
the throne, first by aristocrats and later by the feudal
barons. The preferred position, unearned advan-
tages, and snobbery of the nobility are resented. On
the other hand, the influence of the nobility has
been relatively slight during recent years with the
result that they are not as vulnerable as the zaibatsu
or the gumbatsu.
Kambatsu
The term "kambatsu" means official clique and
refers primarily to the higher ranks of the civil
service.
The civil service is firmly entrenched in Japan.
It is well protected from the influence of the political
parties, and civil-service promotion carries through
to the position of vice minister in the larger depart-
ments. Moreover, because of the prestige which
government servants enjoy, many cabinet ministers
have been chosen from among their ranks. Many
retired government officials are appointed to the
House of Peers or to the Privy Council, where they
continue political influence without the burden of
departmental responsibilities. The exclusiveness and
homogeneity of this group is enhanced by the fact
that the ranks of the higher administrative civil
service, while recruited by competitive examination,
7

=== Page 528 === (confidence 1.00)
are in fact made up largely of graduates of Tokyo
Imperial University.
As a result of their power, their policies, and their
relatively closed ranks, the bureaucrats are disliked
by the party politicians, by private businessmen, and
sometimes by the Army. Since they control the
police, they are frequently unpopular among the
people. During recent years many men from the
civil services in Manchukuo and Korea have risen to
important posts in Japan proper. With the gradual
elimination of the parties from cabinet control, the
civil service has expanded its share in policy deter-
mination. The Tojo Cabinet, for example, is made
up almost exclusively of military men and civil
servants, with no representation of private business
or of the political parties. The kambatsu, or bureau-
crats, have been frequently criticized for inefficiency,
incompetency, red tape, and favoritism. Conse-
quently, when Japan's military position becomes un-
favorable they, like the gumbatsu, will be vulnerable
to attack by propaganda.

dated August 19, 1942.
FOREIGN RELATIONS
After the outbreak of war, Japan's foreign relations
were for practical purposes confined to the countries of
the Axis, Soviet Russia, and to Japan's several Far
Eastern satellites principally Thailand and the conquered
territories.
Axis
Japan's relations with the Axis may be described as
correct but not cordial. There is no evidence that Germany
and Italy had been consulted or were even apprised of Japan's
determinati on to attack on December 8, and it may there-
fore be assumed that Japan struck at the United States with
no definite assurance of Axis support.
After the outbreak of war, Japan lost no time in con-
cluding, on December ll, a pact binding the signatories of
the Tripartite Pact of September 27, 1940, to a new pledge.
By this paot it was agreed that Japan, Germany and Italy
should prosecute the war until victory is won; that they
should not conclude armistice or peace without complete
understanding among them; and that they should cooperate
for the purpose of establishing a new order in the meaning
of the previous Tripartite Pact.
On January 18, there was signed at Berlin a pact which,
according to official announcement, "formulates the basic
prinoiple for guidance of strategy against the common enemy
of the three nations." Although no details of this agreement
were made public in Tokyo, it was announoed that the pact
was

=== Page 538 === (confidence 0.99)
5
- 2 -
was drawn up in line with the first item of the December 11
agreement, above mentioned. In commenting on the January 18
agreement, the CHUGAI SHOGYO declared that it should serve
to quiet Anglo-American propaganda that Japan was waging a
racial war against the whites.
Relations with the Axis were discussed by the Fore ign
Minister in his message to the House of Peers on January 21,
in the following words:
"Alignment of Japan, Germany and Italy is being
further cemented, as you are already aware. Close co-
operation among the three allied Powers is steadily
taking definite form in military, diplomatic, economic,
and various other fields. However frantically America.
and Britain may endeavor to alienate Japan, Germany
and Italy and their allies, there is absolutely no
room for such machinations. The iron solidarity of
the Axis Powers is not to be compared with that of the
so-called 'Allies', to whose camp America and Britain
have recruited those exiled governments which exist
only in name. Thus the cooperation of friendly nations
is contributing materially to the prosecution of war
especially to the execution of Japan's policy in the
southern regions."
On the same occasion the Prime Minister declared:
"We are profoundly gratified that our Allies,
especially Germany and Italy, are steadily winning
victories along with Japan for the establishment of a
new world order. Japan intends to strengthen further
its solidarity with these allied Powers in military,
diplomatic, economic and various other spheres and go
forth toward the attainment of the common purpose."
On January 3l, the Foreign Minister declared in a meet-
ing of the Budget Committee of the House of Representatives:
"The fall of Singapore would mark an important
stage of the war. As regards measures to be taken by
Japan when Singapore is in our hands, some of them are
of a nature whioh require that we be in closer liaison
with the European Axis Powers.
"We have already reaohed an agreement with these
Powers in regard to military measures we have decided
to take when the battle for Singapore is at an end.
There are sundry matters of a political and economic
nature on which an agreement of views between Japan
and

=== Page 539 === (confidence 0.99)
6
- 3 -
and its Axis Allies is necessary.
"There is no doubt that there will be a closer
liaison between East Asia and Europe as a result of
collaboration between Japan and its Axis Allies in
working out the problem of dealing w ith the situation."
In the extraordinary session of the Diet on May 27,
the Prime Minister considered it necessary to comment on
Japan's relations with t he Axis in the followi ng manner:
"Even now, America and Britain are spreading
mendacious propaganda in efforts to alienate Japan,
Germany and Italy, but it is hardly necessary to
mention that all such intrigues will be entirely
wasted upon the iron alignment of the Axis Powers,
which is based upon their common lofty aspirations,
animated by their supreme mutual trust."
U.S.S.R.
Relations with the U.S.S.R., judging by the utterances
of Government spokesmen and the press, were characterized
by extreme caution and restraint. There was little of the
blustering attitude which has been exhibited frequently in
the past in dealing with Soviet affairs, and it is obvious
from comments in the press that it has been the policy of
Japan to avoid any entanglement with the U.S.S.R. which
might make it necessary to create a second front in the north.
The statement on December 9 of the Soviet Vice
Commissar for Foreign Affairs announcing Soviet neutrality
and declaring that relations with Japan would be regulated
by the Pact of April 1l, was greeted with satisfaction in
Japan. In the emergency session of the Diet on December 16,
the Foreign Minister declared:
"In reference to Japan's relations with the Soviet
Union, there is no change in the Imperial Government's
attitude for ensuring security in the North, as already
olarified at the previous session of the Diet. On the
part of the Soviet Government, they, too, have repeated-
ly enunciated the ir intention to abide by the Neutrality
Paot with Japan."
Similarly

=== Page 540 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Similarly, on January 21, the Foreign Minister declared in
the House of Peers:
"The relati ons between Japan and the Soviet Union
have since witnessed no change. Their relations are
still regulated by the Neutrality Pact. The rumors of
various kinds emanating as a result of the Soviet
Union's conversations with America and Britain, should
not have any effect at all upon the present relations
between Japan and the Soviet Union."
In reply to an interpellation in the House of Peers,
the Foreign Minister stated on February 8:
"Negotiations for a fishery pact with Soviet
Russia, whi ch were broken off by the out break of the
Soviet-German war, were resumed last December and
efforts are being directed toward obtaining at least a
provisional agreement for the time being.
"Japanese petroleum enterprises in north Saghalien,
which operations were suspended several months ago, are
now being resumed although there are still many ob-
stacles in t he way of normal oil production there by
Japanese industrialists."
This reference to difficulties in Saghalien appears to have
aroused the interest of the newspaper correspondents who on
the following day, February 9, put several questions to the
spokesman of the Board of Information. The following is an
excerpt from the TIMES AND ADVERTISER:
"'It is reported that the Japanese companies in
the north of Saghalien are encountering difficulties
in their work', said one foreign correspondent. 'Can
you tell us what sort of difficulties these are?'
"'I am not informed about the matter', replied
the spokesman. 'Probably the companies are having
labor diffioulties, which, though they may not be
trivial to the companies concerned, have nothing to do
with politics. There is nothing political about their
difriculties.'n
Announcement of the successful conclusion of a fisheries
modus vivendi was made by the Board of Information on Maroh 23,
as follows:
"The negotiation for the conal usion of a Japanese-
Soviet Fisheries Convention continued to be held last
year but was in abeyance on aocount of the out break of
the

=== Page 541 === (confidence 0.99)
8
- 5 -
the German-Soviet war and consequently the negotiation
could not reach a conclusion by the end of the previous year.
Conversations, therefore, have been conducted si nce December
last for a conclusion of a modus vivendi to extend the
validity of the existing Fisheries Convention to the
end of this year. These negotiations having now reached
a satisfactory conclusion, a modus vivondi was signed
on March 20 at Kuibishev between the Japanese Ambassador,
Lieutenant-General Yoshitsugu Tatekawa, and the Soviet
Acting Foreign Commissar, A. J. Vyschinsky."
Comment by the Japanese press in regard to this agreement
carried an undercurrent of disappointment and distrust.
The YOMIURI'S comment is typical:
"As long as the present agreement remains a modus
vivendi, we cannot expect cessation even temporarily,
of the Anglo-American maneuvers for the alienation of
Japanese-Soviet relations. In view of the importance
of the so-called northern route particularly as the
result of the wholesale losses of Pacific bases, the
United States will desperate ly try to work toward the
Soviet Union for the use of this route."
In a speech at the Japan Industrial Club on April 22, the
Foreign Minister declared:
"Japan-Soviet relations at present are fast becom-
ing the cynosure of the world, and the enemy countries
are trying feverishly to give rise to various groundless

Latin America
For some years past the Japanese Government has evinced
increasing interest in Latin America, where Japanese com-
mercial interests have rapidly developed and where Japanese
immigrants now number more than 200,000. For these reasons,
the attitude of Latin America toward the war was watched
wit h the keenest interest in Tokyo, as was shown by the
preoccupation of the press with developments at the Rio
conference.
In the Diet, on January 21, the Foreign Minister
declared:
"It is Japan's intention to maintain, as much

powers in South America and Europe. We are prepared
to respect fully the position of South American
countries, so long as they are not misled by

regards wit h hostility."
Offic ial chagrin at the result of the Rio conference

"in view of strong opposition by the leading South

cajole or intimidate them in order to attain the

INTERNAL AFFAIRS
The Diet and the Imperial Rule Assistance
Association (Yokusan-Kai)
It will be remembered that the Imperial Rule Assistance
Association (hereafter referred to as the IRAA) was created
by Prince Konoye in the autumn of 1940 at a time when the
prestige of the Diet had fallen to such a low point that there
was considerable talk of the need of a fundamental change
in the constitution of the governmental structure. The
IRAA was intended to perform a function which the Diet had
failed to do, namely to serve as a link between the people
and the Government.
The constitutional status of the IRAA is so dubious;
the aims of its leaders so vague; the scope of its functions
so ill-defined, and its connection with the structure of the
constitutional government so uncertain that the student of
Japanese politics finds it a perplexing subject. So also
do many Japanese, who in recent months have become increas-
ingly critical of the IRAA and its lack of concrete results.
In the Diet session during January the Government was asked
on several occasions whether there was intention of abolish-
ing the IRAA, the Government's reply always being negative.
Since it was first created, the IRAA has repeatedly under-
gone reorganization and renovation in the effort to make it
a useful instrument for the Government and not merely a
fifth wheel. In view of the dissolution last year of the
old-line political parties, there exists a persistent need
for some kind of organization to serve as a steering force
in Japanese politics. The IRAA, which is merely a civio
body not political in character, is specifically debarred
from performing this funotion by the terms of its charter.
The

=== Page 552 === (confidence 0.98)
19
- 16 -
The normal four year term of the members of Parliament
had actually expired a year ago, but had been extended by a
special measure. As a preliminary move towards the setting
up of a centralized political organization, the Cabinet
called for a general election to be held in May. To insure
that acceptable representatives would be elected to the
Diet, the Government set up a nominating Council within the
IRAA, the sole function of which council was to select and
recommend suitable men for election. Of the 46l members
elected, 38l were candidates so recommended by the Council.
Although these results represented an overwhelming victory
for the Council's candidates, there was considerable shaking
of heads on the part of the people over this system of "of-
ficially recommended" candidates. Incidentally, the type
of the candidates successful in this election indicates
clearly the type of men now in control of Japanese politics.
Practically every prominent leader of extreme rightest
affiliation seems to appear on the list: Seigo Nakano, a
notorious reactionary; Colonel Kingoro Hashimoto, another
extreme reactionary notorious for implication in the sinking
of the PANAY; Bin Akao, President of the Kenkoku Kai, a
reactionary society; Toshio Shiratori, the notorious former
Foreign Office spokesman and other
less wellknown re-
actionary leaders many of whom have police records of
implication in crimes of political violence.
Since the election of the new Diet representatives in
May, the Government has been busily engaged in the effort
"to integrate into a cogent political force all those men
who had been chosen by popular ballot". The Government's
offorts seem to have taken two directions: one has been
to

=== Page 553 === (confidence 0.99)
20
-17 -
to bolster up the authority of the original IRAA by giving
to it certain powers and functions, notably by appointing
the Vice Minister of the Association to be State Minister
without portfolio and by giving the Association control over
many national movements and bodies, including the All Japan
Youth Association, the Dai Nippon Woman's Association, and
the Dai Nippon Asia Development League which in turn has
control over a large number of patriotic and reactionary
societies.
The second direction of the Government's effort has
been to create an organization which, it is hoped, will
serve as a substitute for the old political parties. This
organization, which was formally inaugurated on May 20 as
the Imperial Rule Assistance Political Association (IRAPA),
is"pledged to crystallize the total strength of the nation,
to mobilize it for creating strong political power, and to
cooperate with the Government whose duty it is to assist
the Throne". Study of the pronouncements in regard to this
organization reveals that there is still great uncertainty
and confusion in the minds of not only the public but even
of the leaders and organizers of the Association as to its
precise functions. It is clear that the new organization
aspires to be the nucleous of all political thought in Japan,
perhaps patterned after the Fascist Party in Italy or the
Nazi Party in Germany, but it is equally clear that no one,
not even t he sponsors of the Association, have a definite
program in mind. Moreover, there has inevitably arisen
confusion between the functions of the IRAA, of the IRAPA,
and of the Diet itself. Late in the Spring it was reported
that there was acrimonious discussion over the respective
functions of the two associations.
Budget

=== Page 554 === (confidence 1.00)
21
-18-
Budget
The General Accounts Budget for the fiscal year beginning
April 1, 1942, was approved by the 79th Session of the Diet
on the fifth day after it had been submitted by the Government.
to
This speedy action was pointed/by Japanese publicists as
evidence of the cooperative spirit of the nation but it
might also be mentioned that perhaps the most important duty
of the Diet is to examine the budget estimates and that this
important function could certainly not have been discharged
in the short space of five days.
The budget as approved totalled Yen 8,698,434,000,
divided in accordance with Japanese practice into a general
account of Yen 6,235,576,000 and a supplementary account of
Yen 2,462,857,000. This general budget represents, however,
only a portion of the actual expenditure for the fiscal year,
for a few days later a special supplementary budget for war
expenditures amounting to Yen 18,000,000,000 was submitted
to the Diet and duly approved. This latter sum, according
to Japanese practice, represents expenditure incurred by
the Government for war purposes, but actually, of course,
many items in the general budget may also be regarded as
part of the war effort. The special budgets for war purposes
approved by the Diet since July 1937, when the China affair
broke out, now total Yen 47,000,000,000.
Bond Issue
Many Japanese have not forgotten that about ten years
ago, Finance Minister Takahashi warned the country that Yen
10,000,000,000 of bonded debt was the limit that Japan
could

=== Page 555 === (confidence 0.99)
22
-19 -
could safely carry. At the end of March 1942, the total issue
of national bonds passed the Yen 47,000,000,000 mark. The
bond issue during the current fiscal year is estimated at
Ye17,000,000,000.
Food and Clothing
Even prior to the out break of the war, shortages had
taken place in a number of commodities, and controls had been
set up for distribution of the more important daily necessi-
ties, notably, rice, flour, matches, sugar, et cetera. At
the same time, metal and manufactured goods of all variety
had become so scarce that it became difficult to purchase many
household articles. Cotton goods had long di sappeared from
the markets, and wool and sik had become so scarce and ex-
pensive as to be unavailable to the general public. The
textile material in general use at the present time is staple
fibre, made from wood pulp, of extremely poor wearing quality.
While these shortages are real and disturbing to the life of
the people, they are not by any means evidence that the nation
is lacking in materials for making war, as was so mistakenly
viewed by certain superficial foreign observers, but were
rather evidence of the Government's determination to exercise
striotest economy in the use by the people of materials use-
ful in the war effort. The Japanese Government has never
hesitated to apply severe controls to the distribution of
commodities even before such controls became absolutely es-
sential. However, it should be made plain that while the diet
of the people has suffered to some extent from the application
of these controls, there has never, even to the present, been
a

=== Page 556 === (confidence 0.99)
23
-20-
a shortage of those foodstuffs regarded by the Japanese as
essential. Although strictly rationed, rice, bean paste,
and to a lesser extent fish and vegetables, have been avail-
able in quantities sufficient to maintain the health of the
people. The Government has never forgotten the serious
disturbances which broke out in l9l8, when rice became un-
available, and in the recent crisis has taken steps in
advance of acute shortages to control distribution of
essential commodities.
Nevertheless, civilian Japanese at the present time may
be said to be living on a bare subsistance level, which
leaves very little margin for further reduction in their
food supply. The rice crop for this year is considerably
smaller than average, although the crop will be supplemented
by rice from the colonies, and especially from the occupied
territories. The problems of shortages of fertilizer and of
farm labor, both affected by the war effort, continue to
harass the Government and to result annually in a further
diminution of the crops.
Morale
It is not easy for the foreign observer to estimate
with confidence the state of mind of the average Japanese
toward the war. More than four years of struggle in China,
with its attendant losses and hardships, have rendered the
Japanese less susceptible to officially inspired enthusiasm
for further adventures but it has also inured them to stresses
and made them more able to stand the straim of new saorifices.
Certainly, prior to the outbreak of war, Japanese of all
classes

=== Page 557 === (confidence 0.99)
24
-21 -
classes regarded with dread the prospect of conflict with two
of the most powerful nations in the world, and there can be
no doubt that the outbreak of war brought little but dire
forebodings to the majority of the Japanese.
Nevertheless, no people at war whose military forces
can report continued successes, especially the breath-taking
successes achieved by Japan dur ing the first six months of
war, need worry about morale. Despite the appalling dangers
faced by their country; despite the belief among many think-
ing people that a decisive victory over the democracies is
impossible of attainment; despite the increasing difficulty
of obtaining the necessities of life; and despite the absence
of almost all of the more pleasant features of life; it may
safely be said that at the present time the average Japanese
faces the war confidently, resolutely, and fairly cheerfully.
What changes would take place in his attitude in the event
of serious and visible losses can, of course, only be
estimated, but it is the belief of those who have lived long
in Japan that the Japanese people are easily capable of sus-
taining far greater privation and far more saorifice than
they have thus far been called on to undergo. Every Japanese
has been thoroughly indoctrinated with the belief that this
is a war of survival and that whereas winning the war would
open to Japan a glorious vista of wealth and power, loss of
the war would mean certain destruotion of most of Japan's
proud achievements of the last half century.
Certainly, the Japanese leaders have not attempted to
deceive the people into taking an unduly optimistic view of
the

=== Page 558 === (confidence 0.99)
25
- 22 -
the difficulties faced. The contrary is true. A few quota-
tions from the public utterances of Japanese leaders may be
of interest in this connection:
On December 17, the Prime Minister declared, in the
course of his speech in the Diet:
"We are prepared that the war will be a
long one. We must, therefore, definitely bear in
mind that our country will hereafter meet many
difficulties..... The war remains rather to be
fought hereafter. We should not be intoxicated
with individual victories and worry about
individual phenomena."
On February 16, the Prime Minister, in reporting on the
Singapore victory, declared:
"Singapore has now fallen. But this means
that it marks the completion of only the first
stage in t he prosecution of the war of Greater
East Asia. There should be no relaxation of
morale nor allowing themselves to be elated by
victory on the part of our people. The war
remains to be fought hereafter."
On January 8, the President of the Industrial Corporation
declared:
"It is natural that all Japan should now
be exultant over the brilliant war results.
However, if anyone should think that the time of
hardships has passed away or the age of abundance
will soon come, it is an enormous fallacy. We
must tread a rough path for several years more
before we can enjoy the abundant supplies of
southern areas."
On Margh 14, the Home Minister declared in the course of a
public address:
"The decisive in the battle of the war of
Greater East Asia has yet to be fought. Instead
of being intoxicated by the brilliant victories
achieged by the Japanese forces in the initial
stages of the war, therefore, the nation must
realize that the present war is destined to become
protracted and must accordingly put forth its
best endeavors to build up a high degree defense
state to cope successfully with the war situation."
On

=== Page 559 === (confidence 0.98)
26
- 23 -
On April 12, the Chief of the Military Affairs Bureau of
the War Office declared in a public statement:
"Some people seem inclined to conclude from
the remarkably favorable program of Japanese
military operations in the War of Greater East
Asia that the day is not far off whem the United
States and Britain will sue for peace and the
war will come to an end, but such an idea is
vastly mistaken. The harder part of the War of
Greater East Asia is yet to be fought hereafter.
Instead of being intoxicated with the brilliant
successes so far achieved, the nation must form a
firmer resolve and bend its entire energy to
attain the object of scuthern construction and
final victory in the war."
Shipping
Since 1937 when Japan began military operations on the
continent on a large scale, the problem of shipping has
grown increasingly acute. Because of the preoccupation of
Ma...
most of the important shipbuilding plants with naval con-
struction, comparatively little commercial tonnage was
produced during these years. With the outbreak of the war
with the United States and the extension of Japan's
strategic lines to the South Seas, the shortage of shipping
and the problem of shipbuilding have been constantly in the
minds of Government leaders. The Prime Minister was reported
in February to have said that the most important problem
confronting Japan's industry was not so much the shortage of
resources as that of the insufficiency of transportation.
It is not difficult to read through the lines of the
statements of official and industrial leaders their feeling
of anxiety over the shipping situation. Unquestionably
shipping is at once the key to Japan's control of the newly
conquered regions in the South as well as the Achilles heel
of Japan's whole program of military expansion.
A

=== Page 560 === (confidence 0.98)
5
2
-24 -
A detailed report on Japanese shipping has been
prepared by the American Consulate at Kobe.
MISCELLANEOUS
Propaganda - Atrocity Stories
Soon after the outbreak of the war, it became obvious
that an organization had been set up to act as an agency
for propaganda, on the order of Herr Goebbels' Propaganda
Ministry in Germany. In conformity with the pattern familiar
to readers of German propaganda, the stories concocted in
Japan are of two types: those magnifying Japan's achieve-
ments and potentialities, and those belittling the enemy
and describing to him various atrocities. It might come
as a surprise to those familiar with Japanese claims to
chivalry to find Japanese propaganda engaged in sneering
at or disparaging a defeated enemy, such as took place when
the Japanese captured General Wainwright after the desperate
struggle in Corregidor. The best explanation is that the
Japanese have installed a number of German propaganda experts
in their organization who have little patience with chivalry
of either the oriental or occidental varieties.
Atrocity stories began to emanate soon after the out-
break of the war when, on January 15, the press seethed with
indignation over an alleged sinking of a hospital ship, the
HARBIN MARU. Again, during the Philippine campaign, a great
deal was said about alleged brutalities of American soldiers
toward Filipino troops. For example, the ASAHI correspondent
reported:
"The

=== Page 561 === (confidence 0.98)
26
- 25 -
"The United States troops did not hesitate
to resort to unpardonable acts of inhumanity. They
placed the Filipino soldiers on the foremost lines
and chained them to trees in the midst of the
fields exposed to Japanese gun fire so that they
themselves could escape. As a result, Philippine
confidence in Americans and friendly sentiment have
disappeared."
Similer accounts of alleged cruelties practiced by
British and Netherland troops on their native levies were
reported from time to time. Much publicity was given to
the finding of alleged gas shells on Guam when the Japanese
troops occupied that Island. Also, the press reported that
in the Burma campaign the Anglo-Indian forces used poison
gas against the Japanese troops on April 14.
Perhaps the most publicized atrocity story was that of
the alleged murder by American troops at Davao of Japanese
civilians. This incident, in which Japanese were alleged
to have been killed and tortured was referred to in-
numerable times as an instance of American brutality.
On December 28, the Prime Minister, in referring to
these alleged atrocities, stated:
"These cowardly and inhuman acts should not be
passed unnoticed and the Army is accordingly taking
op necessary measures in dealing appropriately
with the cruelty with which the enemy has willfully
treated Japanese subjects. It may be stressed here
however that the Imperial Army's policy of according
cordial treatment to enemy subjects, extending
cooperation to the Japanese forces in the occupied
territories still remains unchanged."
THE JAPAN TIMES AND ADVERTISER of December 28 declared:
"At Hongkong the British disgraced themselves
in a manner which makes their dishonorable conduot
in Europe seem mild. They employed the Indian
troops as their shields, a thing any decent minded
people wo uld not do even to their dogs... ....
"The shameless massacre by the Americans
last week of unarmed Japanese civilians at Mindanao,
the discovery of a poison gas plant on Wake Island,
the

=== Page 562 === (confidence 0.99)
2
- 26 -
the rape of Timor, the British pussillanimity at
Hongkong--these shameful atrocities and outrages
have unveiled the British and Americans in their
true color."
Even after the Foreign Minister had revealed the
actual facts behind the Davao affair, it continued as the
favorite horror story of the Japanese press. He declared
in a committee of the Diet on January 31:
"The Davao incident was a matter of great regret.
Information to hand indicates that about 1o Japanese
were massacred in the incident and that 30 others shot
to death. It seems that the dastardly act was not the
work of the regular Army troops. An inquiry into the
details of the affair is under way. We were prompt
to protest to the United States over the affair
through Switzerland. We also firmly demanded the
United States to see that there be no repetition."
Another cause for an outburst of denunciation in the
Japanese press was the order requiring aliens to evacuate
from certain regions of the West Coast in the United States.
This action was declared to have caused a severe shock to
Christians in Japan.
The aerial attack on Japan by American forces called
for the following remarks from the spokesman of the Board
of Information on April 20:
"The fact should be remembered that they machine-
gunned such institutions as primary schools where
innocent children were either at play or study, and
hospitals where helpless invalids were being taken
care of, causing casualties among these patients and
children and civilians only. Having perpetrated such
barbarous aots, even after they were indicted by the
whole world for their cruel massacre of Japanese
residents at Davao and their manhandling of Filipino
soldiers and civilians, the American authorities as
well as their publicity organs have completely
deprived themselves of any excuse or pretert to accuse
other countries' aerial bombing on the principle
of humanity."
In later months, a considerable portion of space in the
press was given over to accounts of alleged hardships and
shortages

=== Page 563 === (confidence 0.99)
3U
- 27 -
shortages suffered in the United States by reason of the war,
a considerable portion of these accounts being D.A.B. reports
emanating from German propaganda factories in Stockholm and
elsewhere, while many were sent from Japanese propaganda
agencies in Buenos Aires. It would be impracticable to
attempt to describe these articles in detail but the quota-
tion of a few headlines will suffice to convey an idea of the
type of stories:
"United States spread lies to bait South America"
"Material shortage ke enly felt by United States"
"Living costs rise in New York City as result of war"
"Sugar in America seen decreasing"
"United States hates helping Brit ish in Pacific;
Controversy seen"
"Americans antagonistic to rubber saving"
"America no match states Wainwright; Captive Lt.Gen.
Stresses Superiority of Nippon forces"
"General Wainwright cowardly behaviour during
Corregidor negotia tion"
"People in America lack war feeling"
"Cowardly action of United States troops told"
"Japan winning by superior leadership; put MacArthur
in charge, says Lippmann"
"Material shor tage hits United States sever ely"
"War reverses undermining Albions economic strength"
Much of the propaganda appearing under headlines such
as those above quoted is, of course, puerile and naive.
However, its cumulative effect may be judged by the fact
that even American citizens re siding in Japan during the
past six months of the war have repeatedly commented on the
depressing effect of daily perusal of the newspapers in
Japan, even when it was known to them that much of what ap-
peared in the papers was false or misleading. It may safely
be said that while Japanese propaganda has not attained any-
thing like the effectiveness of the Goebbel propaganda factory,
by sheer repetition and persistance it serves the purpose in
Japan

=== Page 564 === (confidence 0.99)
31
- 28 -
Japanof playing up Japanese victories, of concealing
Japanese losses, and of magnifying the losses and diffi-
culties of enemy countries.
American Club
Immediately after the out break of the war, the American
Club of Tokyo was closed by the authorities and its assets
handed over to an administrater appointed by the Japanese
Government. Aside from such physical properties as furniture,
furnishings, and a large library, there was a considerable
stock of spirits worth more than Yen 30,O00, according to
the papers. The Japanese members of the Club made an attempt
to convert the American Club into an international club but
were unable to secure the consent of the American members
of the Club who, by the terms of its constitution, exercise
controlling vote.
Tokyo Club
The Tokyo Club, which for more than sixty years has
been one of the principal centers of social intercourse
between Japanese and foreign residents of Tokyo, was also
ordered dissolved soon after the outbreak of the war. This
club has been for some time an object of suspicion in certain
official quarters because of its strong international
character at a time when Japanese are bent on extirpating
foreign influence from Japan. The large and valuable
properties were taken over for the use of the Foreign Office,
whioh had lost one of its buildings in a fire at the end
of the year.
Enemy

=== Page 565 === (confidence 0.98)
32
- 29 -
Enemy Assets
In a radio speech on December 27, the Director of the
Foreign Exchange Bureau disclosed that enemy assets in
Japan totalled about Yen 1,800,000,000. Properties owned
by nationals of the United States and the British Empire
residing in Japan are estimated at about Yen 400,000,000;
and loans, private and governmental, from those countries
amount to Yen 1,400,000,000. These assets are to be ad-
ministered in accordance with an Enemy Assets Management Law,
approved by the Diet on December 19, under the direction
of the Finance Minister. Early in February, most of the
important enemy concerns in Japan were handed over by the
Finance Ministry to the control of various Japanese firms
and banks and, in certain cases, to individuals.
Go1d
In reply to a question in the House of Peers, the Finance
Minister stated on February 10 that thereafter the Japanese
Government "would regard gold as a mere commodity". He also
said that as a result of the recent revision of the Bank of
Japan Law, the yen had become a managed currency definitely
divorced from gold and that from then on the price of gold
would be fixed by the Government with reference to the
prevailing economic conditions. The Finance Minister
deolared that in view of the importance of gold as a com-
modity, the Government would continue to subsidize the
mining of the metal.
New

=== Page 566 === (confidence 0.99)
33
- 30 -
New Bank of Japan Law
In accordance with the new law approved by the Cabinet
on March 1l, the Bank of Japan has been reorganized, "in
order to undertake more freely financing of the industrial
productivity expansion program and regulation of the monetary
policy in regard to the occupied areas in the south." The
bank note issue will no longer be based on the volume of gold
reserves but will be fixed at the discretion of the Finance
Minister. Instead of dealing exc usively with commercial
financing as heretofore, the Bank will engage in industrial
financing. Furthermore, the Bank has been empowered to
conclude exchange and banking contracts with the central
bank of any member of the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity
Sphere, thereby to serve as the clearing house for bills
of exchange for all countries of the Sphere. Capitalization
of the Bank was increased from Yen 55,000,000 to Yen
100,000,000.
Rumor Law
Even though there already existed severe laws to
punish the spreading or rumors, the Government secured the
paszage, in December, of a new "Law of Speech, Publication,
and Assembly." Under this law, severe penalties may be
imposed on those who spread any kind of rumors concerning
the military, political, financial, and economic situati on.
"Anyone who communicates to others his private opinions,
actual facts, or his personal judgments will be dealt with
according to the law, if the facts, opinions and judgments
confuse or bewilder the public mind."
Chinese

=== Page 567 === (confidence 0.99)
34
- 31 -
Chinese Merchants in Occupied Territories
Considerable attention has been given to the problem
of the Chinese residents of occupied regions in the south,
who are said to number possibly six million. These Chinese
occupy an important position in the economy of Malaya,
Burma, Java, and the Philippines. Although they have taken
an important part in anti-Japanese agitation and in the
financial support of the Chungking Government, the Japanese
realize that these Chinese cannot be eliminated without
serious economic consequences. In the course of a lengthy
statement issued in April by the War Office, it was
declared that "it is Japan's policy to endeavor to make use
of these Chinese merchants and to employ every means to
eliminate them from the Chungking Government. Those Chinese
who refuse to cooperate with Japan will be severely dealt
with." It was reported from Singapore in February that
Chinese merchants suspected of having connection with the
Chungking Government were rounded up with a view to their
deportation.

Consul General Quarton's despatch of

Assistance Association. If one has time to
read eleven single-spaced pages of mystical
nonsense designed to prove that Japan's mission
Is to unite all us humans under the benign
rule of the Japanese Emperor, he may read the
pamphlet, otherwise Mr. Tolischus' summary
is sufficient to convey the sense of the pam-
phlet. That writers of such rubbish should be
honored and be eminent in Japan today reflects
perhaps the decay of objective thinking in
Japan if not the prevalent dementia praecox

PARAPHRASE
A telegram dated September 17, l942, from the
American Embassy at Chungking reads substantially as follows:
The American Military Attaché has been informed by the
Chinese Chief of Military Intelligence that the Japanese
Emperor yesterday presided over a meeting of the Privy
Couneil. No corroborating information has been received by
the Embassy from other sources. The Embassy's informant,
who did mt reveal the source of his information, interprets
those events as an indication of drastic action at an early
date, as, for example, war with the Soviet Union.

September 17, 1942, 3:54 p.m.
The Military Attache has received a report that
the Privy Couneil of Japen met September sixteenth
with the Emperor presiding over the meeting. Early
drastie action by Japan is indicated by this meeting
according to the Chief of Chinese Military Intelligence
who gave the Military Attache the report. He did not
state what was his eouree of information. He believes
that this drastic action might possibly be war with
Russia for example. This report is not confirmed or
supported by any information reaching the Embassy from
other souroes.

KILSOO K. HAAN
President Franklin D. Roosevelt,

Washington Representative
EASTERN AFFAIRS
Pacific War Council
ASEPLAZ
dearvMr. President:
Department of State
On August 24, 1942, I rece1ved word from reliable sources
that there would be a "Cabinet crisis" in Japan.
copy
Our informants reported that there was a definite riff be-
tween Ambassador Ott of Germany and Premier Tojo of Japan over the
question of an invasion of Siberia.
We were informed that the Japanese had promised the Germans
that they would invade Siberia in June 1f the Nazis succeeded in tak-
ing Stalingrad and the Caucasus. The German Embassy in Tokyo has
continually pressed the Japanese to invade Siberia, and the Kwantung
Army group has ardently supported the thesis of the German Embassy.
9
The Tojo Cabinet, we are informed, has steadfastly refused
4
to invade Siberia, excusing itself by stating that the Germans had
not yet taken Stalingrad. Now that the Germans are at the gates of
Stalingrad, they have again refused the German Ambassador's demand,
stating that 1t is too late in the season. In other words, weather
conditions in Siberia make an invasion unfavorable.
f11.001k
Last week, as associate editor of "Affairs Week by Week"
I published the above information as a news release.
G
In view of the above information we believe that the report-
ed "Cabinet crisis" which did take place was caused by the pressure of
Ambassador Ott, the Kwantung Army group, and Foreign Min1ster Togo for
an immediate Siberian campaign. And in view of Ambassador Ott's
failure to force the Japanese Cabinet to attack Siberia we also believe
that the Siberian campaign has been temporarily abandoned in favor of
increased military movement toward the Southwestern Pacific.
According to the published Japanese naval war plans, the key
to the control of Australia is Port Moresby. Hence, it is our belief
that Premier Tojo is now following the "naval school" of thought who
S
favor the consolidation of the ocoupied areas in the Southern Pacific
and aid t Hitler by collaborating withthe radical elements in India
to bring about internal disorders for an eventual attack on India.
As head of the Pacific War Counoil, we hope this information
ill be taken into consideration.
Very respectfully yours,
Kileoo K. Hn
Kilsoo K. Ham

BNO-KOREAN PEOPLES' LEAGUE
101 D STREET. NORTHEAST
WASHINGT N.O C.
Official and Public Comments Regarding Kilsoo K. Haan's Activities
Seoretary of Navy Frank Knox, April 26. 194l:
"Your facts and predictions have indeed been borne out by the passage of
time and I assure you that the informaticn that you have given us has
always been highly appreciated."
Senator Guy M. Gillette. Member of Foreign Relations and Naval Committee. Apr. 9 '39.
"Mr. Haan was a valuable and helpful witness and it seemed to me and
several of us that he was in possession of information and in control
of certain channels of information that not only aided the Committee
but would be of interest to the State Department."
Dr. John R. Leohner, Public Relations Secretary, State Department of California,
Sept. 14, 1940:
"Your report and the subsequent conferences I have had with you give me
the assurance that this nation has many valuable friends among the Korean
people. At a time like this loyalty is most helpful and commendable, and
I express the opinion of many patriotic groups when I tell you that your
work and the work of the League is genuinely appreciated."
Hon. Marshall E. Dimock, Administrative Assistant, U. S. Department of Justice,
Immigration and Naturalization Bureau, Sept. 5, 1940:
"I am sure that the efforts of you and your group on behalf of democracy
are appreciated by everyone loyally attached to the principles of demo-
cratic government."
New York Times. April 15, 194l:
"If the State Department was surprised by the negotiation of the Soviet-
Japanese pact, the Sino-Korean Peoples' League, which maintains offices
in this city (Washington, D.C.) was not. It recalled today that in a
letter to Secretary Hull on March 25 it said that such an agreement
would be signed not later than April 29."
Mr. Willard Harper, National Defense Forum Leader, Los Angeles, Calif. Feb. 24, 194l:
"Mr. Haan was a very effective speaker. He spoke frankly, yet kindly. Some of
the interpretations which he made aroused strong opposition from certain mem-
bers (Japanese and Japanese-American citizens) but he answered the questions
and arguments fram the floor in a courteous and gentlemanly manner."
Miss Hazel Meyer, Secretary, University Dinner Club, Occidental College, Los Angeles
Deo. 8. 1940:
"The enthusiasm and interest engendered by your talk to us on
last Monday evening has reverberated through the week. We do need to be aware
and alert these days and you did help us to open our eyes and mind."

=== Page 603 === (confidence 0.99)
SINO-KOREAN PEOPLES LEAGUE
101 D Street N. E.
Washington, D. C.
June 25, 1942
Honcy Cordell Hull
ry of State
artment of State
Jashington, D. C.
Your Excellency:
The Sino-Korean Peoples' League, representing the newly formed Korean National
Front Federation, composed of four Korean political parties in the Far East, do humbly
and sincerely submit to your Excellency its aims and war activities outlined by the
Sixth Plenary Session of the Korean National Revolutionary Congress recently held in
Chungking, China, November 1941.
We submit the following eleven points in the belief and hope that it will be of
some value to your Excellency in your decision in arriving at some effective policy
giving aid or aids to the Koreans. Today we need more than honorable intentions and
friendly gestures of friendship. We need positive help from America, both moral and
material aid.
The Eleven Points Are:
1. To overthrow Japanese Imperialist rule and to establish in Korea a free, independ-
ent and a democratic republic.
2. To summon a peoples' assembly which is to frame a constitution and make arrangement
for holding a general election.
3. To eliminate the vestiges of old feudal system, detrimental to the future welfare
of Korea.
4. To confiscate the property of all Japanese imperialists.
5. To afford special protection for the welfare and business enterprises of those cit-
izens who have opposed the Axis Powers interests.
6. To institute an agrarian policy and redistribute the land among the tenants and
farmers.
7. To reduce working hours and put into force various measures of social security for
the benefit of the workers.
8. To establish political, economic and social equality of the sexes.
9. To institute freedom of speech, publication, assembly and of worship.
10. To institute compulsory education, vocational training and social insurance at the
expense of the State.
11. To seek cooperation with all countries on a basis of equality, mutual assistance in
the security of national defense, in the pursuit of peace and happiness of mankind:
The eleven points were unanimously approved and accepted by all four Korean party
representatives from Siberia, North China, Manchuria, Korea and Japan.
Sincerely submitted,
Kilsoo K. Haan
Washington Representative
Sino-Korean Peoples League

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
October 21, 1942
Reference underlying confidential des-
patch no. 4434, September 15, 1942 from the
American Ambassador at Santiago, enclosing
a memorandum of an interview with Augusto
Iglesis, one of the Chilean journalists who
recently returned from Japan.
The memorandum states that although
Mr. Iglosios was pro-German (he was in close
association with the Nazis and his trip was
made possible by the intervention of the
German Embassy), and although not previously
a friend of the United States, he came to the
conclusion after visiting Japan that only an
American victory could save the countries of
the Pacific. In arriving at this view, he
lappears to have been influenced strongly by
Japanese manifestations of hatred of the
white races, including Japan's allies, and the
fanatical war discipline of the Japanese army
and people.
Mr. Iglesis found that the Japanese
respected the Russians more than any other
whites and he maintains that, because of
seoret fear based on unsuccessful raids on
Siberian forces, Japan will never attack
Russia. Japan believes, according to
Mr. Iglesies, that it can come to an under-
standing with China and Russia if a negoti-
ated peace am be brought about between
the latter and Germany.

MEMORANDUM
SUBJECT: A Returned Chileen Journalist Speaks about JAPAN
The following was told by AUGUSTO IGLESIAS, a Chilean journalist
recently returned from JAPAN, to a newspaper colleegue in confidence
under promise that no part of his story would be published.
IGLESIAS is a public funtionary and a writer who sometimes col-
laborates with LA OPINION, but is not regularly connected with that
newspaper. He has long been considered PRO-GERMAN, and it was through
the intervention of the Germen Embassy that he went to Japan, and since
no rogular mamber of LA OPINION could make the trip, IGIESIAS went as that
newspaper's representative. The German Embassy even went so fer as to
buy his clothes and it is because of such close association with the
NAZIS, that perhaps he was allowed, if eny exceptions were mede, to
see and hear more than the rest of his party.
Curiously enough his general reaction to the trip is one of dis-
pleasure. He was closely watched by the police and was foroed to eat
what was desoribed as the "stendard" meal which was of poor quality and
is eaten by the people in general from the members of the Gavernmemt down
to the lowest strata - the only difference being that those more furtunate
could have a larger quantity. His private life was constantly spied upon
and on two oocasions his room was entered while he was entertaining
female company. Any associations between Japanese women and foreigners
were forbidden and Japanese women csught by the police were usually pun-
ished by death. All foreign women were considered spies and Japanese men
were not allowed to associate with them. Furthermore there were no eabarets,
or demeing, or diversions of any kind and the people thought only of the
war. There was mo attempt to lift their moral beoause there was no need.
Life mecmt nothing to them but to serve the Emperor.
IGLESIAS was profoundly impressed and amased by the mistieim of
the East. Whem he complimeated a Japemese on the growth of a plant or a
tree, he was told that sueh things took place basking in the rays of
virtue whieh ememsted from the Son of Heevem. Suring a severe shake
that was nearly an earthquake, the people gathered before the Imperial
Palace to ask the Maperor's pardon because they had lied, beseeshing
him to stop the quake and forgive his people.
Following the Japemese vietories in the South, GENERAL YAMASHITA
retursed to Tekye where, ceatrary to what would have taken plase in
any otter part of the world, there were no paredes nor smy here worship.
The viesery was entirely dwe to the fast that the ell-overeeming virtues
of the Eaperor had superimposed themselves on less fortusate people to
whem the Bmperor's faithrul serviters had beem able to bring his all en-
veleping geodaess. However to be more exact, IGLESLAS said that it was
kmomm that powerful wndermining fifth colwms mstivities were a more
petemt fester is the victories than the might of the Mmperor's arms.
The Ameriesm air raid em Tokyo hed a tremendous reperoussion
on the Jepemese peeple. First they had beem constently told that such
a thing wes impessible and the raid, if repeated mamy times, could cause
a semerel breekine demm of the merale of the people bessmse this cme air
astenk hed a surions atfeet in thes the first reastien was to see whether

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
October 28, 1942
Reference underlying despatch no. 636
from Chungking dated September 21, 1942 en-
closing a copy in translation of an article
by Mr. Yang Yun-chu, former Chinese Chargé
d'Affaires at Tokyo and now Director of the
East Asiatic Affairs Department of the Foreign
Ministry, in regard to the resignetion of
Japanese Foreign Minister Togo.
Mr. Yang regards the resignation of Togo
as the final triumph of Japanese militarists
over the Foreign Office and moderete elements
1n Japan. He states that the appointment of
Togo as Foreign Minister was primerily for the
purpose of maintaining friendly relations with
Soviet Ruesia and secondarily, as Togo wes
known as a "moderate", to give America and
Great Britain a false sense of security.
Mr. Yang further states that the Jepanese

=== Page 620 === (confidence 0.99)
Jure No. 1 to Despatch No. 636 dated September 21, 1942
from American Embassy at Chungking.
TRANSLATION FROM CENTRAL DAILY NEWS OF SEPTEMBER 17, 1942
The resignation of Togo as Japanese foreign minister and
the formation of the "Greater East Asiatic Ministry" mean the
final triumph of the Japanese militarists over the diplomats
in the Japanese internal political strife, stated Mr. Yang Yun-chu,
former Chinese charge d'affaires in Tokyo and at present
director of the East Asiatic Affairs Department of the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs, in a signed article appearing in the Central
Daily News, on September 14.
Judging from the present Japanese war policy, Togo resigned
because there is no longer any reason for him to remain in
office, Mr. Yang stated. Although Togo was well-known as an expert
on German questions, General Tojo did not give him the foreign
affairs portfolio to further Japanese relations with the Axis,
but to pacify the U.S.S.R. Togo had brought about the successful
conclusion of the sale of the Chinese Eastern Railway and the
Nomanhan truce. When Japanese relations with America and Great
Britain were rapidly deteriorating last fall, Japan decided she must
keep the U.S.S.R. as a friend instead of antagonizing the Soviets.
Togo was thus invited to head the forein ministry with Nishi, an
expert on S viet affairs, as vice-minister. Also, Togo as foreign
minister, known abroad as a "moderate," would give America and
Great Britain a false sense of security, thus enabling Japan to
better prepare for her treacherous attacks. With the outbreak of the
Pacific War last December, however, Togo's usefulness as a "moderate"
ended.
Togo tried to bring about a closer relationship between Japan
and the U.S.S.R. The appointment of Sato as Japanese ambassador to
Moscow was his idea. But, in spite of Sato's activities in
Moscow and Kuibyshev, relations between the U.S.S.R. and America
and Great Britain steadily improved. On four or five occasions,
Togo emphasized the effectiveness of the Soviet-Japanese
Neutrality Pact, but received no response from Moscow. This, in the
opinion of the Japanese militarists, was a failure.
The Japanese militarists, on their part, were anxious to
strike at the U.S.S.R. to avoid "future regrets." They were
dissatisfied with the activities of Togo and Sato, so set about
making everything uncomfortable for the foreign minister.
Subsequently, Togo had to resign. But his stay or resignation
could have no influence on Japanese foreign relations. He could
not prevent the Pacific War nor influe nce the Soviet-Japanese
relations, although his resignation was an instrument for a possible
Japanese political offensive against the U.S.S.R.
When Tojo took over the foreign affairs portfolio left vacant
by Togo, he told the staff that the latter resigned because "there
was no other alternative." There was a rich historical background
behind this statement. When Gene ral Ugaki was minister in 1937
in the first Konoye cabinet, the Japanese Army and Navy advocated
 "central office for China." Ugaki objected to the idea of
making that office an executive organ over China and insisted on
the indisputable rights of the foreign office. Defending his stand
he was foroed to resign. It was the first serioug open conflict
between the Japanese militarists and diplomats. The militarists
won, and the "Asia Development Bureau" was subsequently establishee

=== Page 621 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The Army proposal of a "Foreign Trade Ministry," when
Nomura was foreign minister in the Hiranuma cabinet in September,
1939, was defeated when the entire foreign office staff resigned
on bloc. This was the second serious conflict, but it turned out
in favor of the diplomats.
The present "Greater East Asiatic Affairs Ministry" caused
the third major conflict between the militarists and the foreign
office. With the establishment of the new ministry, the East
Asiatic and South Seas departments of the Japanese foreign office
will be dissolved. And with Japan at war with the Allied Nations,
there are very little "foreign affairs" left for Togo to bother
about. So,"there was no other alternative" but for him to
resign. This time, it was a definite triumph for the militarists.
For years, the Japanese militarists and extremists looked
upon Kasumigaseki, where the foreign office is situated, as the
stronghold of Japanese liber lism and tried to storm the "Fort
of Evening Clouds" vhenever there was a chance. When Matsuoka
was foreign minis ter in 1940, he dismissed more than 40 ambassadors,
ministers, and other high ranking diplomets to ush the rmy's
anti-American and anti-British movement. till, it was not enough.
When the Imperial assistanc e ssoc iation was reor ganized last
May, Anto of the association openly stated that all liberalists
should be wiped out. Very few of the aministrative staff members
sent to the Japanese occupied terri tories in the South Seas were
men from the foreign office or "liberals." The 'Greater ast
Asiatic Affairs Minis try' is to be staffed by militarists and
civilians, not necessarily by men vith diplomatic training. This
clearly indicates an organized boycott against "liberal" diplomats.
Tojo might not have anything personal against Togo, but-the
latter, classed as a "liberal," had to resign because there was
no other alternat ive." With Tojo holding the portfolio of premier,
war, and foreign affairs, Japan is virtually under a dictatorship.
The resigna tion of Togo was a blow to Japanese diplomats,
according to Mr. Yang, but it does not mean the militarists can
get the ir cooperation. The resignation of Kawagoe and Nishi
Immediately after Togo left office indicated the non-cooperative
attitude of the Japanese diplomats. Liberalism, if any, in
Japan, can be oppressed but not uprooted.
Although the establ ishment of the "Greater East Asiatic
Affairs Ministry" constituted the di rect reason for Togo to
resign, it was not the only reason. The new "ministry" itself is
but one of the results of the traditional strife between the
Japanese military and diplomets. It is a product of Japanese
policy and historical development. It is a timely organization to
meet Japan's current requirements. It is Tojo's preparation for
a prolonged war, as well as a manif estation of Japanese ambitions
over the South Seas. It will be far better for the nations
included under this new "ministry," especially Thailand and
Indo-China, to think twice about their situation under Japanese
rulo.
